<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:anisotropy}
For \(\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_1,\,\ldots,\,\alpha_d)^\top\in\mathbb{R}_+^d\footnote{Here \(\mathbb{R}_+^d=(0,\,\infty)^d\), so each $\alpha_j>0$.}\) and a constant \(L>0\), the anisotropic Nikol'skii density class \(\mathcal{N}_1^d(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,L)\) consists of all Lebesgue probability densities \(f_X\) on \(\mathbb{R}^d\) satisfying the following conditions.

 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        {\begin{enumerate}} 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    [label=(\textbf{N\arabic*}), leftmargin=*]
    \item \textbf{Differentiability.} \label{it:diff}
    For each \(j=1,\,\ldots,\,d\), let \(m_j\coloneqq \lceil \alpha_j \rceil -1\), where 
    \(\lceil \alpha_j \rceil\) denotes the smallest integer that is greater than or equal to \(\alpha_j\). Then, \(f_X\) admits partial derivatives with respect to \(x_j\) up to order \(m_j\), with the convention that \(\partial_j^0 f_X = f_X\) when \(m_j = 0\).\\[-8pt]

\item \textbf{\(L^1\)-bounds on derivatives.} \label{it:L1}
    For every \(j=1,\,\ldots,\,d\) and every \(k=0,\,\ldots,\,m_j\), we have \(\|\partial_j^{k} f_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} \le L\), where \(\partial_j^{k} f_X\) denotes the partial derivative of order \(k\) with respect to the \(j\)-th coordinate.\\[-8pt]

    \item \textbf{Global smoothness of derivatives.} \label{it:smooth}
    For every \(j=1,\,\ldots,\,d\) and all \(\delta\in\mathbb{R}\), we have \(\|\Delta_{\delta\mathbf{e}_j}\partial_j^{m_j} f_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} \le L\,|\delta|^{\alpha_j-m_j}\), where \(\mathbf{e}_j \coloneqq (0,\,\ldots,\,0,\,1,\,0,\,\ldots,\,0)^\top\) is the \(j\)-th standard basis vector of \(\mathbb{R}^d\), and the forward finite difference operator is defined by \(\Delta_{\delta\mathbf{e}_j}\partial_j^{m_j} f_X(\mathbf{x})\coloneqq \partial_j^{m_j} f_X(\mathbf{x}+\delta\mathbf{e}_j) - \partial_j^{m_j} f_X(\mathbf{x})\), \(\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^d\).
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        {\end{enumerate}} 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[1]\relax}
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:unbiasedness}
Assume that \(K \in L^1(\mathbb{R}^d)\), that \(\varphi_K\) has compact support, 
and that \(\varphi_\varepsilon(\mathbf{t}) \neq 0\) for all \(\mathbf{t}\) in the support of \(\varphi_K(\mathbf{H}\,\cdot\,)\).
Then, for every \(\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^d\),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:bias}
\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{x})]
=(K_{\mathbf{H}}\ast F_X)(\mathbf{x}),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
where the expectation is taken over the \(n\)-fold product of the convolution measure \(\mu_X\ast \mu_\varepsilon\).
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:bias}
Let \(F_X\) be a cumulative distribution function whose Lebesgue density \(f_X\) belongs to the anisotropic Nikol'skii class \(\mathcal{N}_1^d(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,L)\), where \(\alpha_j>0\) for each \(j=1,\,\dots,\,d\) and \(L>0\) is a constant. 
For every \(j=1,\,\dots,\,d\), let \(K_j\in L^1(\mathbb R)\cap L^2(\mathbb R)\) be a univariate kernel satisfying the following moment conditions:
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:kernelcond}
\int_{\mathbb R} z^r K_j(z)\,\mathrm{d}z = \delta_{0,r},\quad r=0,\,\dots,\,\lceil\alpha_j\rceil,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
which correspond to the \(d=1\) version of \ref{cond:K1}. We also impose the integrability condition
$\int_{\mathbb R} |z|^{\alpha_j+1}|K_j(z)|\,\mathrm{d}z<\infty$.\footnote{This is the natural condition for controlling the bias of the cumulative distribution function. Note that when \(\alpha_j\) is an integer, this coincides with the \(d=1\) version of \ref{cond:K2}; when \(\alpha_j\) is not an integer, it is slightly weaker than \ref{cond:K2}, but it is sufficient for the bound established below.}
For \(\mathbf h\in(0,\,1)^d\), let \(K_{\mathbf h}\) be the rescaled product kernel defined in \eqref{eq:productkern}.
Then,
\[
\|F_X-(K_{\mathbf h}\ast F_X)\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}
\le L\sum_{j=1}^d C_j h_j^{\alpha_j+1},
\]
where, for every \(j=1,\,\dots,\,d\), with the convention that the empty product equals \(1\),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:constantc_j}
C_j\coloneqq
\prod_{\substack{r=1 \\ r\ne j}}^d \|K_r\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}
\,
\frac{C_{\alpha_j}}{\alpha_j+1}
\int_{\mathbb R}|z|^{\alpha_j+1}|K_j(z)|\,\mathrm{d}z<\infty .

Here, \(C_{\alpha_j}>0\) is a constant depending only on \(\alpha_j\), whose explicit expression is given in the proof.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{rem:samebias}
Although Proposition~\ref{prop:bias} is stated for the \(L^1\)-norm of the bias $B_{\mathbf{h}}$, the same proof carries over to the \(L^2\)-norm of $B_{\mathbf{h}}$, provided that the regularity assumption on the density \(f_X\) is modified accordingly: the \(L^1\)-based Nikol'skii class \(\mathcal N_1^d(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,L)\) is replaced by its \(L^2\)-based counterpart \(\mathcal N_2^d(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\, L)\). In particular, condition \ref{it:smooth} is replaced by its \(L^2\)-analogue: for each \(j=1,\,\ldots,\,d\), there exists a constant \(L>0\) such that, for all \(\delta\in\mathbb R\), we have
\(
\bigl\| \Delta_{\delta\mathbf{e}_j}\partial_j^{m_j} f_X \bigr\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}\le L |\delta |^{\alpha_j - m_j}\),
where \(m_j \coloneqq \lceil \alpha_j\rceil - 1\) as in \ref{it:diff}. Under this strengthened assumption, the bound is
$
\|F_X-(K_{\mathbf h}\ast F_X)\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}
\le
L \sum_{j=1}^d C_j h_j^{\alpha_j+1}$, 
with the same constants \(C_j>0\) as in \eqref{eq:constantc_j}. Further details are provided in Remark \ref{rem:adjusted_proof} in \hyperref[sec:S2]{Section~S2 of the Supplement}.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:variance}
Assume that the noise conditions \ref{eq:E1indep}, \ref{it:E21} and \ref{eq:E3} hold with $\beta_j>0$ for all $j=1,\,\ldots,\,d$. Suppose also that, for some \(p > 1/2\), the following moment conditions are satisfied:

&\mathsf E_{\mu_{\varepsilon_j}}[|\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{2p}] < \infty \quad\text{for every } j=1,\,\dots,\,d. \label{eq:assoneps}

\item $\varphi_{K_j}$ is supported on $[-1,\,1]$ and is twice continuously differentiable on $\mathbb R$.\footnote{An explicit example of a kernel $K_j$ satisfying all the requirements in (\emph{i})--(\emph{iii}) is exhibited in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:variance}.}

<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:ubrate}
Assume that the noise conditions \ref{eq:E1indep}, \ref{it:E21}, and \ref{eq:E3} hold with $\beta_j>0$ for all $j=1,\,\dots,\,d$. 
Suppose further that the moment conditions \eqref{eq:assonX} and \eqref{eq:assoneps} are satisfied for some $p>1/2$, and that the density $f_X$ belongs to an anisotropic Nikol'skii class $\mathcal{N}_1^d(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,L)$ for some $\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}_+^d$ and $L>0$.

Let $\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}$ be the kernel‑based estimator constructed with a product kernel as in \eqref{eq:kernstruc} of order $\boldsymbol{\ell}= \lceil\boldsymbol{\alpha}\rceil$, where each univariate kernel $K_j$ satisfies the conditions (i)--(iii) listed in Proposition~\ref{prop:variance}. 
Let $\widehat F^{\ast}_{n,\mathbf{h}}$ be the $\varrho_n$-approximate minimum-$L^1$-distance estimator with $\varrho_n = O({n}^{-1/2})$. Then, for the bandwidth vector $\mathbf{h}$ chosen as 
$h_j \asymp n^{-{E}/[2(\alpha_j+1)]}$, $j=1,\,\dots,\,d$, 
the $L^1$-risk of $\widehat F^{\ast}_{n,\mathbf{h}}$ satisfies
\[
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\widehat F^{\ast}_{n,\mathbf{h}} - F_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}]
\lesssim\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}} \; (\log n)^{\frac12\sum_{j=1}^d \mathbf{1}_{\{\beta_j = 1/2\}}},
\]
where
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:rate}
\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}} 
\coloneqq n^{-\frac{E}{2}},
\qquad\text{with }
E \coloneqq \Biggl[1 + \sum_{j=1}^d \frac{(\beta_j-\frac12)_+}{\alpha_j+1}\Biggr]^{-1}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation}

<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:ubrateL2}
Under the same assumptions as in Theorem~\ref{thm:ubrate}, except that $f_X$ belongs to the $L^2$-based Nikol'skii class $\mathcal N_2^d(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\, L)$ instead of $\mathcal N_1^d(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\, L)$, the $L^2$-risk of the $\varrho_n$-approximate minimum-$L^2$-distance estimator $\widehat F^{\ast}_{n,\mathbf{h}}$, with $\varrho_n = O({n}^{-1/2})$, satisfies
\(
\mathsf E_{(\mu_X \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}
[\| \widehat F^{\ast}_{n,\mathbf{h}} - F_X \|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}]
\lesssim
\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}
(\log n)^{\frac12 \sum_{j=1}^d \mathbf{1}_{\{\beta_j = 1/2\}}}\),
with rate \(\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}\) as in \eqref{eq:rate}.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
The proof follows the same structure as that of Theorem~\ref{thm:ubrate}, with Proposition~\ref{prop:bias} replaced by its $L^2$-counterpart established in Remark~\ref{rem:samebias}. Moreover, as shown in Proposition~\ref{prop:variance}, the stochastic error bound remains valid in the $L^2$-norm.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def:superkernel}
A \emph{superkernel} is a function $K \in L^1(\mathbb R) \cap L^2(\mathbb R)$ whose Fourier transform $\mathcal F[K]$ is of class $C^\infty(\mathbb R)$, compactly supported, symmetric about zero, and satisfies $\mathcal F[K]= 1$ on $[-1,\,1]$.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:exsuperkern}
A superkernel $K$ in the sense of Definition~\ref{def:superkernel} exists.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
When the density $f_X$ exists and no further smoothness is assumed (this may be regarded as corresponding to $\alpha_j = 0$ for all $j = 1,\,\dots,\,d$) and the noise satisfies $\beta_j = 1$ for all $j = 1,\,\dots,\,d$, the optimal convergence rate is \(n^{-{1}/{(2+d)}}\).
Indeed, substituting \(\alpha_j=0\) and \(\beta_j=1\) into \eqref{eq:rate} gives \((\beta_j-1/2)_+ = 1/2\), hence $
E =[1 + d/2]^{-1} = {2}/{(2+d)}$,
so \(\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}} = n^{-E/2} = n^{-{1}/{(2+d)}}\).
This rate coincides with the classical \emph{noise-free} density estimation rate, where the random vectors $\mathbf{X}_1,\,\dots,\,\mathbf{X}_n\in\mathbb{R}^d$ are observed directly without measurement errors.
The rate follows from the standard bias-variance trade-off. Indeed, the bias is of order $\sum_{j=1}^d h_j$, while the stochastic error term is of order $(n\prod_{j=1}^d h_j)^{-1/2}$. Balancing these two quantities yields $n^{-{1}/{(2+d)}}$. To verify the order of the stochastic error, consider the bound on the squared dominant term:
\[
\frac{1}{n}\prod_{j=1}^d I_{2,j,h_j} \;\lesssim\; \frac{1}{n}\prod_{j=1}^d \int_{1<|t|\le 1/h_j} \frac{|r_{\varepsilon_j}(t)|^2}{|t|^2}\,\mathrm{d}t
\lesssim\frac{1}{n}\prod_{j=1}^d \int_{1<|t|\le 1/h_j} \frac{(1+|t|)^{2\beta_j}}{|t|^2}\,\mathrm{d}t
\lesssim \frac{1}{n}\prod_{j=1}^d h_j^{-1},
\]
where we have used $|r_{\varepsilon_j}(t)| \lesssim (1+|t|)^{\beta_j}$ for all $j$, and the last inequality follows from $(1+|t|)^{2\beta_j}/|t|^2 \lesssim 1$ for $|t|>1$ when $\beta_j=1$. Consequently, the square root of the dominant stochastic term is of order $(n\prod_{j=1}^d h_j)^{-1/2}$. Balancing this with the bias term $\sum_{j=1}^d h_j$ by choosing the bandwidth vector $\mathbf{h}$ optimally gives the rate $n^{-{1}/{(2+d)}}$.


The key insight why this matches the noise-free density estimation rate is that when $\beta_j = 1$, the factor $|r_{\varepsilon_j}(t)|^2$ cancels the $|t|^2$ term in the denominator. Estimating the cumulative distribution function introduces a factor $|t|^{-2}$ in the Fourier domain, which corresponds to a smoothness gain of order $1$ compared to density estimation. At the same time, the measurement error with $\beta_j = 1$ contributes a factor $|r_{\varepsilon_j}(t)|^2 \lesssim (1+|t|)^2$, which grows at the same rate. Hence, the ratio $|r_{\varepsilon_j}(t)|^2/|t|^2$ is asymptotically constant for $|t|> 1$, making the squared stochastic error scale as $(n\prod_{j=1}^d h_j)^{-1}$, exactly as in the noise-free density estimation case; see, \emph{e.g.}, \citet{ComteLacour2013}, p.~575, where $\varphi_{\varepsilon_j}(t)=1$ (\emph{i.e.}, no noise). The smoothness gain from estimating the cumulative distribution function therefore compensates perfectly for the measurement error when $\beta_j = 1$, explaining why the optimal convergence rate $n^{-{1}/{(2+d)}}$ coincides with the classical rate for nonparametric density estimation from direct observations.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{th:LB}
Consider an i.i.d. sample $\mathbf{Y}_1,\,\dots,\,\mathbf{Y}_n$ from the multivariate convolution model in \eqref{eq:model}. 
Suppose that noise conditions \ref{eq:E1indep}, \ref{it:E22}, and \ref{eq:E3} hold with $\beta_j > 0$ for $j = 1,\, \dots,\, d$. Then, for any $\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathbb R_+^d$ and $L>0$,
\[
\inf_{\hat{F}_n}\sup_{f_X\in\mathcal{N}_1^d(\boldsymbol{\alpha},L)}
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\hat{F}_n-F_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}]
\;\gtrsim\; \psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}},
\]
where $\hat{F}_n$ denotes a distribution function estimator based on $n$ observations,
and $\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}$ is as in \eqref{eq:rate}.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:oracle_ineq}
Assume that the noise conditions  \ref{eq:E1indep}, \ref{it:E21} and \ref{eq:E3} hold with \(\beta_j>0\) for all \(j=1,\,\ldots,\,d\). Consider the kernel-based estimator \(\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}\) constructed with a product kernel \(K(\mathbf{x})=\prod_{j=1}^d K_j(x_j)\), \(\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb R^d\), where each \(K_j\) satisfies the conditions (i)--(iii) listed in Proposition~\ref{prop:variance}.
Then, for any cumulative distribution function \(F_X\) for which the bias \(B_{\mathbf{h}} \coloneqq F_X - (K_{\mathbf{h}}\ast F_X)\) belongs to \(L^2(\mathbb R^d)\) for all \(\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal H_{M_n}\), where \(\mathcal H_{M_n}\) is the bandwidth grid defined in \eqref{eq:setH_M} with $M_n=\lfloor n^{1/2} \rfloor$, the minimum-\(L^2\)-distance estimator \(\widehat F_{n,\hat{\mathbf{h}}}^\ast\) (defined in \eqref{eq:defminimizer} but with respect to the \(L^2\)-distance instead of the \(L^1\)-distance) satisfies, with constants \(D_1,\,D_2>0\) depending only on the kernels, the noise distribution, and the dimension \(d\),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:discretegrid}
\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}\Bigl[\|\widehat F_{n,\hat{\mathbf{h}}}^\ast-F_X\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}\Bigr]
\leq D_1 \inf_{\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal H_{M_n}} \bigl\{\|B_{\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)} + \mathcal V(\mathbf{h})\bigr\} + \frac{D_2}{\sqrt{n}},
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
where \(\mathcal V^2(\mathbf{h})\) is the variance proxy (cf. \eqref{eq:varprox}), and the approximation error \(\varrho_n\) arising from the projection of the kernel-based estimator onto the class of all cumulative distribution functions $\mathfrak F$ on $\mathbb R^d$ satisfies \(\varrho_n = O({n}^{-1/2})\), cf. \eqref{eq:reductionstep}; this term has been absorbed into the constant \(D_2/\sqrt{n}\).

Since the grid $\mathcal H_{M_n}$ is chosen so that the discrete infimum is equivalent, up to constants, to the continuous infimum over $(0,\,1)^d$ (see the grid approximation argument in the proof), inequality \eqref{eq:discretegrid} implies the oracle inequality
\(
\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\widehat F_{n,\hat{\mathbf{h}}}^\ast-F_X\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}]
\lesssim
\mathcal R_n
\), where 
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:defRn}
\mathcal R_n \coloneqq \inf_{\mathbf{h} \in (0,1)^d} \bigl\{ \|B_{\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)} + \mathcal V(\mathbf{h}) \bigr\} + \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {

<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:unified_oracle}
Under the assumptions of Theorem~\ref{thm:oracle_ineq}, assume further that there is a constant \(0<C_1<\infty\) such that, for all \(\mathbf{h} \in (0,\,1)^d\), the bias comparability condition 
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:l1l2bias}
\|B_{\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} \le C_1 \|B_{\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {

        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
holds. 
Let \(\check{F}_n \coloneqq \widehat{F}_{n,\hat{\mathbf{h}}}\) be the kernel-based estimator defined in \eqref{eq:defKestim} with bandwidth vector $\hat{\mathbf{h}}$ selected by the G--L method. For \(q \in \{1,\,2\}\), let
$\widehat F_{n,\hat{\mathbf{h}}}^{\ast,q}$
be a $\varrho_{n,q}$-approximate \(L^q\)-minimizer, with $\varrho_{n,q}=O({n}^{-1/2})$, defined in \eqref{eq:defminimizer} for $q=1$ and in \eqref{eq:reductionstep} for $q=2$. Then,
$\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\check{F}_n - F_X\|_{L^q(\mathbb R^d)}]
\lesssim
\mathcal R_n$ and
$\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\widehat F_{n,\hat{\mathbf{h}}}^{\ast,q} - F_X\|_{L^q(\mathbb R^d)}]
\lesssim
\mathcal R_n$ for $q= 1,\, 2$, where $\mathcal R_n$ is defined in \eqref{eq:defRn}.
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def:prodclass}
Let \(\mathcal P_{\text{prod}}\) denote the class of signal probability measures \(\mu_X\) on \(\mathbb R^d\) with product densities
\(
f_X(\mathbf x) \coloneqq \prod_{j=1}^d f_{X,j}(x_j)\), $\mathbf x = (x_1,\,\dots,\,x_d)^\top \in \mathbb R^d$,
where each marginal density \(f_{X,j}\) belongs to the one-dimensional anisotropic Nikol'skii class \(\mathcal N_1^1(\alpha_j,\, L_j)\) as in Definition~\ref{def:anisotropy} (with \(d=1\)), for some \(\alpha_j > 0\) and \(L_j > 0\), and additionally satisfies
$\int_{\mathbb R} |x| f_{X,j}(x) \, \mathrm{d}x < \infty$.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{th:W1minimax}
Let \(\mathcal P_{\textnormal{prod}}\) be the class of probability measures introduced in Definition~\ref{def:prodclass}, and suppose there is at least one active coordinate, i.e., \(|\mathcal P| \ge 1\), where \(\mathcal P\) is defined in \eqref{eq:setactivecoord}. Let \(\widehat{\mu}_{n,\mathbf{h}}^\ast\) be the probability measure above. Then,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        {\begin{enumerate}} 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    [label={(\emph{\roman*})}]
    \item \emph{Lower bound} \quad \(\displaystyle 
    \inf_{\hat{\mu}_n} \sup_{\mu_X \in \mathcal P_{\textnormal{prod}}} 
    \mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}} \big[ W_1^{(\ell^2)}(\hat{\mu}_n,\, \mu_X) \big] \gtrsim \psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}.
    \)

    \item \emph{Upper bound} \quad \(\displaystyle 
    \sup_{\mu_X \in \mathcal P_{\textnormal{prod}}} 
    \mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}} \big[W_1^{(\ell^2)}(\widehat{\mu}_{n,\mathbf{h}}^\ast,\, \mu_X) \big] \lesssim \psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}.
    \)

    \item \emph{Minimax rate} \quad \(\displaystyle 
    \inf_{\hat{\mu}_n} \sup_{\mu_X \in \mathcal P_{\textnormal{prod}}} 
    \mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}} \big[ W_1^{(\ell^2)}(\hat{\mu}_n,\, \mu_X) \big] \asymp \psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}.
    \)

Let \(\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^d\) be fixed. By the definition of \(\widehat{F}_{n,\mathbf{H}}\) as the integral of \(\hat{f}_{n,\mathbf{H}}\) and Fubini's theorem,
\[
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\widehat{F}_{n,\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{x})]
= \int_{-\infty}^{x_1}\!\!\cdots\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{x_d} \mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\hat{f}_{n,\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{u})] \,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}.
\]
The expectation of the deconvolution kernel density estimator is
\[
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\hat{f}_{n,\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{u})]
= \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \frac{1}{|\mathbf{H}|} \tilde{K}_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{H}^{-1}(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{y})) \, (\mu_X \ast \mu_\varepsilon)(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}).
\]
Writing the convolution \(\mu_X \ast \mu_\varepsilon\) in terms of characteristic functions and applying the Fourier inversion formula, we obtain
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {

        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\hat{f}_{n,\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{u})]
&= \frac{1}{(2\pi)^d} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\langle \mathbf{t},\, \mathbf{u}\rangle} 
\frac{\varphi_K(\mathbf{H}\mathbf{t})}{\varphi_\varepsilon(\mathbf{t})} \,
\varphi_\varepsilon(\mathbf{t}) \varphi_X(\mathbf{t}) \,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{t} \\
&= \frac{1}{(2\pi)^d} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d}  \mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\langle \mathbf{t},\, \mathbf{u}\rangle}  
\varphi_K(\mathbf{H}\mathbf{t}) \varphi_X(\mathbf{t}) \,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{t} \\
&= \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} K_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{z}) \, \mu_X(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {

        {\end{align*}}

        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    }

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
where the last equality uses the fact that \(\mathcal{F}[K_{\mathbf{H}}](\mathbf{t})=\varphi_K(\mathbf{H}\mathbf{t})\), $\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb R^d$ and \(\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\varphi_K(\mathbf{H}\mathbf{t})\varphi_X(\mathbf{t})](\mathbf{u}) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} K_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{z}) \mu_X(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z})\), $\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb R^d$. Substituting this expression into the integrated expectation and applying Fubini's theorem (since $K_{\mathbf{H}}\in L^1(\mathbb R^d)$ and $\mu_X$ is a finite measure) gives

\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\widehat{F}_{n,\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{x})]
&= \int_{-\infty}^{x_1}\!\!\cdots\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{x_d} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} K_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{z}) \, \mu_X(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}) \,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \left( \int_{-\infty}^{x_1}\!\!\cdots\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{x_d} K_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{z}) \,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u} \right) \mu_X(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}).

For a fixed \(\mathbf{z}\), change variables \(\mathbf{v} = \mathbf{u}-\mathbf{z}\). Then, \(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u} = \mathrm{d}\mathbf{v}\) and the condition \(\mathbf{u}\le \mathbf{x}\) (understood coordinatewise) becomes \(\mathbf{v}\le \mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\). Thus,
\[
\int_{\{\mathbf{u}\le \mathbf{x}\}} K_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{z}) \,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}
= \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} K_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{v})\,\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{v}\le \mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v},
\]
where \[
\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{v}\le \mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\}} =

1, & \text{if } v_j \le x_j - z_j \text{ for all } j = 1,\,\dots,\,d, \\
0, & \text{otherwise.}

\]
Inserting this into the integral and applying Fubini's theorem once more to swap the order of integration, we obtain

\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\widehat{F}_{n,\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{x})]
&= \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \left( \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} K_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{v})\,\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{v}\le \mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v} \right) \mu_X(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z})\\
&= \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} K_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{v}) \left( \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{z}\le \mathbf{x}-\mathbf{v}\}} \,\mu_X(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}) \right) \mathrm{d}\mathbf{v}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
The inner integral is the measure of the set \(\{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb R^d:\, \mathbf{z}\le \mathbf{x}-\mathbf{v}\}\), which is precisely the cumulative distribution function \(F_X(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{v})\). Therefore,
\[
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\widehat{F}_{n,\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{x})]
= \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} K_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{v}) \, F_X(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{v}) \,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v}
= (K_{\mathbf{H}} \ast F_X)(\mathbf{x}).
\]
This completes the proof.

We begin by expressing the bias in an integral form and then decomposing it into a sum of coordinatewise differences via a telescoping identity. For \(\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb R^d\), the bias is
\[
B_{\mathbf{h}}(\mathbf{x})
= [F_X- (K_{\mathbf{h}}\ast F_X)](\mathbf{x})=
\int_{\mathbb R^d} K(\mathbf{z})
[F_X(\mathbf{x})-F_X(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{h}\odot\mathbf{z})]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z},
\]
where \(\mathbf{h}\odot\mathbf{z}:=(h_1z_1,\,\dots,\,h_dz_d)^\top\) and \(K(\mathbf{z})=\prod_{j=1}^d K_j(z_j)\).
For fixed \(\mathbf{x},\,\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb R^d\), define the points
\[
\mathbf{x}^{(0)}\coloneqq\mathbf{x},\qquad 
\mathbf{x}^{(j)}\coloneqq(x_1-h_1z_1,\,\dots,\,x_j-h_jz_j,\,x_{j+1},\,\dots,\,x_d)^\top.
\]
These points form a chain from \(\mathbf{x}\) to \(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{h}\odot\mathbf{z}\). The difference between the values of the cumulative distribution function at the endpoints of the chain can be decomposed, by telescoping, as
\[
F_X(\mathbf{x})-F_X(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{h}\odot\mathbf{z})
=
\sum_{j=1}^d
[F_X(\mathbf{x}^{(j-1)})-F_X(\mathbf{x}^{(j)})].
\]
Using the triangle inequality and Fubini's theorem, we obtain
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:biasnorm}
\|B_{\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}
\le
\sum_{j=1}^d
\int_{\mathbb R^d}|K(\mathbf{z})|
\int_{\mathbb R^d}
|F_X(\mathbf{x}^{(j-1)})-F_X(\mathbf{x}^{(j)})|\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {

    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation}

    }

Next, we isolate the contribution of the \(j\)-th coordinate by performing a change of variables that shifts the \(j\)-th coordinate only. For a fixed \(j\in\{1,\,\dots,\,d\}\), define
\[
v_r\coloneqq

x_r-h_rz_r,& r<j,\\
x_r,& r\ge j.

\]
Then, \(\mathbf{x}^{(j-1)}=\mathbf{v}\) and \(\mathbf{x}^{(j)}=\mathbf{v}-h_jz_j\mathbf e_j\), and the Jacobian of the transformation is \(1\). The inner integral in \eqref{eq:biasnorm} therefore becomes
\[
\int_{\mathbb R^d}
|F_X(\mathbf{x}^{(j-1)})-F_X(\mathbf{x}^{(j)})|\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=\int_{\mathbb R^d}
|F_X(\mathbf{v})-F_X(\mathbf{v}-h_jz_j\mathbf e_j)|\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v}.
\]
The integrals over \(z_r\) for \(r\ne j\) factor out as the constants \(\|K_r\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\), yielding

    \label{eq:coord}
\|B_{\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}
\le
\sum_{j=1}^d
\left(\prod_{\substack{r=1 \\ r\ne j}}^d \|K_r\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\right)
\int_{\mathbb R}|K_j(z_j)|
\int_{\mathbb R^d}
|F_X(\mathbf{v})-F_X(\mathbf{v}-h_jz_j\mathbf e_j)|\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v}\,\mathrm{d}z_j.

The difference of the cumulative distribution function along the \(j\)-th coordinate admits the exact integral representation
\[
F_X(\mathbf{v})-F_X(\mathbf{v}-h_jz_j\mathbf e_j)
=\int_{v_j - h_j z_j}^{v_j}
f_X(v_1,\,\dots,\,v_{j-1},\, t,\,v_{j+1},\,\dots,\,v_d)\,\mathrm{d}t=
\int_{v_j-h_jz_j}^{v_j} f_X(\mathbf{v}_{-j},\, t)\,\mathrm{d}t,
\]
where, in the joint density \(f_X(\mathbf{v}_{-j},\, t)\), the notation \(\mathbf{v}_{-j}\) indicates that all non-\(j\) coordinates are held fixed, while \(t\) varies along the \(j\)-th axis. Substituting this into \eqref{eq:coord}, we find that

    \label{eq:biasint}
\|B_{\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}
\le
\sum_{j=1}^d
\left(\prod_{\substack{r=1 \\ r\ne j}}^d \|K_r\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\right)
\int_{\mathbb R}|K_j(z_j)|
\int_{\mathbb R^d}
\bigg|
\int_{v_j-h_jz_j}^{v_j} f_X(\mathbf{v}_{-j},\, t)\,\mathrm{d}t
\bigg|
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v}\,\mathrm{d}z_j.

We now expand the density \(f_X\) in the \(j\)-th coordinate using Taylor's theorem with integral remainder. For each fixed \(\mathbf{v}_{-j}\), the function \(t \mapsto f_X(\mathbf{v}_{-j},\, t)\) is expanded around \(t=v_j\) up to order \(m_j \coloneqq \lceil \alpha_j\rceil - 1\). This is justified because the Nikol'skii condition guarantees the existence of derivatives up to order \(m_j\), and the remainder can be controlled in \(L^1(\mathbb R^d)\). For any \(t\in\mathbb R\), writing \(s \coloneqq t - v_j\), we have
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {

f_X(\mathbf{v}_{-j},\, v_j+s)
=
\sum_{k=0}^{m_j}
\frac{s^k}{k!}\partial_j^k f_X(\mathbf{v}_{-j},\, v_j)
+
R_j(\mathbf{v},\, s),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation}

with the remainder
\[
R_j(\mathbf{v},\, s)\coloneqq
\frac{1}{(m_j-1)!}
\int_0^{s} (s-u)^{m_j-1}
[
\partial_j^{m_j} f_X(\mathbf{v}_{-j},\, v_j+u)
-
\partial_j^{m_j} f_X(\mathbf{v}_{-j},\, v_j)
]\, \mathrm{d}u
\]
for \(m_j\ge1\). For \(m_j=0\), the sum over \(k=0,\,\dots,\,m_j\) reduces to the term \(k=0\), and the remainder is defined as
\[
R_j(\mathbf{v},\, s) \coloneqq f_X(\mathbf{v}_{-j},\, v_j+s) - f_X(\mathbf{v}_{-j},\,v_j),
\]
so that the expansion takes the form \(f_X(\mathbf{v}_{-j},\, v_j+s) = f_X(\mathbf{v}_{-j},\, v_j) + R_j(\mathbf{v},\, s)\). 

The remainder satisfies the \(L^1\)-bound

\int_{\mathbb R^d} |R_j(\mathbf{v},\, s)|\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v}
\le C_{\alpha_j} L |s|^{\alpha_j},

where 
\[C_{\alpha_j} \coloneqq
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases}

\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_j - m_j + 1)}{\Gamma(\alpha_j + 1)}, & m_j \ge 1,\\[5pt]
1, & m_j = 0.

&=
C_{\alpha_j} L |s|^{\alpha_j},

        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
where the second inequality follows from condition \ref{it:smooth} \hyperref[it:smooth]{of the Main Article} with \(\eta_j \coloneqq \alpha_j - m_j\). For \(m_j=0\), the same bound is a direct consequence of \ref{it:smooth} \hyperref[it:smooth]{of the Main Article} with \(\eta_j=\alpha_j\). This establishes \eqref{eq:remainder2}.
Substituting the Taylor expansion \eqref{eq:dev} into the integral over \(t\) in \eqref{eq:biasint}, we obtain
\[ 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        {\begin{split}} 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\int_{v_j-h_jz_j}^{v_j} f_X(\mathbf{v}_{-j},\, t)\,\mathrm{d}t
&=
-\sum_{k=0}^{m_j}
\frac{(-h_jz_j)^{k+1}}{(k+1)!}
\partial_j^k f_X(\mathbf{v}_{-j},\, v_j)
+
\int_{v_j-h_jz_j}^{v_j} R_j(\mathbf{v},\, t-v_j)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=
-\sum_{k=0}^{m_j}
\frac{(-h_jz_j)^{k+1}}{(k+1)!}
\partial_j^k f_X(\mathbf{v})
+
\int_{v_j-h_jz_j}^{v_j} R_j(\mathbf{v},\, t-v_j)\,\mathrm{d}t.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        {\end{split}} 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
Multiplying by \(K_j(z_j)\), integrating over \(z_j\), taking the \(L^1\)-norm in \(\mathbf{v}\), and using assumption \ref{it:L1} \hyperref[it:L1]{of the Main Article} that $\|\partial_j^k f_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}<\infty$ for all $k=0,\,\ldots,\,m_j$, the polynomial part becomes
\[
\sum_{k=0}^{m_j}
\frac{h_j^{k+1}}{(k+1)!}
\left|\int_{\mathbb R} z_j^{k+1} K_j(z_j)\,\mathrm{d}z_j\right|
\int_{\mathbb R^d} |\partial_j^k f_X(\mathbf{v})|\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v}=
\sum_{k=0}^{m_j}
\frac{h_j^{k+1}}{(k+1)!}
\left|\int_{\mathbb R} z_j^{k+1} K_j(z_j)\,\mathrm{d}z_j\right|
\|\partial_j^k f_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}.
\]
For each \(k=0,\,\dots,\,m_j\), the exponent satisfies \(k+1 \le m_j+1 = \lceil \alpha_j\rceil\). By the moment condition in \eqref{eq:kernelcond} \hyperref[eq:kernelcond]{of the Main Article}, the integrals \(\int_{\mathbb R} z_j^{k+1} K_j(z_j)\,\mathrm{d}z_j\) vanish for all such \(k\). Hence, all polynomial terms disappear identically, leaving only the remainder.
Using the remainder bound \eqref{eq:remainder2}, we estimate the contribution of the remainder as
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\begin{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:intremaind}
\int_{\mathbb R} |K_j(z_j)|
\int_{\mathbb R^d}
\bigg|
\int_{v_j-h_jz_j}^{v_j} R_j(\mathbf{v},\, t-v_j)\,\mathrm{d}t
\bigg|
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v}\,\mathrm{d}z_j
&\le
\int_{\mathbb R} |K_j(z_j)|
\int_0^{|h_jz_j|}
\int_{\mathbb R^d} |R_j(\mathbf{v}, \,u)|\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v}\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}z_j \nonumber\\
&\le
C_{\alpha_j} L
\int_{\mathbb R} |K_j(z_j)|
\int_0^{|h_jz_j|} |u|^{\alpha_j}\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}z_j \nonumber\\
&=
\frac{C_{\alpha_j}}{\alpha_j+1} L h_j^{\alpha_j+1}
\int_{\mathbb R} |z_j|^{\alpha_j+1} |K_j(z_j)|\,\mathrm{d}z_j.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\end{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Finally, combining \eqref{eq:biasint}, the cancellation of the polynomial terms, and the remainder estimate \eqref{eq:intremaind}, we arrive at the bound
\[
\|B_{\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}
\le
L\sum_{j=1}^d
\left(\prod_{\substack{r=1 \\ r\ne j}}^d \|K_r\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\right)
\frac{C_{\alpha_j}}{\alpha_j+1}
\left(\int_{\mathbb R} |z_j|^{\alpha_j+1} |K_j(z_j)|\,\mathrm{d}z_j\right)
h_j^{\alpha_j+1}.
\]
Recalling the definition of $C_j$ in \eqref{eq:constantc_j} \hyperref[eq:constantc_j]{of the Main Article}
as
\[
C_j\coloneqq
\left(\prod_{\substack{r=1 \\ r\ne j}}^d \|K_r\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\right)
\frac{C_{\alpha_j}}{\alpha_j+1}
\int_{\mathbb R} |z_j|^{\alpha_j+1} |K_j(z_j)|\,\mathrm{d}z_j<\infty,
\]
we obtain the bound
\[
\|F_X-(K_{\mathbf{h}}\ast F_X)\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}
\le
L\sum_{j=1}^d C_j h_j^{\alpha_j+1}.
\]
This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{rem:adjusted_proof}
As noted in Remark~\ref{rem:samebias}, under the \(L^2\)-based Nikol'skii condition $f_X\in\mathcal N_2^d(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\, L)                    
$, the proof proceeds identically up to the remainder estimate in \eqref{eq:intremaind}. Consider the remainder contribution in the \(j\)-th coordinate. By applying Minkowski's integral inequality twice, we obtain
\[
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\begin{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\left\| \int_{\mathbb R} |K_j(z_j)| 
\int_{0}^{h_j z_j} R_j(\cdot,\, u)\,\mathrm{d}u \,\mathrm{d}z_j \right\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}
&\le \int_{\mathbb R} |K_j(z_j)| 
\left\| \int_{0}^{h_j z_j} R_j(\cdot,\, u)\,\mathrm{d}u \right\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)} \,\mathrm{d}z_j \\
&\le \int_{\mathbb R} |K_j(z_j)| 
\int_{0}^{|h_j z_j|} \| R_j(\cdot,\, u) \|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)} \,\mathrm{d}u \,\mathrm{d}z_j\\
&\le
\frac{C_{\alpha_j} L}{\alpha_j+1} h_j^{\alpha_j+1}
\int_{\mathbb R} |z_j|^{\alpha_j+1} |K_j(z_j)|\,\mathrm{d}z_j,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\end{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
where, in the last line, we have substituted the \(L^2\)-remainder bound $
\| R_j(\cdot,\, u) \|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)} \le C_{\alpha_j} L |u|^{\alpha_j}$,
which follows from the Nikol'skii condition in the \(L^2\)-case. 
The cancellation of the polynomial terms is independent of the chosen norm. Therefore, we arrive at the bound
\[
\|B_{\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}
\le
L \sum_{j=1}^d C_j h_j^{\alpha_j+1},
\]
where the constants \(C_j>0\) are exactly those defined in \eqref{eq:constantc_j} \hyperref[eq:constantc_j]{of the Main Article} because the \(C_{\alpha_j}\) (and hence \(C_j\)) are determined solely by the regularity exponent \(\alpha_j\), the kernel moments, and the \(L^1\)-norms of the univariate kernels: they are independent of whether the underlying norm is the \(L^1\) or the \(L^2\). Thus, replacing the \(L^1\)-assumption by the \(L^2\)-assumption does not introduce any new constants into the final bound and the same inequality holds in both settings.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:boundmomY}
Consider the $d$-dimensional convolution model \eqref{eq:model} \hyperref[eq:model]{of the Main Article}
$\mathbf{Y}_i = \mathbf{X}_i + \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_i$, $i=1,\,\dots,\,n$,
where $(\mathbf{X}_i)_{i\in\mathbb N}$ are i.i.d.~random vectors in $\mathbb{R}^d$ with unknown distribution $\mu_X$, and $(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_i)_{i\in\mathbb N}$ are i.i.d.~random vectors in $\mathbb{R}^d$ with known distribution $\mu_\varepsilon$; the two sequences are independent. 
Assume further that the components \(\varepsilon_{1,1},\,\ldots,\,\varepsilon_{1,d}\) of the error vector \(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_1\in\mathbb{R}^d\) are independent random variables (this is assumption \ref{eq:E1indep} \hyperref[eq:E1indep]{of the Main Article}). Assume the following moment conditions hold for some $p>0$:
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\begin{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
&\mathsf E_{\mu_X}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^{d}(1\vee |X_{1,j}|^{2p})\Biggr] < \infty,\label{(A1)}\\
&\mathsf E_{\mu_{\varepsilon_j}}[|\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{2p}] < \infty \quad\text{for every } j=1,\,\dots,\,d,\label{(A2)}
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\end{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
(these are conditions \eqref{eq:assonX} and \eqref{eq:assoneps} \hyperref[eq:assonX]{of the Main Article}, respectively, except that no constraint on $p$ is assumed here).
Then, there exists a constant $C(d,\,p)$, depending only on $d$ and $p$, such that
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:boundprodY}
\mathsf E_{\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^{d}(1\vee |Y_{1,j}|^{2p})\Biggr]
\le C(d,\,p)\;
\mathsf E_{\mu_X}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^{d}(1\vee |X_{1,j}|^{2p})\Biggr]\;
\prod_{j=1}^{d}\bigl(1\vee \mathsf E_{\mu_{\varepsilon_j}}[|\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{2p}]\bigr).
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
By the independence of the vectors \(\mathbf{X}_1=(X_{1,1},\,\dots,\,X_{1,d})^{\!\top}\) and \(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_1=(\varepsilon_{1,1},\,\dots,\,\varepsilon_{1,d})^{\!\top}\), together with assumptions \eqref{(A1)} and \eqref{(A2)},
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\begin{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:dismax}
\mathsf E_{\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^{d}(1\vee |Y_{1,j}|^{2p})\Biggr]&=\mathsf E_{\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^d(1\vee |Y_{1,j}|^{p})^2\Biggr]\nonumber\\
&\lesssim 
\mathsf E_{\mu_X}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^d(1\vee |X_{1,j}|^{2p})\Biggr]\,
\prod_{j=1}^d\bigl(1\vee \mathsf E_{\mu_{\varepsilon_j}}[|\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{2p}]\bigr)<\infty,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\end{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
where the implicit constant in ``\(\lesssim\)'' depends on \(d\) and \(p\) and is finite under the given assumptions. 

We now verify the inequality in~\eqref{eq:dismax} with an explicit constant. For any real numbers \(a,\,b\) and \(p>0\), the inequality \(|a+b|^{p}\le 2^{p}(|a|^{p}+|b|^{p})\) holds. Applying it with \(a=X_{1,j}\) and \(b=\varepsilon_{1,j}\) gives
$
|Y_{1,j}|^{p}=|X_{1,j}+\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{p}\le 2^{p}(|X_{1,j}|^{p}+|\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{p})$.
Hence,
$
1\vee |Y_{1,j}|^{p}\le 1\vee[2^{p}(|X_{1,j}|^{p}+|\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{p})]
\le 2^{p}[(1\vee |X_{1,j}|^{p})+(1\vee |\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{p})]$.
Squaring yields
$
(1\vee |Y_{1,j}|^{p})^{2}\le 2^{2p}[(1\vee |X_{1,j}|^{p})^{2}+(1\vee |\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{p})^{2}
+2(1\vee |X_{1,j}|^{p})(1\vee |\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{p})]$.
Taking the product over \(j=1,\,\dots,\,d\), we obtain
\[
\prod_{j=1}^{d}(1\vee |Y_{1,j}|^{p})^{2}
\le 2^{2pd}
\prod_{j=1}^{d}[(1\vee |X_{1,j}|^{p})^{2}+(1\vee |\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{p})^{2}
+2(1\vee |X_{1,j}|^{p})(1\vee |\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{p})].
\]
The right‑hand side expands into a sum of \(3^{d}\) terms. For each index \(j\), we choose one of the three summands inside the brackets. If we choose the cross term (the third summand) for exactly \(k\) of the \(d\) coordinates, the corresponding coefficient becomes
\(2^{2pd}2^{k}\). Summing over all choices, the total coefficient is
\[
2^{2pd}\sum_{k=0}^{d} 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@pmatrix} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@pmatrix} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@pmatrix} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@pmatrix} 
        {\org@begin{pmatrix}} 
        {\csname pseudo@pmatrix\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@pmatrix\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@pmatrix\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@pmatrix\endcsname[0]\relax} 
     d \cr k 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@pmatrix} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@pmatrix} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@pmatrix} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@pmatrix} 
        {\org@end{pmatrix}} 
        {\csname pseudo@pmatrix\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@pmatrix\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@pmatrix\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@pmatrix\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    2^{k}=2^{2pd}(1+2)^{d}=3^{d}2^{2pd}.
\]
Each term in the expansion is of the form
\[
2^{2pd} 2^{k}\prod_{j=1}^{d}(1\vee |X_{1,j}|^{p})^{\nu_j}(1\vee |\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{p})^{q_j},
\]
where \(\nu_j,\,q_j\in\{0,\,1,\,2\}\), with \(\nu_j+q_j\le 2\), and \(k\) is the number of indices for which \(\nu_j=q_j=1\). 
Because \((1\vee |X_{1,j}|^{p})^{\nu_j}\le 1\vee |X_{1,j}|^{2p}\) and \((1\vee |\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{p})^{q_j}\le 1\vee |\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{2p}\) for every \(j\), each term is bounded by
\[
2^{2pd}2^k\prod_{j=1}^{d}(1\vee |X_{1,j}|^{2p})\,
        \prod_{j=1}^{d}(1\vee |\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{2p}).
\]
Taking expectation and using the independence between the vectors \(\mathbf{X}_1\) and \(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_1\) gives 
\[ 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        {\begin{split}} 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
&\mathsf E_{\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon}\Biggl[2^{2pd}2^k\prod_{j=1}^{d}(1\vee |X_{1,j}|^{p})^{\nu_j}(1\vee |\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{p})^{q_j}\Biggr]\\
&\hspace*{5cm}\le 2^{2pd}2^k\,
   \mathsf E_{\mu_X}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^{d}(1\vee |X_{1,j}|^{2p})\Biggr]\,
   \prod_{j=1}^{d}\mathsf E_{\mu_{\varepsilon_j}}[1\vee |\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{2p}].
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        {\end{split}} 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
Summing over all \(3^{d}\) terms gives
\[
\mathsf E_{\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^{d}(1\vee |Y_{1,j}|^{p})^{2}\Biggr]
\le 3^{d}2^{2pd}\,
   \mathsf E_{\mu_X}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^{d}(1\vee |X_{1,j}|^{2p})\Biggr]\,
   \prod_{j=1}^{d}\mathsf E_{\mu_{\varepsilon_j}}[1\vee |\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{2p}]<\infty
\]
because, by \eqref{(A1)}, \(\mathsf E_{\mu_X}[\prod_{j=1}^{d}(1\vee |X_{1,j}|^{2p})]<\infty\), and, by \eqref{(A2)}, \(\mathsf E_{\mu_{\varepsilon_j}}[|\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{2p}]<\infty\) for every \(j\). Hence, the right-hand side is finite.
Finally, we relate \(\mathsf E_{\mu_{\varepsilon_j}}[1\vee |\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{2p}]\) to \((1\vee\mathsf E_{\mu_{\varepsilon_j}}[|\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{2p}])\). For any non‑negative random variable \(Z\),
\[
\mathsf E[1\vee Z]\le 1+\mathsf E[Z]\le 2(1\vee\mathsf E[Z]).
\]
Applying this with \(Z=|\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{2p}\) yields $
\mathsf E_{\mu_{\varepsilon_j}}[1\vee |\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{2p}]\le 2(1\vee \mathsf E_{\mu_{\varepsilon_j}}[|\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{2p}])$.
Hence,
\[
\prod_{j=1}^{d}\mathsf E_{\mu_{\varepsilon_j}}[1\vee |\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{2p}]
\le 2^{d}\prod_{j=1}^{d}(1\vee \mathsf E_{\mu_{\varepsilon_j}}[|\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{2p}]).
\]
Thus, with the constant $C(d,\,p):=3^{d}2^{2pd} 2^{d}=3^{d}2^{d(2p+1)}$,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathsf E_{\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^{d}(1\vee |Y_{1,j}|^{2p})\Biggr]&\le C(d,\,p)\,
   \mathsf E_{\mu_X}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^{d}(1\vee |X_{1,j}|^{2p})\Biggr]\,
   \prod_{j=1}^{d}(1\vee \mathsf E_{\mu_{\varepsilon_j}}[|\varepsilon_{1,j}|^{2p}]),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
which is \eqref{eq:boundprodY} with a constant depending only on \(d\) and \(p\). The proof is thus complete.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
We begin by exhibiting an example of a kernel $K_j$ satisfying requirements (\emph{i})--(\emph{iii}) from the statement \hyperref[prop:variance]{in the Main Article}. Let $q_j \ge 2$ be a positive integer parameter. Define the Fourier transform of $K_j$ as
$\varphi_{K_j}(t) \coloneqq (1-t^2)^{q_j}\mathbf{1}_{[-1,1]}(t)$, $t\in\mathbb R$.
Then, $K_j$ is given by the inverse Fourier transform
$K_j(x) \coloneqq (2\pi)^{-1}\int_{\mathbb R} \mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tx}\varphi_{K_j}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t$, $x\in\mathbb R$.
This choice of $K_j$ is precisely the explicit example referenced in the footnote of Proposition~\ref{prop:variance}.
We now verify that $K_j$ satisfies all the required properties.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        {

        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    [label=(\emph{\roman*}), align=left, leftmargin=*]
\item \emph{Integrability:} Since $\varphi_{K_j}$ is compactly supported and $q_j\ge 2$, it is twice continuously differentiable on $\mathbb R$. Therefore, its inverse Fourier transform $K_j$ decays as $|x|^{-2}$ as $|x|\to\infty$. This follows by integrating by parts twice in the Fourier inversion formula, using that $\varphi_{K_j}\in C_c^2(\mathbb R)$. In particular, $K_j\in L^1(\mathbb R)\cap L^2(\mathbb R)$.\\[-8pt]
\item \emph{Symmetry:} Since $\varphi_{K_j}$ is real and even, its inverse Fourier transform $K_j$ is real and even.\\[-8pt]
\item \emph{Support and regularity of $\varphi_{K_j}$:} By construction, $\varphi_{K_j}$ is supported on $[-1,\,1]$ and, for $q_j\ge 2$, is twice continuously differentiable on $\mathbb R$.

    }

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Thus, $K_j$ satisfies requirements (\emph{i})--(\emph{iii}) \hyperref[prop:variance]{in the Main Article}.

\smallskip

We now introduce an auxiliary cutoff function $\phi$. Consider a bump function $\phi \colon \mathbb{R} \to [0,\,1]$ satisfying the following properties:
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
    \item[(a)] $\phi$ is even, \emph{i.e.}, $\phi(-t) = \phi(t)$ for all $t \in \mathbb{R}$;
    \item[(b)] $\phi(t) = 1$ for $|t| \leq 1$;
    \item[(c)] $\phi(t) = 0$ for $|t| \geq 2$;
    \item[(d)] $\phi \in C^2(\mathbb{R})$.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        {\end{itemize}} 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Such a function exists. For instance, define the smoothing polynomial 
$\vartheta(u) = 6u^5 - 15u^4 + 10u^3$, $u\in[0,\,1]$,
which satisfies $\vartheta(0)=0$, $\vartheta^{(1)}(0)=0$, $\vartheta^{(2)}(0)=0$
and $\vartheta(1)=1$, $\vartheta^{(1)}(1)=0$, $\vartheta^{(2)}(1)=0$.
Extend \(\vartheta\) to \(\mathbb R\) by setting \(\vartheta(u)=0\) for \(u\le 0\) and \(\vartheta(u)=1\) for \(u\ge 1\). Then, for \(t\ge 0\), set
\[
\phi(t) \coloneqq
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases}

        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
1, & 0\le t\le 1,\\[2pt]
1 - \vartheta(t-1), & 1\le t\le 2,\\[2pt]
0, & t\ge 2,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\end{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
and extend \(\phi\) to all real \(t\) by evenness, \emph{i.e.}, \(\phi(-t)=\phi(t)\) for \(t\in\mathbb R\). This function \(\phi\) is of class \(C^2(\mathbb{R})\), takes values in \([0,\,1]\), equals \(1\) on \([-1,\,1]\), and vanishes outside \((-2,\,2)\). Properties (a)--(d) are thus verified.

\smallskip

Fix $\mathbf{h}\in(0,\,1)^d$. For each $j=1,\,\ldots,\,d$, define 
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\tilde K_{1,j,h_j}(u)&\coloneqq\mathcal F^{-1}\!\left[\frac{\varphi_{K_j}(t)}{\varphi_{\varepsilon_j}(t/h_j)}\phi(t/h_j)\right]\!(u),\quad u\in\mathbb R,\\
\shortintertext{and}
\tilde K_{2,j,h_j}(u)&\coloneqq\mathcal F^{-1}\!\left[\frac{\varphi_{K_j}(t)}{\varphi_{\varepsilon_j}(t/h_j)}[1-\phi(t/h_j)]\right]\!(u),\quad u\in\mathbb R.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
The full kernel $$\tilde K_{j,h_j}(u)\coloneqq\mathcal F^{-1}\!\left[\frac{\varphi_{K_j}(t)}{\varphi_{\varepsilon_j}(t/h_j)}\right]\!(u),\quad u\in\mathbb R,$$ satisfies the decomposition 
$\tilde K_{j,h_j}(u)=\tilde K_{1,j,h_j}(u)+\tilde K_{2,j,h_j}(u)$, $u\in\mathbb R$.
Define the integrated kernels
$$G_{1,j,h_j}(x)\coloneqq\int_{-\infty}^x
\tilde K_{1,j,h_j}(u)\,\mathrm{d}u, \quad 
G_{2,j,h_j}(x)\coloneqq\int_{-\infty}^x
\tilde K_{2,j,h_j}(u)\,\mathrm{d}u,\quad x\in\mathbb R,
$$
and their sum
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:gfun}
G_{j,h_j}(x)\coloneqq\int_{-\infty}^x\tilde K_{j,h_j}(u)\,\mathrm{d}u=G_{1,j,h_j}(x)+G_{2,j,h_j}(x),\quad x\in\mathbb R.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Because the function \([\varphi_{K_j}(\cdot)\phi(\cdot/h_j)/\varphi_{\varepsilon_j}(\cdot/h_j)]\) is compactly supported and twice continuously differentiable, its inverse Fourier transform \(\tilde K_{1,j,h_j}\) belongs to \(L^1(\mathbb R)\) and
\[
\int_{\mathbb R} \tilde K_{1,j,h_j}(u) \, \mathrm{d}u 
= \mathcal F[\tilde K_{1,j,h_j}](0) 
= \left. \frac{\varphi_{K_j}(t)}{\varphi_{\varepsilon_j}(t/h_j)} \phi(t/h_j) \right|_{t=0} 
= 1,
\]
so that
\[
\lim_{x\to\infty} G_{1,j,h_j}(x) = \int_{\mathbb R} \tilde K_{1,j,h_j}(u) \, \mathrm{d}u = 1.\]

                                                   
For a real number $p>1/2$ as in the statement, we define the integral 
$$I_{2p}\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb R}(1\vee |x|^{p})^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}x=
\int_{\mathbb R}(1\vee |x|^{2p})^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{4p}{2p-1}<\infty.$$ 
Then,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\begin{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
&\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}-(K_{\mathbf {h}}\ast F_X)\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}]\nonumber\\
&\,\,\,=
\int_{\mathbb R^d}\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}(\mathbf x)-(K_{\mathbf {h}}\ast F_X)(\mathbf x)|]\,\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}\nonumber\\
&\,\,\,=
\int_{\mathbb R^d}\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}(\mathbf x)-\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}(\mathbf x)]|]\,\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}\nonumber\\
&\,\,\,
\leq
\int_{\mathbb R^d}\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}[\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}(\mathbf x)]}\,\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}\nonumber\tag{\text{by Lyapunov's inequality}}\\[5pt]
&\,\,\,\leq
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{\mathbb R^d}\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^d\int_{-\infty}^{(x_j-Y_{1,j})/h_j}\tilde K_{j,h_j}(u_j)\,\mathrm{d} u_j\Biggr]}\,\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}\nonumber\tag{\text{variance of an empirical mean of i.i.d. terms and change of variable}}\\[5pt]
&\,\,\,=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{\mathbb R^d}\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^d
G_{j,h_j}\left(\frac{x_j-Y_{1,j}}{h_j}\right)\Biggr]}\,\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}\nonumber\\
&\,\,\,=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{\mathbb R^d}\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^d
(G_{1,j,h_j}+G_{2,j,h_j})\left(\frac{x_j-Y_{1,j}}{h_j}\right)\Biggr]}\,\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}\nonumber\tag{\text{by identity \eqref{eq:gfun}}}\\[5pt]
&\,\,\,=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{\mathbb R^d}\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^d
\bigl[G_{1,j,h_j}(s)\mathbf{1}_{\{s\leq0\}}-(1-G_{1,j,h_j}(s))\mathbf{1}_{\{s>0\}}+G_{2,j,h_j}(s)
\bigl]_{s=\frac{x_j-Y_{1,j}}{h_j}}\Biggr]}\,\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}\nonumber\\
&\,\,\,\leq
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{\mathbb R^d}\sqrt{\mathsf E_{\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^d\Biggl(
\bigl[G_{1,j,h_j}(s)\mathbf{1}_{\{s\leq0\}}-(1-G_{1,j,h_j}(s))\mathbf{1}_{\{s>0\}}+G_{2,j,h_j}(s)\bigr]_{s=\frac{x_j-Y_{1,j}}{h_j}}\Biggr)^2\Biggr]}\,\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}\nonumber\\
&\tag{\text{by the inequality $\mathsf{V}\textrm{ar}[\cdot]\leq\mathsf E[|\cdot|^2]$}}\\[5pt]
&\,\,\,\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}
\Biggl(\int_{\mathbb R^d}\prod_{j=1}^d(1\vee |x_j|^{p})^{-2}\,{\mathbf{\mathrm{d}x}}\Biggr)^{1/2}\nonumber\\
&\qquad\qquad\,\,\times\Biggl\{\mathsf{E}_{\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^d\int_{\mathbb R}(1\vee |x_j|^{p})^2\nonumber\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\times
\Biggl(\bigl[G_{1,j,h_j}(s)\mathbf{1}_{\{s\leq0\}}-(1-G_{1,j,h_j}(s))\mathbf{1}_{\{s>0\}}+G_{2,j,h_j}(s)\bigr]_{s=\frac{x_j-Y_{1,j}}{h_j}}\Biggr)^2{\mathrm{d}x_j}\Biggr]\Biggr\}^{1/2}\nonumber\\
&\tag{\text{by the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality}}\\[5pt]
&\,\,\,=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}I_{2p}^{d/2}
\Biggl\{\mathsf{E}_{\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^d\int_{\mathbb R}(1\vee |x_j|^{2p})
\Biggl(\biggl[\mathbf{1}_{\{v\leq0\}}\int_{-\infty}^{v}\frac{1}{h_j}\tilde K_{1,j,h_j}\Bigl(\frac{z_j}{h_j}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z_j\nonumber\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\mathbf{1}_{\{v>0\}}\int_{v}^{\infty}\frac{1}{h_j}\tilde K_{1,j,h_j}\Bigl(\frac{z_j}{h_j}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z_j\nonumber\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\int_{-\infty}^{v}\frac{1}{h_j}\tilde K_{2,j,h_j}\Bigl(\frac{z_j}{h_j}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z_j\biggr]_{v=x_j-Y_{1,j}}\Biggr)^2\,{\mathrm{d}x_j}\Biggr]\Biggr\}^{1/2}\nonumber\\
&\tag{\text{by the change of variable $z_j=h_j u_j$}}\\
       
&\,\,\,\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}
\Biggl\{\mathsf{E}_{\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^d\int_{\mathbb R}(1\vee |x_j|^{2p})
\Biggl[\mathbf{1}_{\{v\leq0\}}\biggl(\int_{-\infty}^{v}\frac{1}{h_j}\tilde K_{1,j,h_j}\Bigl(\frac{z_j}{h_j}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z_j\biggr)^2\nonumber\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\mathbf{1}_{\{v>0\}}\biggl(\int_{v}^{\infty}\frac{1}{h_j}\tilde K_{1,j,h_j}\Bigl(\frac{z_j}{h_j}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z_j\biggr)^2\nonumber\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\biggl(\int_{-\infty}^{v}\frac{1}{h_j}\tilde K_{2,j,h_j}\Bigl(\frac{z_j}{h_j}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z_j\biggr)^2\Biggr]_{v=x_j-Y_{1,j}}\,{\mathrm{d}x_j}\Biggr]\Biggr\}^{1/2}\nonumber\\
&      
&\tag{\text{neglecting $I_{2p}^{d/2}$ and squaring}}\\[5pt]
&\,\,\,\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Biggl\{\mathsf{E}_{\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^d\int_{\mathbb R}(1\vee |w_j+Y_{1,j}|^{2p})
\Biggl[\mathbf{1}_{\{w_j\leq0\}}\biggl(\int_{-\infty}^{w_j}\frac{1}{h_j}\tilde K_{1,j,h_j}\Bigl(\frac{z_j}{h_j}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z_j\biggr)^2\nonumber\\&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\mathbf{1}_{\{w_j>0\}}\biggl(\int_{w_j}^{\infty}\frac{1}{h_j}\tilde K_{1,j,h_j}\Bigl(\frac{z_j}{h_j}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z_j\biggr)^2\nonumber\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\biggl(\int_{-\infty}^{w_j}\frac{1}{h_j}\tilde K_{2,j,h_j}\Bigl(\frac{z_j}{h_j}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z_j\biggr)^2\Biggr]\,\mathrm{d}w_j\Biggr]\Biggr\}^{1/2}\nonumber\\
&\tag{by the change of variable {$w_j=x_j-Y_{1,j}$}}\\[5pt]
&\,\,\,\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\mathsf{E}_{\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^d(1\vee |Y_{1,j}|^{2p})\Biggr]}
\Biggl\{\prod_{j=1}^d\int_{\mathbb R}(1\vee |w_j|^{p})^2
\Biggl[\mathbf{1}_{\{w_j\leq0\}}\biggl(\int_{-\infty}^{w_j}\frac{1}{h_j}\tilde K_{1,j,h_j}\Bigl(\frac{z_j}{h_j}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z_j\biggr)^2\nonumber
\\&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\mathbf{1}_{\{w_j>0\}}\biggl(\int_{w_j}^{\infty}\frac{1}{h_j}\tilde K_{1,j,h_j}\Bigl(\frac{z_j}{h_j}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z_j\biggl)^2\nonumber\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\biggl(\int_{-\infty}^{w_j}\frac{1}{h_j}\tilde K_{2,j,h_j}\Bigl(\frac{z_j}{h_j}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z_j\biggr)^2
\Biggr]\,\mathrm{d}w_j
\Biggr\}^{1/2}\nonumber\\
&\,\,\,\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}
\Biggl\{\prod_{j=1}^d\int_{\mathbb R}(1\vee |w_j|^{p})^2
\Biggl[\mathbf{1}_{\{w_j\leq0\}}\biggl(\int_{-\infty}^{w_j}\frac{1}{h_j}\tilde K_{1,j,h_j}\Bigl(\frac{z_j}{h_j}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z_j\biggr)^2\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\mathbf{1}_{\{w_j>0\}}\biggl(\int_{w_j}^{\infty}\frac{1}{h_j}\tilde K_{1,j,h_j}\Bigl(\frac{z_j}{h_j}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z_j\biggl)^2\nonumber\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\biggl(\int_{-\infty}^{w_j}\frac{1}{h_j}\tilde K_{2,j,h_j}\Bigl(\frac{z_j}{h_j}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z_j\biggr)^2\Biggr]\,\mathrm{d}w_j\Biggr\}^{1/2}\nonumber\\
&\nonumber\tag{\text{by inequality \eqref{eq:boundprodY} of Lemma \ref{lem:boundmomY}}}\\[5pt]
&\,\,\,\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\prod_{j=1}^d\Biggl\{\underbrace{\int_{-\infty}^0(1\vee |w_j|^{p})^2
\biggl(\int_{-\infty}^{w_j}\frac{1}{h_j}\tilde K_{1,j,h_j}\Bigl(\frac{z_j}{h_j}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z_j\biggr)^2\,\mathrm{d}w_j}_{\eqqcolon I^-_{1,j,h_j}}\nonumber\\&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad
+\underbrace{\int_0^{\infty}(1\vee |w_j|^{p})^2
\biggl(\int_{w_j}^{\infty}\frac{1}{h_j}\tilde K_{1,j,h_j}\Bigl(\frac{z_j}{h_j}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z_j\biggr)^2\,\mathrm{d}w_j}_{\eqqcolon I^+_{1,j,h_j}}\nonumber\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb R}(1\vee |w_j|^{p})^2\biggl(\int_{-\infty}^{w_j}\frac{1}{h_j}\tilde K_{2,j,h_j}\Bigl(\frac{z_j}{h_j}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z_j\biggr)^2\,\mathrm{d}w_j}_{\eqqcolon I_{2,j,h_j}}\Biggr\}^{1/2}\nonumber\\
&\,\,\,\lesssim \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\prod_{j=1}^d(I^-_{1,j,h_j}+
I^+_{1,j,h_j}+I_{2,j,h_j})^{1/2}.\label{eq:var}

        {

Under noise conditions \ref{eq:E1indep}, \ref{it:E21}, and \ref{eq:E3} \hyperref[eq:E1indep]{of the Main Article}, the terms $I^-_{1,j,h_j}$, $I^+_{1,j,h_j}$, and $I_{2,j,h_j}$ can be bounded in the same way as the terms $I_2^-$, $I^+$ and $J$, respectively, in the proof of Proposition~3.1 in \citet{Dedecker2015}, pp.~248--253. Specifically, for each $j=1,\,\dots,\,d$, the following asymptotic bounds hold as $h_j \to 0^+$:
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:ub}
I^-_{1,j,h_j}=O(1),\quad I^+_{1,j,h_j}=O(1),\quad I_{2,j,h_j}=O(h_j^{-2(\beta_j-\frac{1}{2})_+}|\log h_j|^{\mathbf{1}_{\{\beta_j=1/2\}}}).
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
From \eqref{eq:var} and \eqref{eq:ub}, recalling \eqref{eq:defV} \hyperref[eq:defV]{of the Main Article}, we obtain\footnote{The symmetry of the kernel $K_j$ (equivalently, the fact that $\varphi_{K_j}$ is real and even) ensures that the quotient $Q(t) \coloneqq \varphi_{K_j}(t)\phi(t/h_j)/\varphi_{\varepsilon_j}(t/h_j)$ satisfies $Q(-t) = \overline{Q(t)}$, which guarantees that the inverse Fourier transform $\tilde K_{1,j,h_j}$ is real-valued. This is essential because the variance of a complex-valued random variable involves the modulus squared, which would introduce additional cross-terms and prevent the factorization of the variance into the three separate integral terms $I^-_{1,j,h_j}$, $I^+_{1,j,h_j}$, and $I_{2,j,h_j}$ used in the proof. Without this real-valued property, the standard variance arguments for real-valued random variables would not apply.}
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}-(K_{\mathbf{h}}\ast F_X)\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}]
&\lesssim \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\prod_{j=1}^d(I^-_{1,j,h_j}+I^+_{1,j,h_j}+I_{2,j,h_j})^{1/2}\\
&\lesssim \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\prod_{j=1}^d \sqrt{I_{2,j,h_j}} \tag{\text{because $I_{2,j,h_j}$ is the dominant term
for small $h_j$}}\\
&\lesssim \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\prod_{j=1}^d h_j^{-(\beta_j-\frac12)_+}|\log h_j|^{\mathbf{1}_{\{\beta_j=1/2\}}/2}\\
&=V(\mathbf{h})\prod_{j=1}^d |\log h_j|^{\mathbf{1}_{\{\beta_j=1/2\}}/2}\\
&=\mathcal V(\mathbf{h}),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
which concludes the proof for the $L^1$-case. 

\smallskip

For the $L^2$-extension, by Jensen's inequality,
\[ 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split}

        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}} - (K_{\mathbf{h}} \ast F_X)\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}]
&\le
\Bigl( \mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}} - (K_{\mathbf{h}} \ast F_X)\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}^2] \Bigr)^{1/2}\\
&=
\left( \int_{\mathbb R^d} \mathrm{Var}[\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}(\mathbf{x})] \, \mathrm{d}\mathbf{x} \right)^{1/2}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        {

        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
The same variance bound as in the $L^1$-case yields
$
\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}} - (K_{\mathbf{h}} \ast F_X)\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}]
\lesssim
\mathcal V(\mathbf{h})$,
which completes the proof.
