<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
The proof proceeds in two steps. First, we verify that the kernel constructed in Proposition~\ref{prop:variance} also satisfies the moment conditions required for the bias bound. Second, we combine the resulting bias and stochastic error bounds and optimize over the bandwidth vector $\mathbf{h}$.

Since the kernel estimator must simultaneously satisfy the conditions required by the bias Proposition~\ref{prop:bias} and the variance Proposition~\ref{prop:variance}, the kernel $K_j$ must be constructed so that both requirements (\emph{i})--(\emph{iii}) \hyperref[prop:variance]{of the Main Article} and the moment conditions in \eqref{eq:kernelcond} \hyperref[eq:kernelcond]{of the Main Article} are simultaneously verified. We now show that the kernel constructed above fulfills all these conditions. Set $\kappa_j \coloneqq \lceil \alpha_j\rceil$ and let $q_j \coloneqq \kappa_j + 3$. Since $\alpha_j > 0$, we have $\kappa_j = \lceil \alpha_j\rceil \ge 1$, hence $q_j = \kappa_j + 3 \ge 4 > 2$, which satisfies the requirement $q_j \ge 2$ of Proposition~\ref{prop:variance}. 

For a function $K_j\in L^1(\mathbb R)$, the ``moments'' are related to the derivatives of its Fourier transform at the origin by
$\int_{\mathbb R} z^r K_j(z)\,\mathrm{d}z = \mathrm{i}^r \varphi_{K_j}^{(r)}(0)$, $r=0,\,1,\,\dots$,
where $\varphi_{K_j}(t)=(1-t^2)^{q_j}\mathbf{1}_{[-1,\,1]}(t)$.
Since $\varphi_{K_j}$ is a polynomial of degree $2q_j$ on $[-1,\,1]$, its derivatives at $0$ satisfy
$\varphi_{K_j}^{(r)}(0) = 0$ for $r=1,\,\dots,\,q_j-1$,
and $\varphi_{K_j}(0)=1$. In particular, with $q_j=\kappa_j+3$, we have
$\varphi_{K_j}^{(r)}(0)=0$ for $r=1,\,\dots,\,\kappa_j+2$.
Therefore, we have $\int_{\mathbb R} z^r K_j(z)\,\mathrm{d}z = 0$ for $r=1,\,\dots,\,\kappa_j+2$,
and $\int_{\mathbb R} K_j(z)\,\mathrm{d}z = 1$. 
Thus, in particular, 
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{(M2)}
\int_{\mathbb R} z^r K_j(z)\,\mathrm{d}z = \delta_{0,r},\quad r=0,\,\dots,\,\lceil \alpha_j\rceil.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
This verifies the moment condition in \eqref{eq:kernelcond} \hyperref[eq:kernelcond]{of the Main Article}.

We now verify that 
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{(M3)}
\int_{\mathbb R} |z|^{\alpha_j+1}|K_j(z)|\,\mathrm{d}z < \infty. 
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Since $\varphi_{K_j}\in C^{q_j-1}(\mathbb R) = C^{\kappa_j+2}(\mathbb R)$, its inverse Fourier transform $K_j$ satisfies the decay estimate $
|K_j(x)| \lesssim (1+|x|)^{-(\kappa_j+2)}$, $x\in\mathbb R$.
Since $\kappa_j+2 > \alpha_j+2$ (because $\kappa_j = \lceil \alpha_j\rceil > \alpha_j$), we get
\[
\int_{\mathbb R} |z|^{\alpha_j+1}|K_j(z)|\,\mathrm{d}z
\lesssim \int_{\mathbb R} |z|^{\alpha_j+1}(1+|z|)^{-(\kappa_j+2)}\,\mathrm{d}z < \infty.
\]
Thus, \eqref{(M3)} is satisfied.

The parameter $q_j$ is therefore chosen sufficiently large to guarantee that conditions \eqref{(M2)} and \eqref{(M3)} are satisfied. Consequently, the kernel $K_j$ is a valid univariate kernel of order $\lceil \alpha_j\rceil$ for the anisotropic Nikol'skii regularity $\alpha_j$ in direction $j$.

Since $\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}^\ast$ is, by definition, a $\varrho_n$-approximate $L^1$-minimizer of $\|F - \widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}$ over all cumulative distribution functions $F$ on $\mathbb R^d$, see condition \eqref{eq:defminimizer} \hyperref[eq:defminimizer]{of the Main Article}, for any $\mathbf{h} \in (0,\,1)^d$ and any sequence $\varrho_n \to 0$ as $n\to\infty$,
we have $\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}^\ast - \widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} = \inf_{F \in\mathfrak F} \|F - \widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} + \varrho_n$.
Because $F_X$ is a cumulative distribution function, $\inf_{F \in\mathfrak F} \|F - \widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}\leq \|F_X - \widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}$, hence 
$$\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}^\ast - \widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} \leq \|F_X - \widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} + \varrho_n.$$
Consequently,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\begin{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:stimdist}
\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}^\ast - F_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} 
&\leq \|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}^\ast - \widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} + \|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}} - F_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} \nonumber\\
&\leq \|F_X - \widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} + \varrho_n + \|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}} - F_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} \nonumber\\
&= 2\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}} - F_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} + \varrho_n.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {\end{align}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Thus, the $L^1$-error of $\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}^\ast$ is bounded by twice the $L^1$-error of $\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}$ plus $\varrho_n$. Hence, analyzing $\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}^\ast - F_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}$ reduces to studying $\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}} - F_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}$ and then choosing $\mathbf{h}$ to minimize this upper bound. The choice 
$\varrho_n=O({n}^{-1/2})$ is seen to preserve the minimax-optimal rates of $\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}$. Noting that $n^{-1/2}\lesssim\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}, \boldsymbol{\beta}}$, we have $\varrho_n=O(\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}, \boldsymbol{\beta}})$. Taking expectations in \eqref{eq:stimdist} and applying the bound (which will be proved below)
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:ratebound}
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}} - F_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}] \lesssim \psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}, \boldsymbol{\beta}}(\log n)^{\frac12\sum_{j=1}^d\mathbf{1}_{\{\beta_j=1/2\}}},
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
we obtain
\[ 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        {\begin{split}} 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathsf E_{(\mu_X \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[ \|\widehat F^\ast_{n,\mathbf{h}} - F_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}] &\leq 2\mathsf E_{(\mu_X \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[ \|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}} - F_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}] + \varrho_n\\ &\lesssim \psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}, \boldsymbol{\beta}}\;(\log n)^{\frac12\sum_{j=1}^d\mathbf{1}_{\{\beta_j=1/2\}}}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        {\end{split}} 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]

We now prove inequality \eqref{eq:ratebound}.
By Proposition~\ref{prop:bias} (approximation error) and Proposition~\ref{prop:variance} (stochastic error), for every \(\mathbf{h}\in(0,\,1)^d\),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
&\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}-F_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}]\\
&\hspace*{3cm}\leq 
\underbrace{\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}-(K_{\mathbf{h}}\ast F_X)\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}]}_{\text{stochastic error}} 
\;+\;
\underbrace{\|F_X-(K_{\mathbf{h}}\ast F_X)\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}}_{\text{approximation error}}\\
&\hspace*{3cm}\lesssim V(\mathbf{h})\;\prod_{j=1}^d |\log h_j|^{\mathbf{1}_{\{\beta_j=1/2\}}/2}
\;+\;
\sum_{j=1}^d h_j^{\alpha_j+1}\\
&\hspace*{3cm}=\mathcal V(\mathbf{h})
\;+\;
\sum_{j=1}^d h_j^{\alpha_j+1}
\eqqcolon\psi_n(\mathbf{h}),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
where $V(\mathbf{h})$ and $\mathcal V(\mathbf{h})$ are defined in \eqref{eq:defV} and  \eqref{eq:varprox} \hyperref[eq:defV]{of the Main Article}, respectively.

We first ignore the logarithmic factors. 
Let \(\mathbf{h}_{\text{opt}}\) be a minimizer of \(\psi_n(\mathbf{h})\). 
For coordinates with \(\beta_j > 1/2\), the first-order optimality conditions give
\[
(\alpha_j+1)h_{\text{opt},j}^{\alpha_j+1} 
= \frac{\beta_j-1/2}{\sqrt{n}}\prod_{k=1}^d h_{\text{opt},k}^{-(\beta_k-1/2)}.
\]
To derive this, we set
\[
\psi_n(\mathbf{h}) =  \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\prod_{k=1}^d h_k^{-(\beta_k-1/2)_+} + \sum_{k=1}^d h_k^{\alpha_k+1},
\]
where for coordinates with \(\beta_k \le 1/2\) we have \((\beta_k-1/2) = 0\) so that the corresponding factor is \(h_k^0 = 1\). For coordinates with \(\beta_j > 1/2\), differentiating with respect to \(h_j\) gives
\[
\frac{\partial \psi_n(\mathbf{h})}{\partial h_j}
= -\frac{\beta_j-1/2}{\sqrt{n}} h_j^{-(\beta_j-1/2)-1}
\prod_{ 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@subarray} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@subarray} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@subarray} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@subarray} 
        {\begin{subarray}} 
        {\csname pseudo@subarray\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@subarray\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@subarray\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@subarray\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    {l} k=1 \\ k\ne j  
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@subarray} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@subarray} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@subarray} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@subarray} 
        {\end{subarray}} 
        {\csname pseudo@subarray\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@subarray\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@subarray\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@subarray\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    }^d h_k^{-(\beta_k-1/2)}
+ (\alpha_j+1)h_j^{\alpha_j}.
\]
Setting this equal to zero gives
\[
(\alpha_j+1)h_j^{\alpha_j+1}
= \frac{\beta_j-1/2}{\sqrt{n}}\prod_{k=1}^d h_k^{-(\beta_k-1/2)}.
\]
Solving this system yields
\[
h_{\text{opt},j} \asymp n^{-\frac{E}{2(\alpha_j+1)}}, \quad j=1,\,\dots,\,d,
\]
where the exponent $E$ is as defined in \eqref{eq:rate} \hyperref[eq:rate]{of the Main Article}. For these bandwidths, the bias and stochastic terms balance:
\[
\sum_{j=1}^d h_{\text{opt},j}^{\alpha_j+1} \asymp V(\mathbf{h}_{\text{opt}}).
\]
Substituting \(\mathbf{h}_{\text{opt}}\) into \(V(\mathbf{h})\) gives
\[
V(\mathbf{h}_{\text{opt}}) = n^{-E/2} 
= \psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}.
\]

When $\beta_j = 1/2$ for some $j$, the factor $|\log h_j|$ satisfies $|\log h_j| \asymp \log n$, since $h_j \asymp n^{-E/[2(\alpha_j+1)]}$ and the exponent is positive. Therefore, the rate acquires the extra logarithmic factor
\[
(\log n)^{\frac12\sum_{j=1}^d\mathbf{1}_{\{\beta_j=1/2\}}}.
\]
This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
The assertion is proved by explicit construction. 
Let $\Phi:\, \mathbb R \to [0,\,1]$ be a function of class $C^\infty(\mathbb R)$ such that
\[
\Phi(t) \coloneqq
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\begin{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
1, &\quad\,\,\,\, |t| \le 1, \\
\psi(|t|), & 1 < |t| < 2, \\
0, & \quad\,\,\,\, |t| \ge 2,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\end{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
where $\psi$ is a function of class $C^\infty(\mathbb R)$ that smoothly connects $1$ to $0$ in a monotone way, with $\psi^{(1)}(1) = \psi^{(1)}(2) = 0$ and all derivatives vanishing at $1$ and $2$. Define $\mathcal F[K](t) \coloneqq \Phi(t)$ for $t\in\mathbb R$. By construction, $\mathcal F[K]$ is even, of class $C^\infty(\mathbb R)$, compactly supported in $[-2,\,2]$, and satisfies $\mathcal F[K](t) = 1$ for $|t| \le 1$.


The kernel $K$ is then defined as the inverse Fourier transform of $\Phi$
\[
K(x) \coloneqq \mathcal F^{-1}[\Phi(t)](x)= \frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb R}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i} tx} \Phi(t) \,\mathrm{d}t,\quad x\in\mathbb R.
\]
Since $\Phi$ is even, $K$ is symmetric about zero:
\[
K(-x) = \frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb R} \mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i} t(-x)} \Phi(t) \, \mathrm{d}t
= \frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb R}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i} ux} \Phi(u)  \, \mathrm{d}u
= K(x),\quad x\in\mathbb R,
\]
where we used the change of variable $u = -t$ and the evenness of $\Phi$.
Since $\mathcal F[K]=\Phi$ and $\Phi\in L^2(\mathbb R)$ (because it is bounded and compactly supported), by Plancherel's theorem we have $K \in L^2(\mathbb R)$ and
\[
\|K\|_{L^2(\mathbb R)}^2 =\frac{1}{2\pi} \|\mathcal F[K]\|_{L^2(\mathbb R)}^2  =\frac{1}{2\pi} \|\Phi\|_{L^2(\mathbb R)}^2 < \infty.
\]
Moreover, since $\Phi$ is of class $C^\infty(\mathbb R)$ with compact support, integration by parts shows that its inverse Fourier transform $K$ decays faster than any power of $1/|x|$, that is, for every $N \in \mathbb N_0$ there exists $C_N > 0$ such that $
|K(x)| \le C_N (1 + |x|)^{-N}$. In particular, $K \in L^1(\mathbb R)$.

The condition $\Phi(t) = 1$ for $|t| \le 1$ implies that $\mathcal F[K](0) =\Phi(0)= 1$ and 
$(\mathcal F[K])^{(j)}(0) = \Phi^{(j)}(0)=0$ for all $j \ge 1$. 
From the relation between moments and derivatives of the Fourier transform,
$\int_{\mathbb R} x^j K(x) \, \mathrm{d}x =\mathrm{i}^j (\mathcal F[K])^{(j)}(0) = 0$ for all $j \ge 1$, and
$\int_{\mathbb R} K(x) \, \mathrm{d}x = \mathcal F[K](0) = 1$. 
Thus, $K$ annihilates all positive moments, that is, it is a kernel of infinite order. 
A kernel $K$ has thus been constructed satisfying all the required properties and the proof is complete.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{defHH}
Let $H:\,\mathbb R\rightarrow\mathbb R$ be a bounded function such that $H\in C^\infty(\mathbb R)\cap L^1(\mathbb R)$ and the following conditions hold:
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        {\begin{enumerate}} 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    [label={(\textbf{B\arabic*})}, leftmargin=*, itemsep=0pt, parsep=0pt]
    \item \label{B1} \(\int_{\mathbb{R}} H(x)\,\mathrm{d}x = 0;\)\\[-8pt]
    \item \label{B2} \(\int_0^1 |H^{(-1)}(z)|\,\mathrm{d}z > 0\), where 
          \(H^{(-1)}(z)\coloneqq \int_{-\infty}^z H(x)\,\mathrm{d}x,\; z\in\mathbb{R}\), is a primitive of \(H;\)\\[-8pt]
    \item \label{B3} \(\mathcal{F}[H](t) = 0\) for \(|t| \notin [1,\,2];\)\\[-8pt]
    \item \label{B4} \((\mathcal{F}[H])^{(\ell)}\) is continuous and \(\|(\mathcal{F}[H])^{(\ell)}\|_\infty\coloneqq \sup_{t\in\mathbb R}|(\mathcal{F}[H])^{(\ell)}(t)|<\infty\) 
          for every \(\ell \in \mathbb{N}_0;\)\\[-8pt]
    \item \label{B5} for a given \(J \in \mathbb{N}\), \(|H(x)| = O(|x|^{-2J})\) as \(|x| \to \infty\).
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate}

<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:chiquad}
Suppose that noise conditions~\ref{eq:E1indep} and~\ref{it:E22} \hyperref[eq:E1indep]{of the Main Article} hold with $\beta_j > 0$ for $j = 1,\, \dots,\, d$. Moreover, assume that each $\varphi_{\varepsilon_j}$ is differentiable and satisfies, for some exponent $\beta'_j \geq \beta_j$ and a constant $B_j>0$,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:condder}
|t|^{\beta'_j}|\varphi^{(1)}_{\varepsilon_j}(t)| \leq B_j, \quad t \in \mathbb{R},
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
where $\varphi^{(1)}_{\varepsilon_j}$ denotes the first derivative of $\varphi_{\varepsilon_j}$.

For each $j = 1,\, \dots,\, d$, define
\[
q_j(x_j) \coloneqq \left(\frac{1}{c_j} g_{s_j}\!\left(\frac{\cdot}{c_j}\right) \ast f_{\varepsilon_j}\right)(x_j)
= \int_{\mathbb{R}} \frac{1}{c_j} g_{s_j}\!\left(\frac{x_j-u}{c_j}\right) f_{\varepsilon_j}(u)\,\mathrm{d}u, \quad x_j \in \mathbb{R},
\]
for $s_j \in (0,\,1)$ and $c_j > 0$.
Then, for any $h_j \in (0,\,1)$,
\[
\int_{\mathbb{R}} \left[\int_{\mathbb{R}} H\!\left(\frac{x_j - u}{h_j}\right) f_{\varepsilon_j}(u) \, \mathrm{d}u \right]^2 q^{-1}_j(x_j) \, \mathrm{d}x_j = O(h_j^{2\beta_j + 1}).
\]
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:foexp}
Let $m \ge 2$ be an integer. For each $\ell \in \{1,\, \dots,\, m\}$, let $A_\ell$ and $B_\ell$ be real numbers (or real-valued functions on some domain $\mathcal X$) with
$\max_{1 \le \ell \le m} |A_\ell| \le M_A$ and 
$\max_{1 \le \ell \le m} |B_\ell| \le M_B$
for some constants $0<M_A,\, M_B < \infty$. Let $\delta \in \mathbb R$ satisfy $|\delta| \le 1$. Then,
\[
\prod_{\ell=1}^m (A_\ell + \delta B_\ell)
= \prod_{\ell=1}^m A_\ell
+ \delta \sum_{r=1}^m B_r \prod_{\substack{\ell=1 \\ \ell \ne r}}^m A_\ell
+ R_m(\delta),
\]
where the remainder 
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {

        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:remainder}
R_m(\delta)
\coloneqq \delta^2\sum_{k=2}^m \,\sum_{\substack{S \subset \{1,\dots,m\} \\ |S| = k}}\, \delta^{k-2} \Bigg( \prod_{j \in S} B_j \Bigg) \prod_{\substack{\ell=1 \\ \ell \notin S}}^m A_\ell,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
with the convention that products over an empty set are equal to $1$. 
Moreover, the remainder satisfies
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {

        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation}

|R_m(\delta)| \le \delta^2 \sum_{k=2}^m

In particular, $R_m(\delta) = O(\delta^2)$ as $\delta \to 0$ for fixed $m,\, M_A,\, M_B$. If the $A_\ell$ and $B_\ell$ are functions, the bound holds pointwise for every $x \in \mathcal X$.

<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
We prove the lemma by induction on \(m\), using the implicit definition of the remainder \(R_m(\delta)\) as the difference between the full product and the first two terms of the expansion. We begin with the base case $m = 2$. 
We have
\[
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\begin{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\prod_{\ell=1}^2 (A_\ell + \delta B_\ell) 
&= (A_1 + \delta B_1)(A_2 + \delta B_2) \\
&= A_1 A_2 + \delta A_1 B_2 + \delta B_1 A_2 + \delta^2 B_1 B_2 \\
&= \prod_{\ell=1}^2 A_\ell
+ \delta \sum_{r=1}^2 B_r \prod_{\substack{\ell=1 \\ \ell \ne r}}^2 A_\ell
+ \delta^2 B_1 B_2.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\end{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
Thus, $R_2(\delta) = \delta^2 B_1 B_2$. This agrees with the general formula for the remainder term in \eqref{eq:remainder}.
Using the bound $\max_{1 \le \ell \le 2} |B_\ell| \le M_B$, we obtain 
$|R_2(\delta)| = \delta^2 |B_1 B_2| \le \delta^2 M_B^2$, which 
matches the bound claimed in \eqref{eq:remainder1} for $m=2$.


We now consider the inductive step. Assume the lemma holds for $m \ge 2$, \emph{i.e.}, the expansion and the bound \eqref{eq:remainder1} hold for $m$. We prove it for $m+1$.
Let $P_m \coloneqq \prod_{\ell=1}^m (A_\ell + \delta B_\ell)$. 
Multiplying by the $(m+1)$-th factor and using the induction hypothesis, we obtain
\[
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\begin{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
P_{m+1} &= P_m (A_{m+1} + \delta B_{m+1})\\
&=\biggl(\prod_{\ell=1}^m A_\ell
+ \delta \sum_{r=1}^m B_r \prod_{\substack{\ell=1 \\ \ell \ne r}}^m A_\ell
+ R_m(\delta)\biggr)(A_{m+1} + \delta B_{m+1})\\
&= \left( \prod_{\ell=1}^m A_\ell \right) A_{m+1}
+ \delta \left( \prod_{\ell=1}^m A_\ell \right) B_{m+1} + \delta \left( \sum_{r=1}^m B_r \prod_{\substack{\ell=1 \\ \ell \ne r}}^m A_\ell \right) A_{m+1}
+ \delta^2 \left( \sum_{r=1}^m B_r \prod_{\substack{\ell=1 \\ \ell \ne r}}^m A_\ell \right) B_{m+1} \\
&\hspace*{8.7cm} + R_m(\delta) A_{m+1} + \delta R_m(\delta) B_{m+1}\\
&=\prod_{\ell=1}^{m+1} A_\ell+\delta \left[ B_{m+1} \prod_{\substack{\ell=1 \\ \ell \ne m+1}}^{m+1} A_\ell + \sum_{r=1}^m B_r \prod_{\substack{\ell=1 \\ \ell \ne r}}^{m+1} A_\ell\right]+
 \delta^2 \left( \sum_{r=1}^m B_r \prod_{\substack{\ell=1 \\ \ell \ne r}}^m A_\ell \right) B_{m+1}\\
&\hspace*{8.7cm}+ R_m(\delta) A_{m+1} + \delta R_m(\delta) B_{m+1}\\
&=\prod_{\ell=1}^{m+1} A_\ell+\delta\sum_{r=1}^{m+1} B_r \prod_{\substack{\ell=1 \\ \ell \ne r}}^{m+1} A_\ell+
\underbrace{ \delta^2 \left( \sum_{r=1}^m B_r \prod_{\substack{\ell=1 \\ \ell \ne r}}^m A_\ell \right) B_{m+1}+ R_m(\delta) A_{m+1} + \delta R_m(\delta) B_{m+1}}_{\eqqcolon R_{m+1}(\delta)},
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\end{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
that is, the desired expansion
\[
P_{m+1}
= \prod_{\ell=1}^{m+1} A_\ell
+ \delta \sum_{r=1}^{m+1} B_r \prod_{\substack{\ell=1 \\ \ell \ne r}}^{m+1} A_\ell
+ R_{m+1}(\delta).
\]
We now bound the remainder term $R_{m+1}(\delta)$. Using the bounds on $A_\ell$ and $B_r$, we obtain
\[
\left| \delta^2 \left( \sum_{r=1}^m B_r \prod_{\substack{\ell=1 \\ \ell \ne r}}^m A_\ell \right) B_{m+1} \right|
\le \delta^2 m M_B^2 M_A^{m-1}.
\]
Also, using the induction bound on $R_m(\delta)$, we get
\[
|R_m(\delta) A_{m+1}| \le \delta^2 \sum_{k=2}^m

\] 
This completes the induction.

Since $m$, $M_A$ and $M_B$ are fixed (independent of $\delta$), the remainder satisfies $R_m(\delta) = O(\delta^2)$ as $\delta \to 0$. The bound is pointwise if the $A_\ell$ and $B_\ell$ are functions, because the inequalities hold for each fixed $x\in\mathcal X$.


We now prove relationship \eqref{eq:remainder}.
The remainder is defined implicitly as
\[
R_m(\delta) = \prod_{\ell=1}^m (A_\ell + \delta B_\ell)
- \prod_{\ell=1}^m A_\ell
- \delta \sum_{r=1}^m B_r \prod_{\substack{\ell=1 \\ \ell \ne r}}^m A_\ell.
\]
Expanding the product, we obtain
\[
\prod_{\ell=1}^m (A_\ell + \delta B_\ell)
= \sum_{S \subseteq \{1,\dots,m\}} \delta^{|S|}
\Bigg( \prod_{j \in S} B_j \Bigg)
\prod_{\substack{\ell=1 \\ \ell \notin S}}^m A_\ell,
\]
which follows by induction on $m\geq 2$.
Separating the terms according to $|S|$, the terms corresponding to $|S|=0$ and $|S|=1$ exactly cancel with the first two terms in the definition of $R_m(\delta)$ (the product of the \(A_\ell\)'s and the first-order correction). Hence,
\[
R_m(\delta)
= \sum_{k=2}^m \sum_{\substack{S \subset \{1,\dots,m\} \\ |S| = k}} \delta^k
\Bigg( \prod_{j \in S} B_j \Bigg)
\prod_{\substack{\ell=1 \\ \ell \notin S}}^m A_\ell.
\]
Factoring out $\delta^2$, we get the expression in \eqref{eq:remainder}.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
The strategy is to construct two probability densities \(f_0\) and \(f_1\) in the anisotropic Nikol'skii class \(\mathcal N_1^d(\boldsymbol\alpha,\, L)\) such that their cumulative distribution functions are well separated in the \(L^1\)-distance, while their convolutions with the noise density, \(f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon\) and \(f_1 \ast f_\varepsilon\), are close in \(\chi^2\)-divergence. This makes the two resulting observation distributions difficult to distinguish, allowing Le Cam's two-point method to yield the minimax rate.

We distinguish two cases depending on the cardinality of $\mathcal P$.
If \(|\mathcal P| = 0\), there are no active coordinates; we follow the approach in the literature based on a translation perturbation of the baseline density \(f_0\). The proof is an adaptation to the cumulative distribution function setting of that of \citet{ComteLacour2013}. If \(|\mathcal P| \ge 1\), the translation perturbation used in the previous case does not extend to the case where multiple active coordinates are present, as it would not yield a perturbed density belonging to the anisotropic Nikol'skii class with a constant that is independent of \(n\). A novel construction of the two test densities \(f_0\) and \(f_1\) is therefore required. 

The two cases are treated separately: we first handle \(|\mathcal P| = 0\), and then address \(|\mathcal P| \ge 1\).\\

                                                       
\noindent\textbf{Case $|\mathcal P| = 0$.}\\[5pt]
The proof follows the general strategy of \cite{ComteLacour2013} for density deconvolution, with adaptations required for the cumulative distribution function setting. Following \citet{dedecker:michel:2013} for the supersmooth case, we exploit the primitive \(H^{(-1)}\) of the kernel \(H\) to control the \(L^1\)-distance between cumulative distribution functions. 
However, the present setting involves ordinary smooth noise and anisotropic Nikol'skii classes; the verification that the perturbed densities belong to \(\mathcal{N}_1^d(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,L)\) is new and does not appear in either \citet{ComteLacour2013} or \citet{dedecker:michel:2013}. These modifications yield a lower bound for the \(L^1\)-risk over anisotropic Nikol’skii classes.

We now proceed with the detailed construction. As in the proof of Theorem~3 in \citet
{ComteLacour2013}, pp.~599--603, we define the density
\[f_0(\mathbf{x})\coloneqq\prod_{j=1}^d\frac{1}{c_j}g_{s_j}\biggl(\frac{x_j}{c_j}\biggr), \quad\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb R^d,\]
where $g_{s_j}$ is the density of a symmetric stable law with characteristic function $\mathcal F [g_{s_j}](t)=\mathrm{e}^{-|t|^{s_j}}$, $t\in\mathbb R$, with parameters ${s_j}\in(0,\,1)$ and $c_j>0$.

Let $H$ be an even function satisfying Definition~\ref{defHH} with $J\in\mathbb N$ such that \(2J > \sum_{j=1}^d(s_j+1)\), \emph{e.g.}, \(J=d+1\). Then, by condition \ref{B5},
\(|H(x)| = O(|x|^{-2J})\) as \(|x| \to \infty\). 

Since \(\mathcal P = \emptyset\), we have \(\beta_j \le 1/2\) for all \(j=1,\,\dots,\,d\). 
We set \(h_j = 1\) for every \(j=1,\,\dots,\,d\).
For $\mathbf{k}_0=(1,\,\dots,\,1)^\top\in\mathcal{K}$, define the grid points
$x_{\mathbf{k}_0j} \coloneqq a_j + {k_0}_j(b_j-a_j)h_j=b_j$, $j=1,\,\dots,\,d$,
where $a_j,\,b_j$ are chosen so that $b_j-a_j>0$ and
\[
\int_0^{(b_j-a_j)/2} |H^{(-1)}(-z)|\,\mathrm{d}z > 0.
\]
If \(H\) is even, since \(\int_{\mathbb{R}} H(x)\,\mathrm{d}x = 0\) by condition \ref{B1}, then its primitive \(H^{(-1)}\) is odd. Indeed, for any \(z\in\mathbb{R}\),
\[
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\begin{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
H^{(-1)}(-z) &= \int_{-\infty}^{-z} H(x)\,\mathrm{d}x \\
&= \int_{\infty}^{z} H(-u)\,(-\mathrm{d}u) \qquad (x = -u,\; \mathrm{d}x = -\mathrm{d}u)\\
&= \int_{z}^{\infty} H(-u)\,\mathrm{d}u \\
&= \int_{z}^{\infty} H(u)\,\mathrm{d}u \qquad (\text{since } H \text{ is even})\\
&= \int_{\mathbb R} H(u)\,\mathrm{d}u - \int_{-\infty}^{z} H(u)\,\mathrm{d}u \\
&= 0 - H^{(-1)}(z) = -H^{(-1)}(z).
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\end{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
Thus, \(H^{(-1)}(-z) = -H^{(-1)}(z)\), so \(|H^{(-1)}(-z)| = |H^{(-1)}(z)|\) for all $z\in\mathbb R$.
Condition \ref{B2} gives \(\int_0^1 |H^{(-1)}(z)|\,\mathrm{d}z > 0\). Hence, by choosing \(a_j,\, b_j\) such that \((b_j-a_j)/2= 1\), \emph{e.g.}, \(a_j = 0,\; b_j = 2\), we obtain
\[
\int_{0}^{(b_j-a_j)/2} |H^{(-1)}(-z)|\,\mathrm{d}z =\int_{0}^{(b_j-a_j)/2} |H^{(-1)}(z)|\,\mathrm{d}z= \int_0^1 |H^{(-1)}(z)|\,\mathrm{d}z > 0,
\]
which verifies the required positivity condition. 
The choice \(a_j=0\) and \(b_j=2\) simplifies the construction: indeed, \(x_{\mathbf{k}_0j} = b_j=2\). The shifted arguments become
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:translate}
\frac{x_j - x_{\mathbf{k}_0j}}{2h_j} = \frac{x_j - x_{\mathbf{k}_0j}}{2}=\frac{x_j}{2} - {k_0}_j=\frac{x_j}{2} - 1.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
For a constant $c>0$ small enough to be specified later, define the function
\[
f_1(\mathbf{x}) \coloneqq f_0(\mathbf{x}) + cn^{-\frac12}\prod_{j=1}^d  H\!\left(\frac{x_j}{2}-1\right),\quad \mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^d,
\]
which will be proved to be a perturbed probability density. The constant \(c>0\) is chosen sufficiently small, independently of \(n\), to satisfy two requirements: 
first, to ensure that \(f_1\ge 0\) for all sufficiently large \(n\) (this is verified below); 
second, to guarantee that the constant in the \(\chi^2\)-divergence bound 
\(\chi^2(f_1 \ast f_\varepsilon \,\|\, f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon) \le C c^2n^{-1}\) 
can be made arbitrarily small. 
The independence of \(c\) from \(n\) is crucial: it ensures that the separation rate 
\(\|F_1 - F_0\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} \asymp c n^{-1/2}\) 
is of the correct order \(n^{-1/2}\), with a constant that does not vanish as \(n\to\infty\).

Since $H$ integrates to zero by condition \ref{B1}, which implies 
$$\int_{\mathbb R^d}\prod_{j=1}^d  H\!\left(\frac{x_j}{2}-1\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf x=0,$$ 
we have $\int_{\mathbb{R}^d} f_1(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x} = \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} f_0(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x} = 1$. 
We now show that \(f_1\) is strictly positive for all sufficiently large \(n\).
We have
\[
|f_1(\mathbf{x})-f_0(\mathbf{x})|
\le c n^{-1/2}\prod_{j=1}^d \left|H\!\left(\frac{x_j-2}{2}\right)\right|.
\]
For any fixed \(R>0\), the density \(f_0\) attains a positive minimum on the compact set \(\{\|\mathbf{x}\|_2\le R\}\), where \(\|\mathbf{x}\|_2 \coloneqq (\sum_{j=1}^d x_j^2)^{1/2}\) denotes the Euclidean norm of \(\mathbf{x}\). Thus, for \(c>0\) small enough depending on \(R\), we have 
$
c\, n^{-1/2} (C_H)^d \le (1/2) \inf_{\|\mathbf{x}\|_2\le R} f_0(\mathbf{x})$,
where \(C_H \coloneqq \sup_{z\in\mathbb R} |H(z)| < \infty\).
Fix \(R_0 = 4\sqrt{d}+1\). For \(\|\mathbf{x}\|_2 > R_0\), since \(\|\mathbf{x}\|_2 \le \sqrt{d}\max_{j=1,\dots,d}|x_j|\), we have \(\max_{j=1,\dots,d}|x_j| \ge R_0/\sqrt{d} > 4\). Choose \(j_0\) such that \(|x_{j_0}| = \max_{j=1,\dots,d}|x_j|\). Then, \(|x_{j_0}| > 4\). Therefore,
\[
\left|\frac{x_{j_0}-2}{2}\right| \ge \frac{|x_{j_0}|-2}{2} \ge \frac{|x_{j_0}|}{4}.
\]
Using the decay property of \(H\) from condition \ref{B5}, \(|H(z)| \le C_J (1+|z|)^{-2J}\), we obtain
\[
\left|H\!\left(\frac{x_{j_0}-2}{2}\right)\right|
\le C_J \left(1+\frac{|x_{j_0}|}{4}\right)^{-2J}
\le C_J' |x_{j_0}|^{-2J}.
\]
For the other coordinates \(j \neq j_0\), we use the boundedness of \(H\).
Hence,
\[
\prod_{j=1}^d \left|H\!\left(\frac{x_j-2}{2}\right)\right|
\le C_J' C_H^{d-1} |x_{j_0}|^{-2J}.
\]
Consequently, $|f_1(\mathbf{x})-f_0(\mathbf{x})| \lesssim n^{-1/2} |x_{j_0}|^{-2J}$. Since $|x_{j_0}| \ge \|\mathbf{x}\|_2/\sqrt{d}$, we have $$|x_{j_0}|^{-2J} \le (\sqrt{d})^{2J} \|\mathbf{x}\|_2^{-2J}.$$ Hence, $|f_1(\mathbf{x})-f_0(\mathbf{x})| \lesssim n^{-1/2} \|\mathbf{x}\|_2^{-2J}$.
Now, note that there exists a constant $c_0>0$ such that for all $\|\mathbf{x}\|_2 > R_0$, we have $f_0(\mathbf{x}) \ge c_0 (1+\|\mathbf{x}\|_2)^{-\sum_{j=1}^d (s_j+1)}$. Indeed, for $s_j \in (0,1)$, the symmetric stable density satisfies $g_{s_j}(x) \sim C_{s_j} |x|^{-1-s_j}$ as $|x|\to\infty$.
Therefore,
\[
\frac{|f_1(\mathbf{x})-f_0(\mathbf{x})|}{f_0(\mathbf{x})}
\lesssim n^{-1/2}
\|\mathbf{x}\|_2^{-2J+\sum_{j=1}^d (s_j+1)}.
\]
Since \(J\in\mathbb N\) is chosen so that \(2J > \sum_{j=1}^d (s_j+1)\), the exponent of \(\|\mathbf{x}\|_2\) is negative. Hence, the right-hand side is maximal at \(\|\mathbf{x}\|_2 = R_0\). Thus, for all \(\|\mathbf{x}\|_2 > R_0\),
\[
\frac{|f_1(\mathbf{x})-f_0(\mathbf{x})|}{f_0(\mathbf{x})}
\lesssim n^{-1/2} R_0^{-2J+\sum_{j=1}^d (s_j+1)}.
\]
Since \(n^{-1/2} \le 1\) for \(n\ge 1\), by choosing \(c>0\) sufficiently small (depending on \(C_J'\), \(d\), \(R_0\), \(C_H\), and \(\inf_{\|\mathbf{x}\|_2\le R_0} f_0(\mathbf{x})\)), we can ensure that
$
|f_1(\mathbf{x})-f_0(\mathbf{x})| \le f_0(\mathbf{x})/2$ for all $\|\mathbf{x}\|_2 > R_0$,
for all sufficiently large \(n\). For the compact set \(\{\|\mathbf{x}\|_2 \le R_0\}\), the uniform bound
$
|f_1(\mathbf{x})-f_0(\mathbf{x})|
\le c n^{-1/2} (C_H)^d$
ensures that, for \(c>0\) small enough depending on \(\inf_{\|\mathbf{x}\|_2\le R_0} f_0(\mathbf{x})\) and for all sufficiently large \(n\),
\[
|f_1(\mathbf{x})-f_0(\mathbf{x})|
\le \frac12 \inf_{\|\mathbf{x}\|_2\le R_0} f_0(\mathbf{x})
\le \frac12 f_0(\mathbf{x}),
\]
because \(\inf_{\|\mathbf{x}\|_2\le R_0} f_0(\mathbf{x}) > 0\) is a fixed positive constant.

Combining the two regimes, for all sufficiently large \(n\) and all \(\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb R^d\),
$
f_1(\mathbf{x}) = f_0(\mathbf{x}) + [f_1(\mathbf{x})-f_0(\mathbf{x})]
\ge f_0(\mathbf{x}) - f_0(\mathbf{x})/2
= f_0(\mathbf{x})/2 > 0$.
Thus, \(f_1\) is a strictly positive probability density for all sufficiently large \(n\).

\smallskip

The rest of the proof can be split into three steps.\\[5pt]
                                               
                                              
\noindent{\em{$\bullet$ $f_0$ and $f_1$ belong to the anisotropic Nikol'skii class $\mathcal N_1^d(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,L)$.}}\\[3pt]
We begin by showing that $f_0 \in \mathcal N_1^d(\boldsymbol\alpha,\, L')$ for some suitable constant $L'>0$. 
Recalling that \(\partial_j^{m_j} f_0\) denotes the partial derivative of $f_0$ of order \(m_j\coloneqq \lceil \alpha_j \rceil -1\) with respect to the \(j\)-th coordinate, for any $\delta\in\mathbb{R}$, we have
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\bigl\|\Delta_{\delta\mathbf e_j}\partial^{m_j}_j f_0
\bigr\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}
&= \int_{\mathbb{R}^d}\big| \Delta_{\delta\mathbf e_j}
\partial^{m_j}_j f_0
(\mathbf{x})\big| \, \mathrm{d}\mathbf{x} \\
&=\frac{1}{c_j^{m_j}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^d}
\biggl| g^{(m_j)}_{s_j}\biggl(\frac{x_j+\delta}{c_j}\biggr)-g^{(m_j)}_{s_j}\biggl(\frac{x_j}{c_j}\biggr)\biggr| \prod_{\substack{r=1 \\ r \neq j}}^d\frac{1}{c_r}\biggl|g_{s_r}\!\left(\frac{x_r}{c_r}\right)\biggr|\, \mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}
\\&=\frac{1}{c_j^{m_j}}c_j\bigg\| g^{(m_j)}_{s_j}\bigg(\cdot+\frac{\delta}{c_j}\bigg)-g^{(m_j)}_{s_j}(\cdot)\bigg\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\underbrace{\prod_{\substack{r=1 \\ r \neq j}}^d\|g_{s_r}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}}_{=1}\\
&=\frac{1}{c_j^{m_j-1}}\bigg\| g^{(m_j)}_{s_j}\bigg(\cdot+\frac{\delta}{c_j}\bigg)-g^{(m_j)}_{s_j}(\cdot)\bigg\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
We prove that, for every \(j=1,\,\dots,\,d\), setting \(\delta_j\coloneqq \delta/c_j\),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:main-est}
\bigl\| g^{(m_j)}_{s_j}(\cdot+\delta_j)-g^{(m_j)}_{s_j}(\cdot)\bigr\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\le C(\alpha_j,\,c_j,\,s_j)\,|\delta|^{\alpha_j-m_j}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
We now distinguish two cases: \(|\delta|\leq 1\) and \(|\delta|>1\).
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        {\begin{itemize}} 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\item Case \(|\delta|\leq1\). The proof uses the elementary fact that all derivatives of a symmetric stable density are in \(L^1(\mathbb R)\).
Indeed, for \(s\in(0,\,1)\),
\[
g_s^{(k)}(x) \sim C_{s,k} |x|^{-(1+s+k)} \quad \text{as }|x|\to\infty,
\]
and \(g_s^{(k)}\) is bounded near \(0\), so \(g_s^{(k)}\in L^1(\mathbb R)\) for every \(k\ge 0\).
Using the mean-value theorem,
\[
\bigl\| g^{(m_j)}_{s_j}(\cdot+\delta_j)-g^{(m_j)}_{s_j}(\cdot)\bigr\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}
\le |\delta_j|\, \bigl\| g^{(m_j+1)}_{s_j}\bigr\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}
= c_j^{-1}\bigl\| g^{(m_j+1)}_{s_j}\bigr\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\, |\delta|.
\]
Because \(|\delta|\le 1\) and \(\alpha_j-m_j\le 1\), we have \(|\delta|\le |\delta|^{\alpha_j-m_j}\). Hence
\[
\bigl\| g^{(m_j)}_{s_j}(\cdot+\delta_j)-g^{(m_j)}_{s_j}(\cdot)\bigr\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}
\le C(\alpha_j,\,c_j,\,s_j)\, |\delta|^{\alpha_j-m_j},
\]
with 
\[
C(\alpha_j,\,c_j,\,s_j)\coloneqq c_j^{-1}\bigl\| g^{(m_j+1)}_{s_j}\bigr\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} < \infty.
\]
\item Case \(|\delta|>1\). Recall that, for \(s\in(0,\,1)\), 
\[
g_s(x) \sim C_s \, |x|^{-(1+s)}\,\,\,\text{ as }|x|\to\infty,
\]
and, for any integer \(k\ge 0\), the derivatives behave as
\(
g_s^{(k)}(x) \sim C_{s,k} \, |x|^{-(1+s+k)}\) for \(|x|\to\infty\).
This follows from term-by-term differentiation of the asymptotic expansion.
The function \(|x|^{-(1+s+k)}\) is integrable at infinity when \(
1+s+k > 1\), which is always true because \(s>0\) and \(k\ge0\).  
Near the origin, \(g_s^{(k)}\) is continuous and hence bounded on compact sets, so it is locally integrable.
Consequently, \(\|g_s^{(k)}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} <\infty\) for every \(k\ge0\).
In particular, \(\|g_{s_j}^{(m_j)}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} < \infty\), which yields
\[
\bigl\| g^{(m_j)}_{s_j}(\cdot+\delta_j)-g^{(m_j)}_{s_j}(\cdot)\bigr\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}
\le 2\bigl\|g_{s_j}^{(m_j)}\bigr\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}.
\]
Because \(|\delta|>1\) implies \(|\delta|^{\alpha_j-m_j}> 1\), we obtain
\[
\bigl\| g^{(m_j)}_{s_j}(\cdot+\delta_j)-g^{(m_j)}_{s_j}(\cdot)\bigr\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}
< 2\bigl\|g_{s_j}^{(m_j)}\bigr\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\,|\delta|^{\alpha_j-m_j}.
\]
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        {\end{itemize}} 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Thus, \eqref{eq:main-est} holds for all \(\alpha_j>0\). We conclude that \(f_0\in\mathcal N_1^d(\boldsymbol\alpha,\,L')\), with \[L'\geq \max_{1\leq j\leq d}\max\big\{C(\alpha_j,\,c_j,\,s_j),\,2\|g_{s_j}^{(m_j)}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\big\}.\]



\smallskip

We now prove that \((f_1-f_0)\in\mathcal N_1^d(\boldsymbol\alpha,\,L'')\) for some suitable constant \(L''>0\). For every \(j=1,\,\dots,\,d\), set
\[
H_{j}(x_j)\coloneqq H\!\left(\frac{x_j - 2}{2}\right)
\quad\text{and}\quad
H_{j}^{(m_j)}(x_j)\coloneqq 2^{-m_j} H^{(m_j)}\!\left(\frac{x_j - 2}{2}\right), \quad x_j\in\mathbb R.
\]
Since the partial derivative \(\partial_j^{m_j}\) acts only on the \(j\)-th factor and \(f_1-f_0 = c n^{-1/2} \prod_{r=1}^d H_r(x_r)\), we have
\[
\partial_j^{m_j} (f_1-f_0)(\mathbf{x})
= c n^{-1/2} H_{j}^{(m_j)}(x_j) 
   \prod_{\substack{r=1 \\ r \neq j}}^d H_{r}(x_r),\quad \mathbf{x}\in\mathbb R^d.
\]
For any \(\delta\in \mathbb R\), 
\[
\Delta_{\delta\mathbf e_j}\partial_j^{m_j} (f_1-f_0)(\mathbf{x})
= c n^{-1/2}
\bigl[ H_{j}^{(m_j)}(x_j + \delta) - H_{j}^{(m_j)}(x_j) \bigr]
\prod_{\substack{r=1 \\ r \neq j}}^d H_{r}(x_r),\quad \mathbf{x}\in\mathbb R^d.
\]
Because the variables separate, the \(L^1\)-norm is bounded by
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:diff}
\big\|\Delta_{\delta\mathbf e_j}\partial_j^{m_j} (f_1-f_0)\big\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} \le 
c n^{-1/2}
\big\| H_{j}^{(m_j)}(\cdot + \delta) - H_{j}^{(m_j)}(\cdot)\big\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}
\prod_{\substack{r=1 \\ r \neq j}}^d \| H_{r} \|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}.

        {

    }

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
For \(r \neq j\), we have \(\| H_{r} \|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}= 2\|H\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} <\infty\).
Because \(\mathcal F[H]\) has compact support (condition \ref{B3}) and is \(C^\infty\) (condition \ref{B4}), its inverse Fourier transform \(H\) is smooth, bounded, and decays faster than any polynomial. The same holds for all derivatives \(H^{(k)}\), so \(H^{(k)}\in L^1(\mathbb R)\) for every \(k\ge 0\). For any \(\delta\in\mathbb R\),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        {

        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\item if \(|\delta|\le 1\),
    \[
    \|H^{(m_j)}(\cdot+\delta)-H^{(m_j)}(\cdot)\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}
    \le |\delta|\,\|H^{(m_j+1)}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}
    \le \|H^{(m_j+1)}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} |\delta|^{\alpha_j-m_j},
    \]
    because \(|\delta|\le |\delta|^{\alpha_j-m_j}\);\\
\item if \(|\delta|> 1\),
    \[
    \|H^{(m_j)}(\cdot+\delta)-H^{(m_j)}(\cdot)\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}
    \le 2\|H^{(m_j)}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} < 2\|H^{(m_j)}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} |\delta|^{\alpha_j-m_j}.
    \]
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize}

        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Thus, there exists \(L_j>0\) such that
\[
\|H^{(m_j)}(\cdot+\delta)-H^{(m_j)}(\cdot)\|_1 \le L_j|\delta|^{\alpha_j-m_j}.
\]
Hence, for each coordinate direction \(j\), the function \(H\) satisfies the one-dimensional Nikol'skii condition 
\(H\in \mathcal N_1^1(\alpha_j,\, L_j)\). For the \(j\)-th factor, the change of variable \(u_j = x_j - 2\) gives
\[

\big\| H_{j}^{(m_j)}(\cdot + \delta) - H_{j}^{(m_j)}(\cdot) \big\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} 
&= \frac{2}{2^{m_j}} 
   \bigg\| H^{(m_j)}\bigg(\cdot + \frac{\delta}{2}\bigg) - H^{(m_j)}(\cdot) \bigg\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\\
&\le \frac{2}{2^{m_j}} \, L_j 
   \biggl(\frac{|\delta|}{2}\biggr)^{\alpha_j - m_j}\\&= L_j 2^{1-\alpha_j} |\delta|^{\alpha_j - m_j}.

        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
Inserting these bounds in \eqref{eq:diff}, we obtain
\[

\bigl\|\Delta_{\delta\mathbf e_j}\partial_j^{m_j} (f_1-f_0)\bigr\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} 
&\le c n^{-1/2} L_j 2^{1-\alpha_j} |\delta|^{\alpha_j - m_j}
   \prod_{\substack{r=1 \\ r \neq j}}^d 2 \|H\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\\
&= c  n^{-1/2}2^{d-\alpha_j} L_j \|H\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}^{d-1}
   |\delta|^{\alpha_j - m_j}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        {

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
Define $
D_j \coloneqq c\,2^{d-\alpha_j} L_j \|H\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}^{d-1}$.
Then, $
\bigl\|\Delta_{\delta\mathbf e_j}\partial_j^{m_j} (f_1-f_0)\bigr\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} 
\le D_j n^{-1/2} |\delta|^{\alpha_j - m_j}$. Since \(n^{-1/2} \le 1\) for \(n\ge 1\), we have
\[
\bigl\|\Delta_{\delta\mathbf e_j}\partial_j^{m_j} (f_1-f_0)\bigr\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} 
\le D_j |\delta|^{\alpha_j - m_j}.
\]
with \(D_j\) independent of \(n\). Therefore, \((f_1-f_0)\in \mathcal N_1^d(\boldsymbol\alpha,\,L'')\), where $L'' \coloneqq \max_{1\le j\le d} D_j < \infty$.
We conclude that \(f_0,\,f_1\in\mathcal N_1^d(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,L)\), with 
$L \coloneqq L' + L'' < \infty$. This completes the verification that both densities belong to the anisotropic Nikol'skii class with a constant independent of \(n\) when \(\mathcal P = \emptyset\).\\[5pt]



                                                
                                                 
\noindent{\em{$\bullet$ $\chi^2$-divergence.}}\\[3pt]
We recall that the \(\chi^2\)-divergence between two probability densities \(p\) and \(q\) on \(\mathbb R^d\) is defined as
\[
\chi^2(p\,\|\, q)\coloneqq \int_{\mathbb R^d} \frac{[p(\mathbf{x}) - q(\mathbf{x})]^2}{q(\mathbf{x})}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x},
\]
whenever \(q>0\) on the support of \(p\); otherwise \(\chi^2(p\,\|\, q)=\infty\). 


Because the noise components are independent (condition \ref{eq:E1indep} \hyperref[sec:noise]{of the Main Article}), the convolution factorizes coordinate‑wise. 
Condition \ref{it:E22}, together with condition \ref{eq:E3} \hyperref[sec:noise]{of the Main Article}, implies that 
$\varphi_{\varepsilon_j}$ is continuously differentiable and that its derivative satisfies the bound 
$|\varphi^{(1)}_{\varepsilon_j}(t)| = |\varphi_{\varepsilon_j}(t)|^2\, |r^{(1)}_{\varepsilon_j}(t)|
\le c_{1,j}^2\, d_{1,j}\, (1+|t|)^{-\beta_j}
$, $t\in\mathbb R$,
which is precisely condition \eqref{eq:condder} with $\beta'_j=\beta_j$ and $B_j=c_{1,j}^2d_{1,j}$.
Using Lemma~\ref{lem:chiquad}, which gives a bound for each coordinate, it is shown in Step 2 of the proof of Theorem~3 in \cite{ComteLacour2013}, p.~601, that
the $\chi^2$-divergence between the convolved densities \(f_1\ast f_{\varepsilon}\) and \(f_0\ast f_{\varepsilon}\) is of order $O(n^{-1})$. Specifically, 
\[\chi^2\bigl(f_1\ast f_{\varepsilon}\,\|\,f_0\ast f_{\varepsilon}\bigr)\lesssim 
\frac 1n\prod_{j=1}^d h_j^{-(2\beta_j+1)} h_j^{2\beta_j+1}=O(n^{-1}).\]

                                                              


                                            
       


\noindent{\em{$\bullet$ Separation rate and lower bound rate}}\\[3pt]
Let \(F_i\) denote the cumulative distribution function associated with the density \(f_i\), for \(i = 0,\,1\). 
The following two facts will be used to establish the separation between \(F_0\) and \(F_1\), which is a key ingredient in the lower bound proof.

    [label=\textbf{Fact \arabic*.}, leftmargin=*]
\item The absolute difference between the cumulative distribution functions \(F_1\) and \(F_0\) at a point \(\mathbf{x}\) satisfies

\bigl|F_1(\mathbf{x})-F_0(\mathbf{x})\bigr|
&= c n^{-1/2}\prod_{j=1}^d 
   \left|{\int_{-\infty}^{x_j} H\!\left(\frac{u-2}{2}\right)\mathrm{d}u}\right| \nonumber\\
&= c n^{-1/2} 2^d \prod_{j=1}^d
    \left|{\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{x_j-2}{2}} H(z)\,\mathrm{d}z}\right| \nonumber\\
&= c n^{-1/2} 2^d
   \prod_{j=1}^d
   \left|{H^{(-1)}\!\left(\frac{x_j-2}{2}\right)}\right|,
\quad \mathbf{x}\in\mathbb R^d,\label{eq:diffcdfs}

where we used the change of variable \(z = (u-2)/2\) in each coordinate.\\

\item For the change of variable \(z = (x_j - 2)/2\), we have
\[
\int_{0}^{2} \left|{H^{(-1)}\!\left(\frac{x_j - 2}{2}\right)}\right|\,\mathrm{d}x_j 
= 2 \int_{-1}^{0} |H^{(-1)}(z)|\,\mathrm{d}z
= 2 \int_{0}^{1} |H^{(-1)}(z)|\,\mathrm{d}z > 0,
\]
where the last equality follows from the oddness of \(H^{(-1)}\), which is a consequence of condition \ref{B1} and the evenness of \(H\). The strict positivity is guaranteed by condition \ref{B2}.

Let \(\hat{F}_n\) be any estimator of the cumulative distribution function based on an i.i.d. sample \(\mathbf{Y}_1,\,\dots,\,\mathbf{Y}_n\) drawn from either \(f_1 \ast f_{\varepsilon}\) or \(f_0 \ast f_{\varepsilon}\) (where \(f_{\varepsilon}\) is the noise density). We have already shown that $
\chi^2(f_1 \ast f_{\varepsilon} \,\|\, f_0 \ast f_{\varepsilon}) = O(n^{-1})$,
with a constant that can be made arbitrarily small by choosing \(c>0\) sufficiently small. 
Since the constant in the \(\chi^2\)-divergence bound is proportional to \(c^2\), choosing \(c>0\) sufficiently small ensures that $
\chi^2(f_1 \ast f_\varepsilon \parallel f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon) \le {\eta}{n}^{-1}$
for any prescribed \(\eta > 0\). Then, by tensorization of the \(\chi^2\)-divergence and Pinsker's inequality,
\[
\| (\mu_1 \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n} - (\mu_0 \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n} \|_{\mathrm{TV}}
\le \sqrt{\frac12 \left[ \left(1 + \frac{\eta}{n}\right)^n - 1 \right]}
\to \sqrt{\frac12 (e^\eta - 1)}.
\]
Since \(\eta > 0\) can be chosen arbitrarily small, the total variation distance can be made arbitrarily close to \(0\), uniformly in \(n\), by taking \(c>0\) sufficiently small. Hence, Le Cam's two-point method applies.
Hence, for \(n\) large enough,
\[
\int_{(\mathbb R^d)^n} \min\bigl\{(f_1 \ast f_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n},\,(f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}\bigr\}(\mathbf{y}_1,\,\ldots,\,\mathbf{y}_n)\,
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_1\,\ldots\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_n \ge \frac12.
\]
Therefore, by Le Cam's two-point method, the minimax risk is bounded below by

        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
&\inf_{\hat{F}_n}\sup_{f_X\in\mathcal{N}_1^d(\boldsymbol{\alpha},L)} 
\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\otimes\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\hat{F}_n-F_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}]\\
&\hspace*{1.5cm}\ge \frac12 \int_{\mathbb R^d} |F_1(\mathbf{x})-F_0(\mathbf{x})| \, \mathrm{d}\mathbf{x} \\
&\hspace{3.5cm} \times \int_{(\mathbb R^d)^n} \min\{(f_1 \ast f_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n},\,(f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}\}(\mathbf{y}_1,\,\ldots,\,\mathbf{y}_n)\,
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_1\,\ldots\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_n \\
&\hspace*{1.5cm}\ge \frac14 \int_{\mathbb R^d} |F_1(\mathbf{x})-F_0(\mathbf{x})| \, \mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

&= c n^{-1/2} 2^d
   \prod_{j=1}^d \int_{\mathbb R}
   \left|{H^{(-1)}\!\left(\frac{x_j-2}{2}\right)}\right| \mathrm{d}x_j \\
&\geq c n^{-1/2} 2^d
   \prod_{j=1}^d
   \left( 2 \int_{0}^{1} |H^{(-1)}(z)|\,\mathrm{d}z \right) \\
&= c n^{-1/2} 2^{2d}
   \left( \int_{0}^{1} |H^{(-1)}(z)|\,\mathrm{d}z \right)^d\gtrsim n^{-1/2}

because \(\int_0^1 |H^{(-1)}(z)|\,\mathrm{d}z>0\) by condition \ref{B2}, and \(c>0\) is fixed.
Combining this with the lower bound on the testing integral, we have that
\[
\inf_{\hat{F}_n} \sup_{f_X \in \mathcal{N}^d_1(\boldsymbol{\alpha},L)} 
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\hat{F}_n - F_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}] 
\gtrsim n^{-1/2}.
\]
Since when \(\mathcal P = \emptyset\) we have \(\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}} = n^{-1/2}\), this yields
\[
\inf_{\hat{F}_n} \sup_{f_X \in \mathcal{N}^d_1(\boldsymbol{\alpha},L)} 
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\hat{F}_n - F_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}] 
\gtrsim \psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}},
\]
which completes the proof for the case \(\mathcal P = \emptyset\).\\[5pt]
