        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
Let $\omega(x) \coloneqq \psi(x)/\int_{-1}^1 \psi(s)\,\mathrm{d}s$, $x\in\mathbb R$. Properties \ref{it:W1}--\ref
{it:W3} are readily verified. To verify \ref{it:W4}, for $|x| \le 3/4$,
\[
\omega(x) = \frac{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{1-x^2}\right)}{\int_{-1}^1 \psi(s)\,\mathrm{d}s} \ge \frac{\mathrm{e}^{-16/7}}{\int_{-1}^1 \psi(s)\,\mathrm{d}s} \eqqcolon c_\omega > 0.
\]
Thus, we may take $\gamma_\omega = 3/4$.

\smallskip
\noindent
Let $\xi \in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R)$ be any function satisfying the following conditions:\\[-8pt]
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        {\begin{enumerate}} 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    [label=(\bf{U\arabic*}), align=left, leftmargin=*, itemsep=0pt]
    \item \label{it:U1} $\operatorname{supp}(\xi) \subset [-\gamma_\xi,\, \gamma_\xi] \subset (-\gamma_\omega,\, \gamma_\omega)$, with $0 < \gamma_\xi < \gamma_\omega < 1$;\\[-8pt]
    \item \label{it:U2} $\xi(x) \ge 0$ for $x \in [1/2,\, 1/2+\eta]$, for some $\eta > 0$ satisfying $1/2+\eta < \gamma_\xi$;\\[-8pt]
    \item \label{it:U3} $\int_{\mathbb R} \xi(x) \,\mathrm{d}x = 0$;\\[-8pt]
    \item \label{it:U4} $\int_{\mathbb R} x^k \xi(x) \,\mathrm{d}x = 0$ for $k = 1,\, \dots,\, K$, for some integer $K \geq0$;\\[-8pt]
    \item \label{it:U5} $\int_{-\infty}^x \xi(s)\,\mathrm{d}s \ge c_\Xi > 0$ for all $x \in [1/2,\, 1/2+\eta]$, for some constant $c_\Xi > 0$.\\[-8pt]
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        {\end{enumerate}} 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
We show how such a function $\xi$ can be constructed. Choose $\eta > 0$ such that $1/2 + \eta < 3/4$.
Let $\phi_1,\, \phi_2,\,\phi_3 \in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R)$ be non-negative bump functions satisfying
\[
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\begin{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\operatorname{supp}(\phi_1) &\subset [1/4,\, 1/2 - \epsilon], & \|\phi_1\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} &= 1, \\
\operatorname{supp}(\phi_2) &\subset [1/2,\, 1/2 + \eta], & \|\phi_2\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} &= 1, \quad \phi_2 \ge 0, \\
\operatorname{supp}(\phi_3) &\subset [1/2+\eta,\, 3/4], &  \|\phi_3\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} &= 1,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\end{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
where $0 < \epsilon < 1/4$ is small. Define the preliminary function
$\xi_0(x) \coloneqq a\phi_1(x) + b\phi_2(x) - (a+b)\phi_3(x)$,
where $a,\, b > 0$ are constants to be chosen later.
Let $K \geq0$. Choose distinct points $x_0,\,\dots,\, x_K \in [1/4,\,3/4] \setminus [1/2,\, 1/2+\eta]$ and bump functions $\zeta_k \in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R)$ with sufficiently small disjoint supports centered at $x_k$, contained in $[1/4,\,3/4] \setminus [1/2,\, 1/2+\eta]$, such that $\int_{\mathbb R} \zeta_k(x)\,\mathrm{d}x = 1$ and the matrix $
V_{j,k} \coloneqq \int_{\mathbb R} x^j \zeta_k(x)\,\mathrm{d}x$, $j,\,k = 0,\,\dots,\,K$,
is invertible (this holds, for instance, if the supports are sufficiently small and the points $x_k$ are distinct, since $V$ is then close to a Vandermonde matrix).
Define $
\xi(x) = \xi_0(x) + \sum_{k=0}^K \theta_k \zeta_k(x)$,
where the constants $\theta_k$ are chosen to satisfy the moment conditions
$
\int_{\mathbb R} x^j \xi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x = 0$, $j = 0,\, \dots,\, K$.
Since the matrix $V$ is invertible, this linear system has a unique solution $(\theta_0,\,\dots,\,\theta_K)$.
It remains to choose $a,\,b > 0$. Observe that the solution $\theta_k$ is linear in $a$ and $b$, \emph{i.e.}, $\theta_k = a \theta_k^{(1)} + b \theta_k^{(2)}$ for constants $\theta_k^{(1)},\, \theta_k^{(2)}$ independent of $a,\,b$. Since the supports of $\zeta_k$ are contained in $[1/4,\,3/4] \setminus [1/2,\, 1/2+\eta]$, the correction terms do not affect $\xi$ on $[1/2,\, 1/2+\eta]$. Moreover, for $x \in [1/2,\, 1/2+\eta]$, we have
\[
\Xi(x) = \int_{-\infty}^x \xi(s)\,\mathrm{d}s = a + b\int_{1/2}^x \phi_2(s)\,\mathrm{d}s + \sum_{k: x_k < x} \theta_k \int_{-\infty}^x \zeta_k(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.
\]
Choose $b > 0$ first, then choose $a > 0$ sufficiently large (\emph{e.g.}, $a = b^3$) so that the positive contribution $a$ dominates any negative contributions from the correction terms. This is possible because the coefficients $\theta_k^{(1)}$ and $\theta_k^{(2)}$ are fixed, and the supports of $\zeta_k$ can be chosen arbitrarily small; we may first choose the supports so small that the total variation of each correction term is less than, say, $1/(K+1)$. Then, choosing $a = b^3$ ensures that $a$ dominates the correction terms. This guarantees that $\Xi(x) \ge c_\Xi > 0$ for all $x \in [1/2,\, 1/2+\eta]$. Also, since $\xi(x) = b\phi_2(x) \ge 0$ on $[1/2,\, 1/2+\eta]$, positivity is automatic.
Finally, taking $\gamma_\xi \in (1/2+\eta,\, 3/4)$, we have $\operatorname{supp}(\xi) \subset [-\gamma_\xi,\, \gamma_\xi] \subset (-\gamma_\omega,\, \gamma_\omega)$ since $\gamma_\omega = 3/4$. Thus, all conditions \ref{it:U1}--\ref{it:U5} are satisfied.

\smallskip

Define the cumulative distribution functions
\[
\Omega(x) \coloneqq \int_{-\infty}^x \omega(s)\,\mathrm{d}s \quad\text{and}\quad \Xi(x) \coloneqq \int_{-\infty}^x \xi(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.
\]
The function $\Omega$ is continuous and strictly increasing on $(-1,\,1)$, and there exist constants $c_\Omega,\,c_\Xi > 0$ such that 
\[
\Omega(x) \ge c_\Omega > 0 \quad\text{and}\quad \Xi(x) \ge c_\Xi > 0 \quad\text{for all } x \in [1/2,\, 1/2+\eta].
\]

                                                      

\item \emph{Construction of the density with inactive coordinates $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}$.}\\[2pt]
Choose $\gamma_M < 1/2$ and let $\rho \in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R)$ be a bump function such that $\rho \ge 0$, $\operatorname{supp}(\rho) \subset [-\gamma_M/8, \,\gamma_M/8]$, and $\int_{\mathbb R} \rho(x)\,\mathrm{d}x = 1$.
For $\tau > 0$, define $\rho_\tau(x) \coloneqq \tau^{-1}\rho(x/\tau)$. We have $\operatorname{supp}(\rho_\tau) \subset [-\tau, \,\tau]$.
For each coordinate $j = 1,\, \dots,\, d-|\mathcal P|$, define
\[
\rho_j(x)\coloneqq  \left(\mathbf{1}_{\left[-\frac{\gamma_M}{2}M + \tau,\,\frac{\gamma_M}{2}M - \tau\right]} \ast \rho_\tau\right)(x),\quad x\in\mathbb R,
\]
with $\tau < (\gamma_M/8)M$. 
Since $\operatorname{supp}(\rho_\tau) \subset [-\tau, \,\tau]$, the integral
\[
\rho_j(x) = \int_{x - \frac{\gamma_M}{2}M + \tau}^{x + \frac{\gamma_M}{2}M - \tau} \rho_\tau(u) \, \mathrm d u
\]
is nonzero only if the interval of integration intersects $[-\tau,\, \tau]$. This occurs if and only if
$x - ({\gamma_M}/{2})M + \tau \le \tau$ and $ x + ({\gamma_M}/{2})M - \tau \ge -\tau$,
which gives $|x| \le ({\gamma_M}/{2})M$.
Hence, $
\operatorname{supp}(\rho_j) \subset [-({\gamma_M}/{2})M, \, ({\gamma_M}/{2})M]$.
Let
\[
f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}(\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P}) \coloneqq \frac{1}{(\gamma_M M - 2\tau)^{d-|\mathcal P|}} \prod_{j=1}^{d-|\mathcal P|} \rho_j(x_j).
\]
It is easy to verify that $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}} \in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^{d-|\mathcal P|})$ and 
$
\int_{\mathbb R^{d-|\mathcal P|}} f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}(\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P} = 1$. Also, 
$\operatorname{supp}(f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}) \subset [-({\gamma_M}/{2})M,\, ({\gamma_M}/{2})M]^{d-|\mathcal P|}$. 
Besides, for $x \in [-({\gamma_M}/{4})M, \,({\gamma_M}/{4})M]$, we have
\[
\rho_j(x) = \int_{x - \frac{\gamma_M}{2}M + \tau}^{x + \frac{\gamma_M}{2}M - \tau} \rho_\tau(u) \, \mathrm d u=1.
\]
Consequently,
\[ 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        {\begin{split}} 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}(\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P}) &= \frac{1}{(\gamma_M M - 2\tau)^{d-|\mathcal P|}} \prod_{j=1}^{d-|\mathcal P|} \rho_j(x_j)\\
&= \frac{1}{(\gamma_M M - 2\tau)^{d-|\mathcal P|}}\eqqcolon c_0>0, \quad \mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P} \in\Bigl[-\frac{\gamma_M}{4} M,\, \frac{\gamma_M}{4} M\Bigr]^{d-|\mathcal P}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        {\end{split}} 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
Moreover, $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}} \in \mathcal N_1^{d-|\mathcal P|}(\boldsymbol\alpha_{\setminus\mathcal P},\, L_0)$ for some $L_0 > 0$. This follows from the following Nikol'skii-type estimate: for any $f \in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R)$, any integer $q \ge 0$, and any $r > q$ such that $0 < r - q \le 1$, there exists $C > 0$ such that
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:holderinity}
\int_{\mathbb R} |f^{(q)}(u+h) - f^{(q)}(u)|\,\mathrm{d}u \le C |h|^{r - q}
\quad \forall\,h \in \mathbb R.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Indeed, let $D := \operatorname{supp}(f)$. If $|h| \le 1$, the Mean Value Theorem gives
\[
|f^{(q)}(u+h) - f^{(q)}(u)| \le \|f^{(q+1)}\|_{\infty} |h|,
\]
and the difference is supported in $D \cup (D-h)$, whose measure is bounded by a constant depending only on $f$, yielding
\[
\int_{\mathbb R} |f^{(q)}(u+h) - f^{(q)}(u)|\,\mathrm{d}u \le C_f \|f^{(q+1)}\|_{\infty} |h| \le C_f \|f^{(q+1)}\|_{\infty} |h|^{r-q},
\]
since $|h| \le 1$ and $r-q \le 1$.
If $|h| > 1$, then
\[
\int_{\mathbb R} |f^{(q)}(u+h) - f^{(q)}(u)|\,\mathrm{d}u \le 2\|f^{(q)}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} \le 2\|f^{(q)}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} |h|^{r-q},
\]
since $|h|^{r-q} \ge 1$.
Combining the two cases yields the desired estimate. Applying this estimate to each $\rho_j$ with $q_j = \lceil \alpha_j \rceil - 1$ and using the product structure of $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}$ gives that $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}\in \mathcal N_1^{d-|\mathcal P
|}(\boldsymbol\alpha_{\setminus\mathcal P},\, L_0)$.\\


\item \emph{Definition of the test densities $f_0$ and $f_1$.}\\[2pt]
For each $j \in \mathcal P$, let the scaled functions
\[
\omega_j(x) \coloneqq R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)} \omega(R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}x)
\quad \text{and} \quad
\xi_j(x) \coloneqq R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)} \xi(R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}x),\quad x\in\mathbb R,
\]
and their cumulative distribution functions
\[
\Omega_j(x) \coloneqq \int_{-\infty}^x \omega_j(u)\,\mathrm{d}u = \Omega(R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}x)
\,\,\, \text{ and } \,\,\,
\Xi_j(x) \coloneqq \int_{-\infty}^x \xi_j(u)\,\mathrm{d}u = \Xi(R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}x),\quad x\in\mathbb R.
\]
We can now define the test densities. For every $\mathbf x\in\mathbb R^d$,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:testdensities}
f_0(\mathbf x) \coloneqq \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} \omega_j(x_j)  f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}(\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P})
\,\, \text{ and } \,\,
f_1(\mathbf x) \coloneqq \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} [\omega_j(x_j) + \lambda_n \xi_j(x_j)]  f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}(\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P}),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
for some non-negative sequence $(\lambda_n)_{n\in\mathbb N}$ to be chosen later.
The product structure of the densities is preserved when passing to cumulative distribution functions because integration factorizes over the coordinates. Consequently, for every $\mathbf x\in\mathbb R^d$, the cumulative distribution functions are given by
\[
F_0(\mathbf x) \coloneqq \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} \Omega_j(x_j)  F_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}(\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P})
\quad \text{and} \quad
F_1(\mathbf x) \coloneqq \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} [\Omega_j(x_j) + \lambda_n \Xi_j(x_j)]  F_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}(\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P}),
\]
where $F_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}$ is the cumulative distribution function of $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}$.\\


\item \emph{Verification that $f_0$ and $f_1$ are probability densities.}\\[2pt]
We now verify that, for sufficiently large $n$, the functions $f_0$ and $f_1$ are probability densities.
Regarding $f_0$, since $f_0$ is a product of non-negative functions, we have $f_0 \ge 0$. Moreover, by Fubini's theorem,
\[
\int_{\mathbb R^d} f_0(\mathbf x)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf x 
= \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} \left(\int_{\mathbb R} \omega_j (x_j)\,\mathrm{d}x_j\right) 
\left(\int_{\mathbb R^{d-|\mathcal P|}} f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}} (\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P}\right) = 1,
\]
because, by construction, $\int_{\mathbb R} \omega_j (x_j)\,\mathrm{d}x_j = 1$ for each $j \in \mathcal P$, and $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}$ is a density, hence it integrates to one. Therefore, $f_0$ is a probability density.

For $f_1$, we first note that, since $\int_{\mathbb R} \xi_j(x_j)\,\mathrm{d}x_j = 0$ by construction,
\[
\int_{\mathbb R} [\omega_j(x_j) + \lambda_n \xi_j(x_j)]\,\mathrm{d}x_j
= \int_{\mathbb R} \omega_j(x_j)\,\mathrm{d}x_j + \lambda_n \int_{\mathbb R} \xi_j(x_j)\,\mathrm{d}x_j
= 1.
\]
Therefore, again by Fubini's theorem,
\[
\int_{\mathbb R^d} f_1(\mathbf x)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf x
= \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} \left(\int_{\mathbb R} [\omega_j(x_j) + \lambda_n \xi_j(x_j)]\,\mathrm{d}x_j\right)
\left(\int_{\mathbb R^{d-|\mathcal P|}} f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}} (\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P}\right)
= 1.
\]
It remains to verify that $f_1 \ge 0$. Since $\operatorname{supp}(\xi) \subset [-\gamma_\xi,\, \gamma_\xi] \subset (-\gamma_\omega,\, \gamma_\omega)$ and $\omega \ge c_\omega > 0$ on $[-\gamma_\omega,\, \gamma_\omega]$, it follows that $\omega_j(x) + \lambda_n \xi_j(x) \ge R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}(c_\omega - \lambda_n \|\xi\|_\infty)$ for all $x \in \operatorname{supp}(\xi_j)$.
Because $\lambda_n \to 0$ as $n \to \infty$, for sufficiently large $n$, we have $\lambda_n \|\xi\|_\infty \le c_\omega/2$. Hence, $\omega_j(x) + \lambda_n \xi_j(x) \ge R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}c_\omega/2 > 0$ on $\operatorname{supp}(\xi_j)$, while outside $\operatorname{supp}(\xi_j)$ we have $\omega_j(x) + \lambda_n \xi_j(x) = \omega_j(x) \ge 0$. Therefore, for each $j \in \mathcal P$, we have $\omega_j(x_j) + \lambda_n \xi_j(x_j) \ge 0$ for all $x_j \in \mathbb R$. Thus, $f_1(\mathbf x) \ge 0$ for all $\mathbf x \in \mathbb R^d$, since it is a product of non-negative factors. Consequently, $f_1$ is also a probability density for sufficiently large $n$.\\


\item \emph{Verification that $f_0, \,f_1 \in \mathcal N_1^d(\boldsymbol\alpha, L)$.}\\[2pt]
We now show that, for sufficiently large $n$, both densities $f_0,\, f_1$ belong to $\mathcal N_1^d(\bm\alpha,\, L)$ for some constant $L > 0$ independent of $n$. We need to verify conditions \ref{it:diff}, \ref{it:L1}, and \ref{it:smooth} from Definition \ref{def:anisotropy} \hyperref[def:anisotropy]{of the Main Article} for both $f_0$ and $f_1$.

We begin by verifying condition \ref{it:diff} \hyperref[it:diff]{of the Main Article}. Since $\omega,\,\xi \in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R)$, the scaled functions $\omega_j$ and $\xi_j$ are also $C^\infty(\mathbb R)$, so $ (\omega_j + \lambda_n \xi_j) \in C^\infty(\mathbb R)$ for each $j \in \mathcal P$. By assumption, $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}\in \mathcal N_1^{d-|\mathcal P|}(\bm\alpha_{\setminus\mathcal P},\, L_0)$, so it admits partial derivatives up to order $m_j \coloneqq \lceil \alpha_j \rceil - 1$ in each inactive coordinate. Since the product structure separates active and inactive coordinates, $f_0$ and $f_1$ admit all partial derivatives up to order $m_j$ in each coordinate direction. Thus, condition \ref{it:diff} \hyperref[it:diff]{of the Main Article} holds.

We now consider the verification of \ref{it:L1} \hyperref[it:L1]{of the Main Article}. For each $j \in \mathcal P$ and each $k = 0,\,\dots,\, m_j$, we consider the partial derivative
\[
\partial_j^k f_1(\mathbf x) = g_j^{(k)}(x_j) \prod_{\ell \in \mathcal P \setminus \{j\}} g_\ell(x_\ell) f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}(\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P}),\quad\mathbf x\in\mathbb R^d, 
\]
where $g_j^{(k)}$ denotes the $k$-th derivative of $g_j\coloneqq \omega_j+\lambda_n\xi_j$.
Now, we need a uniform bound on $\|\partial_j^k f_1\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}$. By Fubini's theorem, and since $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}$ is a density, for sufficiently large $n$ such that $\lambda_n \le 1$, we have
\[ 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\begin{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\|\partial_j^k f_1\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}
&= \left(\int_{\mathbb R} |g_j^{(k)}(x_j)|\,\mathrm{d}x_j\right)
\prod_{\ell \in \mathcal P \setminus \{j\}} \left(\int_{\mathbb R} |g_\ell(x_\ell)|\,\mathrm{d}x_\ell\right)
\left(\int_{\mathbb R^{d-|\mathcal P|}} |f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}(\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P})|\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P}\right)\\
&\leq \left(R_n^{-k(\alpha_j+1)} 
\int_{\mathbb R} |\omega^{(k)}(u) + \lambda_n \xi^{(k)}(u)|\,\mathrm{d}u\right)(1 + \lambda_n \|\xi\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)})^{|\mathcal P|-1}\\
&\leq R_n^{-k(\alpha_j+1)}\bigl(\|\omega^{(k)}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} + \|\xi^{(k)}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\bigr)\bigl(1 + \|\xi\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\bigr)^{|\mathcal P|-1}\\
&\leq \bigl(\|\omega^{(k)}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} + \|\xi^{(k)}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\bigr)\bigl(1 + \|\xi\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\bigr)^{|\mathcal P|-1}<\infty,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\end{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
where in the last inequality we used the fact that $R_n\geq1$, so $R_n^{-k(\alpha_j+1)}\leq 1$.
Thus, the bound is independent of $n$. A similar reasoning leads to an analogous bound for $f_0$ with $\lambda_n=0$. For inactive coordinates $j \notin \mathcal P$, since $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}} \in \mathcal N_1^{d-|\mathcal P|}(\bm\alpha_{\setminus\mathcal P},\, L_0)$, we already have $
\|\partial_j^k f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^{d-|\mathcal P|})} \le L_0$,
and since the active factors integrate to a constant independent of $n$, the same uniform bound holds for the full density.
Thus, condition \ref{it:L1} \hyperref[it:L1]{of the Main Article} holds with some constant $L$ independent of $n$. 

We now verify condition \ref{it:smooth} \hyperref[it:smooth]{of the Main Article}. For each $j \in \mathcal P$ and each $\delta \in \mathbb R$, we need to bound
$\|\Delta_{\delta\mathbf{e}_j}\partial_j^{m_j} f_r\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}$ for $r=0,\,1$. We prove the condition for $f_1$; the proof for $f_0$ is analogous (with $\lambda_n = 0$). Reasoning as before, we get 
\[

\|\Delta_{\delta\mathbf{e}_j}\partial_j^{m_j} f_1\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}
&= \left(\int_{\mathbb R} \big|g_j^{(m_j)}(x + \delta) - g_j^{(m_j)}(x)\big|\,\mathrm{d}x\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\times 
\prod_{\ell \in \mathcal P \setminus \{j\}} \left(\int_{\mathbb R} |g_\ell(x_\ell)|\,\mathrm{d}x_\ell\right)
\left(\int_{\mathbb R^{d-|\mathcal P|}} |f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}|\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P}\right)\\
&\leq \left(\int_{\mathbb R} \big|g_j^{(m_j)}(x + \delta) - g_j^{(m_j)}(x)\big|\,\mathrm{d}x\right)\bigl(1 + \|\xi\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\bigr)^{|\mathcal P|-1}.

        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
It remains to bound the first factor. We have
\[

\int_{\mathbb R}\big|g_j^{(m_j)}(x+\delta) - g_j^{(m_j)}(x)\big|\,\mathrm{d}x &= R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)(m_j+1)}\\&
 \qquad \times \int_{\mathbb R} \Big|
\omega^{(m_j)}(R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}(x+\delta)) - \omega^{(m_j)}(R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}x) \\
&\qquad\quad + \lambda_n \bigl[ \xi^{(m_j)}(R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}(x+\delta)) - \xi^{(m_j)}(R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}x) \bigr] \Big|\,\mathrm{d}x \\
&\le R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)(m_j+1)}\\
&\qquad\times  \left[
\int_{\mathbb R} \big|\omega^{(m_j)}(R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}(x+\delta)) - \omega^{(m_j)}(R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}x)\big |\,\mathrm{d}x \right. \\
&\qquad\quad \left. + \lambda_n \int_{\mathbb R} \big|\xi^{(m_j)}(R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}(x+\delta)) - \xi^{(m_j)}(R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}x)\big|\,\mathrm{d}x
\right]\\
&=R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)m_j} \left[
\int_{\mathbb R} \big|\omega^{(m_j)}(u + R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}\delta) - \omega^{(m_j)}(u)\big|\,\mathrm{d}u \right. \\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad \left. + \lambda_n \int_{\mathbb R}\big|\xi^{(m_j)}(u + R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}\delta) - \xi^{(m_j)}(u)\big|\,\mathrm{d}u
\right],
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
where we used the change of variable $u = R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}x$.
Since $\omega,\, \xi \in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R)$, by the Nikol'skii property \eqref{eq:holderinity} there exist constants $C_\omega,\, C_\xi > 0$ such that, for any $\eta \in \mathbb R$,
\[
\int_{\mathbb R} |\omega^{(m_j)}(u+\eta) - \omega^{(m_j)}(u)|\,\mathrm{d}u \le C_\omega |\eta|^{\alpha_j - m_j}\]
and 
\[
\int_{\mathbb R} |\xi^{(m_j)}(u+\eta) - \xi^{(m_j)}(u)|\,\mathrm{d}u \le C_\xi |\eta|^{\alpha_j - m_j}.
\]
Applying these estimates with $\eta \coloneqq R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}\delta$, we obtain
\[

        {

        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \int_{\mathbb R}\big|g_j^{(m_j)}(x+\delta) - g_j^{(m_j)}(x)\big|\,\mathrm{d}x
&\le R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)m_j} \\&\qquad\times\bigl[
C_\omega (R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}|\delta|)^{\alpha_j - m_j}
+ \lambda_n C_\xi (R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}|\delta|)^{\alpha_j - m_j}
\bigr] \\
&= (C_\omega + \lambda_n C_\xi) R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)\alpha_j} |\delta|^{\alpha_j - m_j}\\
& \le (C_\omega + C_\xi) |\delta|^{\alpha_j - m_j},
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {

        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
since $R_n \ge 1$ and $\lambda_n \le 1$ for sufficiently large $n$.
Combining this with the bound for the product of the other factors yields
\[
\|\Delta_{\delta\mathbf{e}_j}\partial_j^{m_j} f_1\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}
\le (C_\omega + C_\xi) (1 + \|\xi\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)})^{|\mathcal P|-1} |\delta|^{\alpha_j - m_j}.
\]
Thus, condition \ref{it:smooth} \hyperref[it:smooth]{of the Main Article} holds for $f_1$ with constant $
L \coloneqq (C_\omega + C_\xi) (1 + \|\xi\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)})^{|\mathcal P|-1}$
which is independent of $n$. For inactive coordinates $j \notin \mathcal P$, the bound follows directly from $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}} \in \mathcal N_1^{d-|\mathcal P|}(\bm\alpha_{\setminus\mathcal P},\, L_0)$, with the active factors contributing a constant independent of $n$.
Thus, condition \ref{it:smooth} \hyperref[it:smooth]{of the Main Article} holds with some constant $L$ independent of $n$.

We have verified that both $f_0$ and $f_1$ satisfy conditions \ref{it:diff}, \ref{it:L1}, and \ref{it:smooth} \hyperref[it:diff]{of the Main Article} with constants that are independent of $n$. Therefore, $f_0,\,f_1 \in \mathcal N_1^d(\bm\alpha,\, L)$ for some $L > 0$ independent of $n$.\\

                                                         

\item \emph{Separation rate of the cumulative distribution functions.}\\[2pt]
We now derive the separation rate of the cumulative distribution functions.  
Let $F_0$ and $F_1$ be the cumulative distribution functions corresponding to $f_0$ and $f_1$.  
We show that there exists a constant $c_1 > 0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $n$,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:separate}
\|F_1 - F_0\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} \ge c_1 n^{-E/2}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Using Lemma \ref{lem:foexp}, we obtain
\[
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\begin{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
F_1(\mathbf{x}) - F_0(\mathbf{x}) 
&= \int_{(-\infty, x_1] \times \cdots \times (-\infty, x_d]} [f_1(\mathbf{u}) - f_0(\mathbf{u})] \, \mathrm{d}\mathbf{u} \\
&=\Bigg[\prod_{j\in\mathcal P} [\Omega_j(x_j) + \lambda_n \Xi_j(x_j)] - \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} \Omega_j(x_j)\Bigg] F_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}(\mathbf{x}_{\setminus\mathcal P})\\
&= \lambda_n \underbrace{\Bigg[\sum_{j\in\mathcal P} \Xi_j(x_j) \prod_{\substack{\ell\in\mathcal P \\ \ell \ne j}} \Omega_\ell(x_\ell
)\Bigg] F_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}(\mathbf{x}_{\setminus\mathcal P})}_{\eqqcolon S(\mathbf{x})} + O(\lambda_n^2),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\end{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
where $F_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}$ is the cumulative distribution function of $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}$, 
\[
\Omega_j(x) \coloneqq \int_{-\infty}^x \omega_j(u)\,\mathrm{d}u = \Omega(R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}x)
\,\,\,\text{ and } \,\,\,
\Xi_j(x) \coloneqq \int_{-\infty}^x \xi_j(u)\,\mathrm{d}u = \Xi(R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}x),\quad x\in\mathbb R,
\]
with $\Omega(t) \coloneqq \int_{-\infty}^t \omega(s)\,\mathrm{d}s$ and $\Xi(t) \coloneqq \int_{-\infty}^t \xi(s)\,\mathrm{d}s$. Choose $\eta > 0$ such that $1/2 + \eta < 1$. Define the region
\[
\mathcal S_n \coloneqq \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} \left[\frac12 R_n^{\alpha_j+1},\, \left(\frac12+\eta\right) R_n^{\alpha_j+1}\right] \times \left[-\frac M2,\, \frac M2\right]^{d-|\mathcal P|}.
\]
Since $\Omega$ is continuous and strictly positive on $(-1,\,1)$, and the interval $[1/2,\,1/2 + \eta]$ is a compact subset of $(-1,\,1)$, there exists a constant $c_\Omega > 0$ such that $
\Omega(x) \ge c_\Omega$ for all  $x \in [1/2,\,1/2 + \eta]$.
Therefore, for $x_j \in [R_n^{\alpha_j+1}/2,\,(1/2+\eta) R_n^{\alpha_j+1}]$, we have
$
(R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}x_j) \in [1/2,\,1/2 + \eta],
$
so $
\Omega_j(x_j) = \Omega(R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}x_j) \ge c_\Omega > 0$.
Similarly, since $\Xi$ is continuous and $\int_{-\infty}^1 \xi(u)\,\mathrm{d}u > 0$ by assumption, there exists a constant $c_\Xi> 0$ such that $\Xi(x) \ge c_\Xi$ for all $x \in [1/2,\,1/2 + \eta]$. Therefore, for $x_j$ in the same interval, $
\Xi_j(x_j) = \Xi(R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}x_j) \ge c_\Xi > 0$.
Also, for $\mathbf{x}_{\setminus\mathcal P} \in [-M/2,\, M/2]^{d-|\mathcal P|}$, we have 
$F_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}(\mathbf{x}_{\setminus\mathcal P}) \ge \kappa_0 > 0$ for some constant 
$\kappa_0 > 0$. Indeed, since $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}$ is continuous and strictly positive on the 
compact set $[-M/2,\, M/2]^{d-|\mathcal P|}$, its cumulative distribution function 
$F_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}$ is also continuous and strictly positive there; hence, it attains a positive minimum $\kappa_0 \coloneqq \min_{\mathbf{x} \in [-M/2,\, M/2]^{d-|\mathcal P|}} F_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}(\mathbf{x}) > 0$ on this compact set. Thus, for $\mathbf{x} \in \mathcal S_n$, each term in the sum is nonnegative. 
Using the reverse triangle inequality, $|A+B| \ge |A| - |B|$, with 
$A = \lambda_n S(\mathbf{x}) \ge 0$ and $B = O(\lambda_n^2)$, we obtain
\[
|F_1(\mathbf{x}) - F_0(\mathbf{x})| 
= |\lambda_n S(\mathbf{x}) + O(\lambda_n^2)| \ge \lambda_n S(\mathbf{x}) - |O(\lambda_n^2)|.
\]
Since $|O(\lambda_n^2)| \le C\lambda_n^2$ for some constant $C > 0$, it follows that
$|F_1(\mathbf{x}) - F_0(\mathbf{x})| \ge \lambda_n S(\mathbf{x}) - C\lambda_n^2$.
On the region $\mathcal S_n$, we have $S(\mathbf{x}) \ge |\mathcal P| c_\Xi c_\Omega^{|\mathcal P|-1} \kappa_0$. Therefore, since $|\mathcal P| \ge 1$,
\[
|F_1(\mathbf{x}) - F_0(\mathbf{x})| \ge \lambda_n |\mathcal P| c_\Xi c_\Omega^{|\mathcal P|-1} \kappa_0 - C\lambda_n^2 \ge \lambda_n c_\Xi c_\Omega^{|\mathcal P|-1} \kappa_0 - C\lambda_n^2,
\]
where $C > 0$ is a constant. For sufficiently large $n$, $\lambda_n$ is small enough so that the linear term dominates, hence there exists a constant $c > 0$ such that $
|F_1(\mathbf{x}) - F_0(\mathbf{x})| \ge c \lambda_n$.
The volume of $\mathcal S_n$ equals
$
\prod_{j\in\mathcal P} (\eta R_n^{\alpha_j+1}) M^{d-|\mathcal P|} = \eta^{|\mathcal P|} M^{d-|\mathcal P|} \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{\alpha_j+1}$.
Therefore, since $\lambda_n\coloneqq n^{-E/2}\prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}$, we get
\[
\|F_1 - F_0\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} 
\ge \int_{\mathcal S_n} |F_1(\mathbf{x}) - F_0(\mathbf{x})|\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x} 
\ge c \lambda_n \eta^{|\mathcal P|} M^{d-|\mathcal P|} \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{\alpha_j+1} = c \eta^{|\mathcal P|}  M^{d-|\mathcal P|} n^{-E/2}.
\]
Since $\eta^{|\mathcal P|} M^{d-|\mathcal P|} > 0$ is a constant independent of $n$, inequality \eqref{eq:separate} follows. \\

                                                     
\item \emph{Bound on the $\chi^2$-divergence.}\\[2pt]
We now prove that $\chi^2(f_1 \ast f_\varepsilon \parallel f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)=o( n^{-1})$.
To this end we refine the construction of $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}$ 
to ensure a uniform lower bound on its support. The previous construction gave 
$f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}$ as a product of smoothed indicator functions, which vanish at the 
boundary of their support. To control the $\chi^2$-divergence, we need a stronger property: 
$f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}$ must be bounded below by a positive constant times a power of the 
distance to the boundary of its support. This is achieved by replacing the standard bump 
function $\rho$ with a refined bump function $\varrho$ having polynomial decay near the 
boundary.

Let $\zeta \in C_c^\infty(0,\,1)$ be defined by
\[
\zeta(s) \coloneqq
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\begin{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
[s(1-s)]^m, & s \in (0,\,1), \\
0, & \text{otherwise},
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\end{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
so that $\zeta > 0$ on $(0,\,1)$ and there exists $c_\zeta > 0$ such that
$
\zeta(s) \ge c_\zeta (1-s)^m$ for all $s \in [1/2,\, 1)$. 
Define
\[
T(y) \coloneqq \frac{\int_{y}^{1} \zeta(s)\,\mathrm{d}s}{\int_{0}^{1} \zeta(t)\,\mathrm{d}t}, \quad y \in [0,\,1].
\]
Then, $T \in C^\infty([0,\,1])$, $T(0)=1$, $T(1)=0$, all derivatives vanish at the endpoints, and there exists a constant $c_T > 0$ such that $
T(y) \ge c_T (1-y)^{m+1}$ for all $y \in [0,\,1]$.
Fix $0 < \delta < 1$ (for instance, $\delta = 1/2$). Define $\varrho_0 \in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R)$ as
\[
\varrho_0(x)\coloneqq
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\begin{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
1, & |x| \le \delta, \\[4pt]
T\!\left(\dfrac{|x| - \delta}{1 - \delta}\right), & \delta < |x| < 1, \\[4pt]
0, & |x| \ge 1.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\end{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
For $x \in [\delta,\, 1]$, we have
\[
\varrho_0(x) = T\!\left(\frac{x - \delta}{1 - \delta}\right)
\ge c_T \left(1 - \frac{x - \delta}{1 - \delta}\right)^{m+1}
= \frac{c_T}{(1-\delta)^{m+1}} (1-x)^{m+1}
\eqqcolon c_\varrho (1-x)^{m+1}.
\]
The same estimate holds symmetrically for $x \in [-1,\, -\delta]$. For $x \in [-\delta,\, \delta]$, the bound follows from the flat top.
Finally, since $\int_{-1}^{1} \varrho_0(x)\,\mathrm{d}x > 0$, we normalize to obtain 
\[
\varrho(x)\coloneqq \frac{\varrho_0(x)}{\int_{-1}^{1} \varrho_0(s)\,\mathrm{d}s}.
\]
Then, $\varrho \in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R)$, $\operatorname{supp}(\varrho) = [-1,\,1]$, $\int_{\mathbb R} \varrho(x)\,\mathrm{d}x = 1$, and there exists a constant $c_\varrho' > 0$ such that
\[
\varrho(x) \ge c_\varrho' \operatorname{dist}(x,\, \partial \operatorname{supp}(\varrho))^{m+1}
\quad \text{for all } x \in [-1,\,1],
\]
where $\partial \operatorname{supp}(\varrho)=\{-1,\,1\}$.
The polynomial decay of $\varrho$ transfers to the density $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}$. Indeed, $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}$ is constructed as a product of smoothed indicator functions, each built from $\varrho$ via scaling and convolution. By the scaling properties of the distance function and the product structure of $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}$, the decay estimate above implies the existence of a constant $c > 0$ such that
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:lowerboundonfinactive}
f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}(\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P})
\ge c \operatorname{dist}(\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P},\, \partial \operatorname{supp}(f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}))^{m+1},
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
where recall that $
\operatorname{supp}(f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}) = \left[-({\gamma_M}/{2})M,\, ({\gamma_M}/{2})M\right]^{d-|\mathcal P|}$.

We now introduce the regions that are used to localize the $\chi^2$-integral and to lower bound $f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon$.
Choose $\gamma$ such that $\gamma_\xi < \gamma < \gamma_\omega = 3/4$.
We define the full support region
\[
\mathcal K_n^{\mathrm{full}} \coloneqq 
\prod_{j\in\mathcal P} \big[-\gamma R_n^{\alpha_j+1},\, \gamma R_n^{\alpha_j+1}\big]
\times \left[-\frac{\gamma_M}{2}M,\, \frac{\gamma_M}{2}M\right]^{d-|\mathcal P|}.
\]
Since $\gamma_\xi < \gamma$, it follows from the support properties of $\xi$ and $\omega$ that $
\operatorname{supp}(f_1 - f_0) \subset \mathcal K_n^{\mathrm{full}}$.
We also define the region where $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}$ is constant as 
\[
\mathcal K_n \coloneqq 
\prod_{j\in\mathcal P} \big[-\gamma R_n^{\alpha_j+1},\, \gamma R_n^{\alpha_j+1}\big]
\times \left[-\frac{\gamma_M}{8}M,\, \frac{\gamma_M}{8}M\right]^{d-|\mathcal P|}.
\]
Define the boundary region
\[
\mathcal B_n \coloneqq 
\bigg(\prod_{j\in\mathcal P} \big[-\gamma R_n^{\alpha_j+1},\, \gamma R_n^{\alpha_j+1}\big]\bigg)
\times \bigg( \left[-\frac{\gamma_M}{2}M,\, \frac{\gamma_M}{2}M\right]^{d-|\mathcal P|}
\setminus \left[-\frac{\gamma_M}{8}M,\, \frac{\gamma_M}{8}M\right]^{d-|\mathcal P|} \bigg).
\]
We have the partition \(\mathcal K_n^{\mathrm{full}} = \mathcal K_n \cup \mathcal B_n\).
On $\mathcal K_n$, we have $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}(\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P}) = c_0 > 0$. 
For the active coordinates, since $\gamma < \gamma_\omega$ and $\omega(x) \ge c_\omega > 0$ for $|x| \le \gamma_\omega$, we have $
\omega_j(x_j) \ge c_\omega R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}$ for $|x_j| \le \gamma R_n^{\alpha_j+1}$.
Therefore, on $\mathcal K_n$,
\[
f_0(\mathbf x) = \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} \omega_j(x_j) f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}(\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P})
\ge c_\ast^{\mathrm{core}} \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)},
\]
where $c_\ast^{\mathrm{core}} \coloneqq c_0 \, c_\omega^{|\mathcal P|} > 0$.
On $\mathcal B_n$, the distance from $\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P}$ to the boundary of $\operatorname{supp}(f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}})$ is at least $(\gamma_M/4) M$ (since the core set extends to $(\gamma_M/8)M$ and the full support to $(\gamma_M/2)M$). Applying the decay estimate \eqref{eq:lowerboundonfinactive}, we obtain a positive lower bound for $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}$ on $\mathcal B_n$,
\[
f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}(\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P}) \ge c \left(\frac{\gamma_M}{4} M\right)^{m+1} \eqqcolon c_0'' > 0.
\]
Consequently, on $\mathcal B_n$, for $c_\ast^{\mathrm{bound}} \coloneqq c_0'' \, c_\omega^{|\mathcal P|} > 0,$
\[
f_0(\mathbf x) \ge c_\ast^{\mathrm{bound}} \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}.
\]
Defining $\underline c_\ast \coloneqq \min\{c_\ast^{\mathrm{core}}, \, c_\ast^{\mathrm{bound}}\} > 0$,
we obtain the uniform lower bound on $\mathcal K_n^{\mathrm{full}}$,

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:lb}
f_0(\mathbf x) \ge \underline c_\ast \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}
\quad \text{for all } \mathbf x \in \mathcal K_n^{\mathrm{full}}.

We now show that the lower bound \eqref{eq:lb} is preserved under convolution with the noise density $f_\varepsilon$, up to a multiplicative constant. Since $f_\varepsilon$ is a probability density, for any $0 < \varpi < 1$ there exists a compact set $K_\varpi \subset \mathbb R^d$ such that 
$\int_{K_\varpi} f_\varepsilon(\mathbf z) \, \mathrm{d}\mathbf z \ge 1 - \varpi$.
Let 
$
R_{K_\varpi}\coloneqq \max_{\mathbf z \in K_\varpi} \max_{1 \le j \le d} |z_j| < \infty.
$
We choose $K_\varpi$ sufficiently small so that

    \label{eq:boundonR_K}
R_{K_\varpi} \le \min\left\{(\gamma_\omega - \gamma) R_n^{\alpha_j+1},\; \frac{\gamma_M}{16}M \right\}.

Define the enlarged region for the core set $\mathcal K_n$ as
\[
\mathcal K_n^\ast \coloneqq
\prod_{j\in\mathcal P} \big[-\gamma_\omega R_n^{\alpha_j+1},\, \gamma_\omega R_n^{\alpha_j+1}\big]
\times \left[-\frac{\gamma_M}{4}M,\, \frac{\gamma_M}{4}M\right]^{d-|\mathcal P|}.
\]
We claim that $f_0$ is bounded below on $\mathcal K_n^\ast$. Indeed, on the active coordinates we have the same lower bound $c_\omega R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}$. On the inactive coordinates, since
\[
\left[-\frac{\gamma_M}{4}M,\, \frac{\gamma_M}{4}M\right]^{d-|\mathcal P|}
\subset \left[-\frac{\gamma_M}{2}M,\, \frac{\gamma_M}{2}M\right]^{d-|\mathcal P|},
\]
and $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}$ is continuous and positive on this compact set 
(it equals $c_0 > 0$ on the set $[-(\gamma_M/4)M, \,(\gamma_M
/4)M]^{d-|\mathcal P|}$), there exists $\tilde c_0 > 0$ such that $
f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}(\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P}) \ge \tilde c_0 > 0$.
Therefore, on $\mathcal K_n^\ast$,

    \label{eq:lowerboundonKast}
f_0(\mathbf x) \ge c_\ast^{(1)} \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)},

where $c_\ast^{(1)} \coloneqq \tilde c_0 \, c_\omega^{|\mathcal P|} > 0$.
For $\mathbf x \in \mathcal K_n$ and $\mathbf z \in K_\varpi$, using \eqref{eq:boundonR_K} for the active coordinates we have
\[
|x_j - z_j| \le |x_j| + |z_j|
\le \gamma R_n^{\alpha_j+1} + R_{K_\varpi}
\le \gamma R_n^{\alpha_j+1} + (\gamma_\omega - \gamma) R_n^{\alpha_j+1}
= \gamma_\omega R_n^{\alpha_j+1},
\]
and for the inactive coordinates
\[
|x_k - z_k| \le |x_k| + |z_k|
\le \frac{\gamma_M}{8}M + \frac{\gamma_M}{16}M
= \frac{3\gamma_M}{16}M < \frac{\gamma_M}{4}M.
\]
Thus, $\mathbf x - \mathbf z \in \mathcal K_n^\ast$. Equivalently, $\mathbf x - K_\varpi \coloneqq \{\mathbf x - \mathbf z:\, \mathbf z\in K_\varpi\}\subset \mathcal K_n^\ast$.
Now, for every $\mathbf x \in \mathcal K_n$, using \eqref{eq:lowerboundonKast} and the fact that $\mathbf x - K_\varpi \subset \mathcal K_n^\ast$, we have

    \label{eq:lbeta}
(f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)(\mathbf x)
= \int_{\mathbb R^d} f_0(\mathbf u) f_\varepsilon(\mathbf x - \mathbf u) \, \mathrm{d}\mathbf u
&\ge \int_{\mathcal K_n^\ast} f_0(\mathbf u) f_\varepsilon(\mathbf x - \mathbf u) \, \mathrm{d}\mathbf u\nonumber\\
&\geq 
c_\ast^{(1)} \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}
\int_{\mathcal K_n^\ast} f_\varepsilon(\mathbf x - \mathbf u) \, \mathrm{d}\mathbf u \nonumber\\
&= c_\ast^{(1)} \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}
\int_{K_\varpi} f_\varepsilon(\mathbf z) \, \mathrm{d}\mathbf z \nonumber\\
&\ge c_\ast^{(1)} (1 - \varpi) \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}.

For $\mathbf x \in \mathcal B_n$, we proceed similarly. Since $\mathbf x$ is at distance at least $(\gamma_M/4)M$ from the boundary of $\operatorname{supp}(f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}})$, and $R_{K_\varpi} \le (\gamma_M/16)M$, the set $\mathbf x - K_\varpi$ remains within the compact subset of $\operatorname{supp}(f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}})$ where $f_0$ is bounded below by $c_\ast^{(2)} \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}$. The same convolution argument yields that for some constant $c_\ast^{(2)} > 0$
\[
(f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)(\mathbf x)
\ge c_\ast^{(2)} (1 - \varpi) \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}
\quad \text{for all } \mathbf x \in \mathcal B_n.
\]
Taking $c_\ast\coloneqq \min\{c_\ast^{(1)},\, c_\ast^{(2)}\} > 0$, we obtain the uniform lower bound 
\[
(f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)(\mathbf x)
\ge c_\ast (1 - \varpi) \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}
\quad \text{for all } \mathbf x \in \mathcal K_n^{\mathrm{full}}.
\]
Since $\operatorname{supp}(f_1 - f_0) \subset \mathcal K_n^{\mathrm{full}}=\mathcal K_n \cup \mathcal B_n$,
the $\chi^2$-divergence between the convolved densities can be written as an integral over $\mathcal K_n^{\mathrm{full}}$,
\[

\chi^2(f_1 \ast f_\varepsilon \parallel f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)
&= \int_{\mathcal K_n^{\mathrm{full}}}
\frac{[(f_1\ast f_\varepsilon)(\mathbf x) - (f_0\ast f_\varepsilon)(\mathbf x)]^2}{(f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)(\mathbf x)}
\, \mathrm{d}\mathbf x\\
&= \int_{\mathcal K_n \cup \mathcal B_n}
\frac{[(f_1\ast f_\varepsilon)(\mathbf x) - (f_0\ast f_\varepsilon)(\mathbf x)]^2}{(f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)(\mathbf x)}
\, \mathrm{d}\mathbf x.

\]
By the lower bound on $f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon$ established above, there exists $c_0\coloneqq c_\ast (1 - \varpi) > 0$ independent of $n$ such that uniformly on $\mathcal K_n \cup \mathcal B_n$,
\[
(f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)(\mathbf x) \ge c_0 \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}.
\]
Consequently,
\[
\chi^2(f_1 \ast f_\varepsilon \parallel f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon) \le C \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{\alpha_j+1} \int_{\mathcal K_n \cup \mathcal B_n} [(f_1\ast f_\varepsilon)(\mathbf x) - (f_0\ast f_\varepsilon)(\mathbf x)]^2 \, \mathrm{d}\mathbf x.
\]
Extending the integral to all of $\mathbb R^d$, applying Young's inequality with $\|f_\varepsilon\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} = 1$, and using Plancherel's theorem, we obtain

        {

\chi^2(f_1 \ast f_\varepsilon \parallel f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)
&\le C \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{\alpha_j+1} \|(f_1 - f_0) \ast f_\varepsilon\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}^2 \notag \\
&\le C \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{\alpha_j+1} \|f_1 - f_0\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}^2\|f_\varepsilon\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}  \notag \\
&= C \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{\alpha_j+1} \frac{1}{(2\pi)^d}\|\mathcal F[f_1-f_0]\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}^2. \notag

By Lemma \ref{lem:foexp} applied with $m = |\mathcal P|$, $A_\ell = \omega_\ell(x_\ell)$, $B_\ell = \xi_\ell(x_\ell)$, and $\delta = \lambda_n$, we obtain pointwise for each $\mathbf x \in \mathbb R^d$
\[
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned}

f_1(\mathbf x) - f_0(\mathbf x)
&= \Bigg[ \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} [\omega_j(x_j) + \lambda_n \xi_j(x_j)] - \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} \omega_j(x_j) \Bigg] f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}(\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P}) \\
&
= \lambda_n \sum_{j\in\mathcal P} \xi_j(x_j) \prod_{\substack{\ell\in\mathcal P \\ \ell \neq j}}  \omega_\ell(x_\ell) \, f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}(\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P})
+ R(\lambda_n;\, \mathbf x),

\]
where the remainder $R(\lambda_n;\, \mathbf x)$ from \eqref{eq:remainder} in Lemma \ref{lem:foexp} has the explicit form
\[
R(\lambda_n;\, \mathbf x)
= \lambda_n^2 \sum_{k=2}^{|\mathcal P|}\lambda_n^{k-2} \sum_{\substack{S \subset \mathcal P \\ |S| = k}}
\Bigg( \prod_{j \in S} \xi_j(x_j) \Bigg)
\Bigg( \prod_{\ell \notin S} \omega_\ell(x_\ell) \Bigg)
 f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}(\mathbf x_{\setminus\mathcal P}).
\]
Taking the Fourier transform, which factorizes over independent coordinates, we get
\[
\mathcal F[R(\lambda_n;\, \cdot)](\mathbf t)
= \lambda_n^2 \sum_{k=2}^{|\mathcal P|} \lambda_n^{k-2} \sum_{\substack{S \subset \mathcal P \\ |S| = k}}
\Bigg( \prod_{j \in S} \mathcal F[\xi_j](t_j) \Bigg)
\Bigg( \prod_{\ell \notin S} \mathcal F[\omega_\ell](t_\ell) \Bigg)
\mathcal F[f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}](\mathbf t_{\setminus\mathcal P}).
\]
Since $\omega \in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R)$ and $f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^{d-|\mathcal P|})$ are smooth and compactly supported, we have $\|\mathcal F[\omega]\|_\infty \le C_\omega$ and 
$\|\mathcal F[f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}]\|_\infty \le C_0$ for suitable constants $C_0$ and $C_\omega$. 
Using $\| \mathcal F[\xi_j]\|_{L^2(\mathbb R)}^2=R_n ^{-(\alpha_j+1)}\|\mathcal F[\xi]\|_{L^2(\mathbb R)}^2<\infty$, for a suitable constant $C_2>0$ we obtain
\[
\|\mathcal F[R(\lambda_n;\, \cdot)]\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}^2
\le C_2 \lambda_n^4 \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{-2(\alpha_j+1)}.
\]
Indeed, the dominant term in the sum over $S$ is $|S|=2$; all higher-order terms are smaller since $R_n \ge 1$ and $\lambda_n \le 1$ for sufficiently large $n$. 

Taking the Fourier transform of $f_1-f_0$, and using the same arguments as those used for bounding the remainder term, we obtain
\[

\mathcal F[f_1-f_0](\mathbf t)
&= \lambda_n \sum_{j\in\mathcal P} \mathcal F[\xi_j](t_j)  \prod_{\substack{\ell\in\mathcal P \\ \ell \neq j}} \mathcal F[\omega_\ell](t_\ell)\mathcal F[f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}](\mathbf t_{\setminus\mathcal P})+\mathcal F[R(\lambda_n;\,\cdot)](\mathbf t)\\
&=\lambda_n \sum_{j\in\mathcal P} \mathcal F[\xi](R_n ^{\alpha_j+1}t_j)  \prod_{\substack{\ell\in\mathcal P \\ \ell \neq j}} \mathcal F[\omega](R_n ^{\alpha_\ell+1}t_\ell) \mathcal F[f_0^{\mathrm{inactive}}](\mathbf t_{\setminus\mathcal P})+\mathcal F[R(\lambda_n;\,\cdot)](\mathbf t).

Using the same arguments as those used for bounding the remainder term, we obtain
\[

\|\mathcal F[f_1-f_0]\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}^2&\leq 2\lambda_n^2 C_0^2C_\omega^{2(|\mathcal P|-1)}\sum_{j\in\mathcal P} \|\mathcal F[\xi](R_n ^{\alpha_j+1}\cdot)\|_{L^2(\mathbb R)}^2 +2\|\mathcal F[R(\lambda_n;\,\cdot)]\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}^2\\
& =2
\lambda_n^2 C_0^2C_\omega^{2(|\mathcal P|-1)}\|\mathcal F[\xi]\|_{L^2(\mathbb R)}^2\sum_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n ^{-(\alpha_j+1)} +O\!\left(\lambda_n^4 \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{-2(\alpha_j+1)}\right).

Since $R_n\geq1$ and $\lambda_n \to 0$, the remainder term contributes only lower-order terms. Thus, for a suitable constant \(
C_1 \coloneqq 2(2\pi)^{-d} C C_0^2 C_\omega^{2(|\mathcal P|-1)} |\mathcal P| \|\mathcal F[\xi]\|_{L^2(\mathbb R)}^2\), 
we have
\[
\chi^2(f_1 \ast f_\varepsilon \parallel f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)
\le C_1 \lambda_n^2 \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{\alpha_j+1}.
\]
Taking into account that $\lambda_n = n^{-E/2} \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}$, it follows that 
$$\lambda_n^2 \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{\alpha_j+1}=n^{-E} \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}.$$ Choosing $R_n\coloneqq n^{1/E}$, we obtain
\[

\chi^2(f_1 \ast f_\varepsilon \parallel f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)
&\le C_1 n^{-E} \prod_{j\in\mathcal P} R_n^{-(\alpha_j+1)}
= C_1 n^{-E-\frac{1}{E}\sum_{j\in\mathcal P}(\alpha_j+1)}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned}

\]
Since $\sum_{j\in\mathcal P} (\alpha_j+1) > |\mathcal P| \ge 1$ and $E > 0$, we have
\[
E + \frac{1}{E}\sum_{j\in\mathcal P} (\alpha_j+1)
> E + \frac{1}{E} 
> 1.
\]
Consequently,
$$\chi^2(f_1 \ast f_\varepsilon \parallel f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)=o( n^{-1}).$$


\item \emph{Application of Le Cam's two-point method.}\\[2pt]
We can now prove the minimax lower bound.
By Le Cam's two-point method, see, \emph{e.g.}, Theorem 2.6 in \citet{tsybakov2009introduction}, p. 100,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline*} 
        {\begin{multline*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@multline*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@multline*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@multline*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@multline*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\inf_{\hat F_n} \sup_{f_X \in \mathcal N_1^d(\boldsymbol\alpha,\, L)}
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}
\bigl[ \| \hat F_n - F_X \|_{L^1(\mathbb{R}^d)} \bigr]
\ge \frac{1}{2} \| F_1 - F_0 \|_{L^1(\mathbb{R}^d)} \\
\times [
1 - \| (\mu_1 \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}
    - (\mu_0 \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}
\|_{\mathrm{TV}} ],
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline*} 
        {\end{multline*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@multline*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@multline*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@multline*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@multline*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
where \(\mu_i\) is the distribution corresponding to the density \(f_i\) for \(i = 0,\, 1\).
By the $\chi^2$ bound established above, we have $\chi^2(f_1 \ast f_\varepsilon \parallel f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)=o(n^{-1})$.
By tensorization of the $\chi^2$ divergence,
\[
\chi^2\bigl((f_1 \ast f_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n} \parallel (f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}\bigr)
= [1 + \chi^2(f_1 \ast f_\varepsilon \parallel f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)]^n - 1
= (1 + o(n^{-1}))^n - 1 \to 0.
\]
Then, by Pinsker's inequality,
\[
\| (\mu_1 \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}
    - (\mu_0 \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}
\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
\le
\sqrt{\frac12 \chi^2\bigl((f_1 \ast f_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n} \parallel (f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}\bigr)}
\to 0.
\]
Therefore,
$1 - \| (\mu_1 \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}
    - (\mu_0 \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}
\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \to 1$.
Hence, from Le Cam's lower bound, since \(\|F_1 - F_0\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}\geq c_1 n^{-E/2}\) from \eqref{eq:separate}, for $n$ sufficiently large we obtain
\[
\inf_{\hat F_n} \sup_{f_X \in \mathcal N_1^d(\boldsymbol\alpha,\, L)}
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}} [\|\hat F_n - F_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}]
\ge \frac12 \|F_1 - F_0\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} [1 - o(1)]\geq\frac{c_1}{4}n^{-\frac E2}.
\]
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
        {\end{enumerate}} 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lemma:talagrand}
Let \(\mathbf{Y}_1,\,\dots,\,\mathbf{Y}_n\) be i.i.d. random vectors in \(\mathbb R^d\), and let \(\bar{B}\) be a countable subset of a function space. For each \(f\in \bar{B}\), let 
$
\psi_f:\mathbb R^d \to \mathbb R
$
be a real-valued measurable function indexed by \(f\). Define the centered empirical process
\[
\nu_n(f) \coloneqq \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n 
[ \psi_f(\mathbf{Y}_i) - \mathsf{E}[\psi_f(\mathbf{Y}_1)]],
\quad f\in \bar{B}.
\]
Assume that there exist constants \(0<\Gamma,\,M,\,\upsilon<\infty\) such that
\[
\mathsf{E}\bigg[\sup_{f\in \bar{B}} |\nu_n(f)|\bigg]\le \Gamma,\qquad
\sup_{f\in \bar{B}} \| \psi_f\|_{\infty} \le M, \qquad
\sup_{f\in \bar{B}} \operatorname{Var}
[\psi_f(\mathbf{Y}_1)]\le \upsilon,
\]
where \(\| \psi_f\|_{\infty}\coloneqq\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb R^d}|\psi_f(\mathbf{y})|\) denotes the supremum norm. Then, for any \( \eta > 0 \),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:Talineq}
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split}

        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathsf{E}\bigg[\bigg( \sup_{f\in \bar{B}} |\nu_n(f)| - (1+2\eta)\Gamma \bigg)_+ \bigg]
&\le \sqrt{\frac{3\pi}{2}} \sqrt{\frac{\upsilon}{n}} \,
\exp\left(- \frac{\eta^{2}}{6} \cdot \frac{n \Gamma^{2}}{\upsilon}\right)\\
&\hspace*{3cm} + \frac{21}{\eta\wedge 1} \cdot\frac{M}{n}
\exp\left(- \frac{\eta (\eta\wedge 1)}{21} \cdot \frac{n \Gamma}{M}\right),

with \((x)_+ \coloneqq \max\{x,\,0\}\) the positive part of $x$.

<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:oracle}
Assume that the noise conditions \ref{eq:E1indep}, \ref{it:E21} and \ref{eq:E3} \hyperref[eq:E1indep]{of the Main Article} hold with \(\beta_j>0\) for all \(j=1,\,\ldots,\,d\). Consider the kernel-based estimator \(\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}\) constructed with a product kernel \(K(\mathbf{x})=\prod_{j=1}^d K_j(x_j)\), \(\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb R^d\), where each univariate kernel \(K_j\) satisfies the conditions (i)--(iii) listed in Proposition~\ref{prop:variance}.
Then, there exist constants \(C_1,\,C_2,\,C_3>0\), depending only on the kernels \(K_j\) and the noise distribution, such that, for every bandwidth vector \(\mathbf{h}\in(0,\,1)^d\), for any sequence $C_0(\mathbf{h})\geq1$ (which may depend on $\mathbf{h}$), and for every \(\eta>0\), the following inequality holds:

        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
&\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}\Bigl[\Bigl(
\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}-(K_{\mathbf{h}}\ast F_X)\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)} 
- (1+2\eta) C_0(\mathbf{h})C_1
\mathcal V(\mathbf{h})
\Bigr)_+\Bigr] \\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\le \sqrt{\frac{3\pi}{2}} \sqrt{\frac{\upsilon}{n}} \,
\exp\biggl(- \frac{\eta^{2}C_1^2 }{6C_3^2}C_0^2(\mathbf{h})\biggr)
+ \frac{21}{\eta\wedge 1} C_2
\frac{\mathcal V(\mathbf{h})}{\sqrt{n}}
\exp\biggl(- \frac{\eta (\eta\wedge 1)C_1}{21C_2} \sqrt{n}\biggr),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {

        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}}

    }

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
where $\mathcal V(\mathbf{h})$ is defined in \eqref{eq:varprox} \hyperref[eq:varprox]{of the Main Article} and \(\upsilon \coloneqq C_3^2 n \mathcal V^2(\mathbf{h})\).

<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
The proof proceeds by applying Talagrand's inequality~\eqref{eq:Talineq}, stated in Lemma~\ref{lemma:talagrand}. Let 
\[
B(0,\,1)\coloneqq \bigl\{ f\in L^1(\mathbb R^d)\cap L^2(\mathbb R^d):\, \|f\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)} = 1 \bigr\}
\]
and let \(\bar{B}(0,\,1)\) be a countable dense subset of \(B(0,\,1)\), whose existence follows from the separability of \(L^2(\mathbb R^d)\). For \(f\in \bar{B}(0,\,1)\), define
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\psi_f(\mathbf{y})
&\coloneqq \int_{\mathbb R^d} \overline{f(\mathbf{v})}
\left(
\int_{-\infty}^{v_1} \cdots \int_{-\infty}^{v_d}
\mathcal F^{-1}\!\left[
\frac{\varphi_K(\mathbf{h}\odot \mathbf{t})\, \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} \langle \mathbf{t},\, \mathbf{y} \rangle}}
{\varphi_\varepsilon(\mathbf{t})}
\right](\mathbf{u})
\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}
\right)
\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v},
\quad \mathbf{y}\in\mathbb R^d,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
where \(\mathbf{h}\odot\mathbf{t} \coloneqq (h_1 t_1,\, \ldots,\, h_d t_d)^\top\) denotes the componentwise (Hadamard) product of the two vectors $\mathbf{h}$ and $\mathbf{t}$\footnote{Recall that the componentwise product corresponds to the action of the diagonal bandwidth matrix \(\mathbf{H}=\operatorname{diag}(h_1,\,\ldots,\,h_d)\) on \(\mathbf{t}\), \emph{i.e.}, \(\mathbf{H}\mathbf{t} = \mathbf{h}\odot\mathbf{t}\).}, and \(\overline{z}\) denotes the complex conjugate of \(z\in\mathbb C\).


Recalling the definition of \(G_{j,h_j}\) in \eqref{eq:gfun}, we note that \(\psi_f(\mathbf{y})\) can be equivalently written as
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:termproc}
\psi_f(\mathbf{y})
= \int_{\mathbb R^d} \overline{f(\mathbf{v})}
\prod_{j=1}^d G_{j,h_j}\!\left(\frac{v_j-y_j}{h_j}\right)
\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v},
\quad \mathbf{y}\in\mathbb R^d.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
We define the empirical process indexed by \(f\in \bar{B}(0,\,1)\) as follows:
\[
\nu_n(f) \coloneqq \langle \widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}} - (K_{\mathbf{h}}\ast F_X),\, f \rangle_2
= \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n [ \psi_f(\mathbf{Y}_i) - \mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)}[\psi_f(\mathbf{Y}_1)] ].
\]
The continuity of the mapping \(f \mapsto \nu_n(f)\) is required to justify the equality of suprema
\[
\sup_{f \in B(0,1)} |\nu_n(f)| = \sup_{f \in \bar{B}(0,1)} |\nu_n(f)|,
\]
which is used below in \eqref{eq:taldiseq} to write
\[
\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}-(K_{\mathbf{h}}\ast F_X)\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)} = \sup_{f \in \bar{B}(0,1)} |\nu_n(f)|.
\]
This continuity is essential for the application of Talagrand's inequality. Indeed, Talagrand's concentration inequality for empirical processes is typically stated for separable function classes; the supremum over a non-separable index set may fail to be measurable, and the constants in the inequality may depend on separability assumptions. By constructing the countable dense subset \(\bar{B}(0,\,1)\) and establishing continuity of the empirical process, we ensure that the supremum over the countable subclass equals that over the entire unit ball. This allows us to apply Talagrand's inequality to the countable class \(\bar{B}(0,\,1)\) without measurability issues, while still controlling the supremum over the whole ball \(B(0,\,1)\).

To prove that the mapping \(f \mapsto \nu_n(f)\) is continuous on \(\bar{B}(0,\,1)\subset L^2(\mathbb R^d)\) equipped with the \(L^2\)-norm, we proceed as follows.
First, note that \(\nu_n(f)\) is linear in \(f\), since \(\psi_f\) is linear in \(f\) as seen from its integral definition. Thus, it suffices to show that \(\nu_n\) is bounded on the unit ball of \(L^2(\mathbb R^d)\), \emph{i.e.}, there exists a constant \(B<\infty\) such that for all \(f\in B(0,\,1)\), we have \(|\nu_n(f)|\le B\). Then, linearity combined with boundedness implies continuity.
Using the representation \eqref{eq:termproc} and the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality, for any \(\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb R^d\),
\[
|\psi_f(\mathbf{y})|
\le \|f\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}
\bigg(
\int_{\mathbb R^d}
\prod_{j=1}^d
\left| G_{j,h_j}\!\left(\frac{v_j-y_j}{h_j}\right) \right|^2
\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v}
\bigg)^{1/2}
=
\|f\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}
\prod_{j=1}^d
\bigg(
\int_{\mathbb R}
\left| G_{j,h_j}\!\left(\frac{u_j}{h_j}\right) \right|^2
\,\mathrm{d}u_j
\bigg)^{1/2},
\]
where we used the change of variables \(u_j=v_j-y_j\) and the product structure. The last expression is independent of \(\mathbf{y}\). Therefore,
\[
\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb R^d} |\psi_f(\mathbf{y})|
\le \|f\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)} \prod_{j=1}^d \|G_{j,h_j}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R)},
\]
where each \(\|G_{j,h_j}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R)}<\infty\) under the assumptions on \(K_j\) as in Proposition~\ref{prop:variance}. Consequently, since \(\|f\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}=1\),
\[ 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        {\begin{split}} 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
|\nu_n(f)|
&\le \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n
[|\psi_f(\mathbf{Y}_i)| + \mathsf E[|\psi_f(\mathbf{Y}_1)|]]\\
&\le 2\|f\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)} \prod_{j=1}^d \|G_{j,h_j}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R)}\\
&=2\prod_{j=1}^d \|G_{j,h_j}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R)}\eqqcolon B<\infty.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        {\end{split}} 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
Thus, \(\nu_n\) is bounded on \(B(0,\,1)\) and, by linearity, it is continuous. Since \(\bar{B}(0,\,1)\) is dense in \(B(0,\,1)\), the continuity extends to the closure, and the supremum over the dense subset equals the supremum over the whole ball.

We now compute the constants $\Gamma$, $M$ and $\upsilon$ required in Lemma~\ref{lemma:talagrand}.

    [label=\bf{$\bullet$}]
    \item \emph{Evaluation of the constant $\Gamma$.} Note that 
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@eqnarray}

    \label{eq:taldiseq}
\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}} - (K_{\mathbf{h}} \ast F_X)\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}
&=& \sup_{f \in B(0,1)} |\langle \widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}} - (K_{\mathbf{h}} \ast F_X),\, f \rangle_2|
\nonumber\\&=& \sup_{f \in \bar{B}(0,1)} |\langle \widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}} - (K_{\mathbf{h}} \ast F_X),\, f \rangle_2|=\sup_{f \in \bar{B}(0,1)} |\nu_n(f)|.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@eqnarray}

Then, by Jensen's inequality and using the identity $ \mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}(\mathbf{x})]=(K_{\mathbf{h}} \ast F_X)(\mathbf{x})$, which holds pointwise for every $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^d$ as established in \eqref{eq:bias} \hyperref[eq:bias]{of the Main Article}, we proceed analogously to Proposition~\ref{prop:variance} to obtain 
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
        {

        {

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:ineqproc}
&\Bigg(\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}\Bigg[\sup_{f\in\bar{B}(0,1)}|\nu_n(f)|\Bigg]\Bigg)^2\\
&\hspace*{4cm}\leq 
\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}\Bigg[\sup_{f\in\bar{B}(0,1)}|\nu_n(f)|^2\Bigg]\nonumber\\
&\hspace*{4cm}=
\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}\Bigg[\sup_{f\in\bar{B}(0,1)}|\langle \widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}-(K_{\mathbf{h}}\ast F_X),\, f \rangle_2|^2\Bigg]\nonumber\\
&\hspace*{4cm}\leq\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}\Bigg[\sup_{f\in\bar{B}(0,1)}
\Big(\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}-(K_{\mathbf{h}}\ast F_X)\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}^2 \|f\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}^2\Big)\Bigg]\nonumber\\
&\hspace*{4cm}=\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}\Bigl[
\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}-(K_{\mathbf{h}}\ast F_X)\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}^2\Bigr]\nonumber\\
&\hspace*{4cm}=\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}\Bigl[
\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}-\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}]\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}^2\Bigr]\nonumber\\
&\hspace*{4cm}=\int_{\mathbb R^d}\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}\bigl[
|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}(\mathbf{x})-\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}(\mathbf{x})]|^2\bigr]\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\nonumber\\
&\hspace*{4cm}=\int_{\mathbb R^d}
\operatorname{Var}[\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}(\mathbf{x})]
\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\nonumber\\
&\hspace*{4cm}\lesssim \mathcal V^2(\mathbf{h}).

        {

        {

        {

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
We set $C_1(\mathbf{h})\coloneqq C_1 \prod_{j=1}^d |\log h_j|^{\mathbf{1}_{\{\beta_j=1/2\}}/2}$,
where \(C_1^2>0\) is the multiplicative constant omitted in \eqref{eq:ineqproc}. We obtain
\[
\Bigg(\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}\Bigg[\sup_{f\in\bar{B}(0,1)} |\nu_n(f)|\Bigg]\Bigg)^2 \leq [C_1(\mathbf{h}) V(\mathbf{h})]^2,
\]
and, for any function $C_0(\mathbf{h})\geq1$, 
\[
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}\Bigg[\sup_{f\in\bar{B}(0,1)} |\nu_n(f)|\Bigg] \leq C_1(\mathbf{h}) V(\mathbf{h})\leq C_0(\mathbf{h})C_1(\mathbf{h}) V(\mathbf{h}).
\]
Define

Conclude that $$\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}\Bigg[\sup_{f\in\bar{B}(0,1)} |\nu_n(f)|\Bigg]
\leq \Gamma.$$
\item \emph{Evaluation of the constant $M$.} Using the expression for $\psi_f$ given in \eqref{eq:termproc} and applying the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality, we reason as in Proposition~\ref{prop:variance} to obtain 
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align}

    \label{eq:constsupnorm}
\sup_{f\in\bar{B}(0,1)}\|\psi_f\|_\infty&=
\sup_{f\in\bar{B}(0,1)}
\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb R^d}
|\psi_f(\mathbf{y})|\nonumber\\
&=\sup_{f\in\bar{B}(0,1)}
\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb R^d}
\biggl|\int_{\mathbb R^d}\overline{f(\mathbf{v})}\prod_{j=1}^dG_{j,h_j}\bigg(\frac{v_j-y_j}{h_j}\bigg)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v}\biggr|\nonumber\\
&\leq\biggl(\sup_{f\in\bar{B}(0,1)}\|f\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}\biggr)
\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb R^d}
\Biggl(\int_{\mathbb R^d}\prod_{j=1}^d\biggl|G_{j,h_j}\bigg(\frac{v_j-y_j}{h_j}\bigg)\biggr|^2\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v}\Biggr)^{1/2}\nonumber\\
&=\prod_{j=1}^d\Bigg(\sup_{y_j\in\mathbb R}\int_{\mathbb R}\bigg|G_{j,h_j}\bigg(\frac{v_j-y_j}{h_j}\bigg)\bigg|^2\mathrm{d}v_j\Bigg)^{1/2}\nonumber\\
&\lesssim 
\sqrt{n}\,\mathcal V(\mathbf{h}).

Let $C_2(\mathbf{h})\coloneqq C_2 \prod_{j=1}^d |\log h_j|^{\mathbf{1}_{\{\beta_j=1/2\}}/2}$, where $C_2$ is the multiplicative constant omitted in \eqref{eq:constsupnorm}. We obtain
\[
\sup_{f\in\bar{B}(0,1)}\|\psi_f\|_\infty \leq C_2(\mathbf{h}) \sqrt{n}V(\mathbf{h}).
\]
We can therefore define $M\coloneqq C_2(\mathbf{h})\sqrt{n}V(\mathbf{h})$ and conclude that 
\[
\sup_{f\in\bar{B}(0,1)}\|\psi_f\|_\infty \leq M.
\]

&=\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)}\Biggl[\prod_{j=1}^d\int_{\mathbb R}\biggl|G_{j,h_j}\biggl(\frac{v_j-Y_{1,j}}{h_j}\biggr)\biggr|^2\mathrm{d}v_j\Biggr]\nonumber\\
&\leq\prod_{j=1}^d\biggl(\sup_{y_j\in\mathbb R}\int_{\mathbb R}\biggl|G_{j,h_j}\biggl(\frac{v_j-y_j}{h_j}\biggr)\biggr|^2\mathrm{d}v_j\biggr)\nonumber\\
&\lesssim n\, \mathcal V^2(\mathbf{h}).

However, it should be kept in mind that these quantities do depend on $\mathbf{h}$; this dependence will always be clear from the context and is essential for the subsequent application of Talagrand's inequality.
\item \emph{Application of Talagrand's inequality.}
Having evaluated all the constants $\Gamma,\,M,\,\upsilon$, and recalling from \eqref{eq:taldiseq} that
$\sup_{f\in\bar{B}(0,1)}|\nu_n(f)|=\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}-(K_{\mathbf{h}}\ast F_X)\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}$, Talagrand's inequality \eqref{eq:Talineq} yields, for any $\eta>0$,

&\mathsf{E}\Big[\Big(\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}-(K_{\mathbf{h}}\ast F_X)\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)} -(1+2\eta) C_0(\mathbf{h})C_1(\mathbf{h})V(\mathbf{h})\Big)_+\Big]\\
&\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\le \sqrt{\frac{3\pi}{2}} \sqrt{\frac{\upsilon}{n}} \,
\exp\bigg(- \frac{\eta^{2}C_1^2}{6C_3^2}C^2_0(\mathbf{h})\bigg)
+ \frac{21}{\eta\wedge 1} C_2(\mathbf{h})\frac{V(\mathbf{h})}{\sqrt{n}}
\exp\bigg(- \frac{\eta (\eta\wedge 1)}{21} \frac{C_0(\mathbf{h})C_1(\mathbf{h})}{C_2(\mathbf{h})}\sqrt{n}\bigg)\\
&\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\leq\sqrt{\frac{3\pi}{2}} \sqrt{\frac{\upsilon}{n}} \,
\exp\bigg(- \frac{\eta^{2}C_1^2}{6C_3^2}C^2_0(\mathbf{h})\bigg)+ \frac{21}{\eta\wedge 1} C_2\frac{\mathcal V(\mathbf{h})}{\sqrt{n}}\exp\bigg(- \frac{\eta (\eta\wedge 1)C_1}{21C_2} \sqrt{n}\bigg),

\item \emph{Reduction to the adaptive kernel-based estimator.}\label{st:reduc}\\[2pt]
Recall the definition of the adaptive kernel-based estimator
\[
\check{F}_n \coloneqq \widehat{F}_{n,\hat{\mathbf{h}}}, \quad \text{with }\,
\hat{\mathbf{h}} := \operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\mathbf{h} \in \mathcal H_{M_n}} \big\{D(\mathbf{h}) + \tilde V(\mathbf{h}) \big\},
\]
where \(D(\mathbf{h})\) and \(\tilde V(\mathbf{h})\) are defined in \eqref{eq:Ah} and \eqref{eq:proxyvar} \hyperref[eq:Ah]{of the Main Article} (see also \eqref{eq:defvtilde}), respectively.

For an arbitrary bandwidth vector \(\mathbf{h} \in \mathcal H_{M_n}\), we will show that the \(L^2\)-error of \(\check{F}_n\) is bounded, up to universal constants, by the oracle risk associated with this fixed \(\mathbf{h}\), plus a negligible remainder. Since \(\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal H_{M_n}\) is arbitrary, this will allow us to take the infimum over all \(\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal H_{M_n}\) at the end of the proof.

By the same arguments used to obtain~\eqref{eq:stimdist} in Theorem~\ref{thm:ubrate}, for every \(\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal H_{M_n}\), the minimum-$L^2$-distance estimator \(\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}^\ast\) satisfies
\[
\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}^\ast - F_X\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}
\leq 2\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}} - F_X\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)} + \varrho_n.
\]
In particular, choosing \(\mathbf{h} = \hat{\mathbf{h}}\) yields (see also \eqref{eq:reductionstep} \hyperref[eq:reductionstep]{of the Main Article})
\[
\|\widehat F_{n,\hat{\mathbf{h}}}^\ast - F_X\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}
\leq 2\|\check{F}_n - F_X\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)} + \varrho_n.
\]
Therefore, it suffices to prove an oracle inequality for the adaptive kernel-based estimator \(\check{F}_n\). This is the goal of the following steps.
                                        
                                            
\item \emph{Error decomposition via convolution symmetry.}\label{st:decomp}\\[2pt]
For every pair of bandwidth vectors $\mathbf{h},\,\mathbf{h}'\in\mathbb R_+^d$, the following convolution identity holds.\footnote{Recall that for $\mathbf{a}=(a_1,\,\dots,\,a_d)^{\!\top}$ and $\mathbf{t}=(t_1,\,\dots,\,t_d)^{\!\top}$, 
we denote by $\mathbf{a}\odot\mathbf{t}$ the componentwise (Hadamard) product 
$
\mathbf{a}\odot\mathbf{t}=(a_1t_1,\,\dots,\,a_dt_d)^{\!\top}$.} For each $\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb R^d$,

    \label{eq:identityhh'}
(K_{\mathbf{h}'}\ast \widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}})(\mathbf{x})
&= \biggl(K_{\mathbf{h}'}\ast \mathcal F^{-1}\biggl[\frac{\varphi_n(\mathbf{t})\varphi_K(\mathbf{h}\odot \mathbf{t})}{\varphi_\varepsilon(\mathbf{t})}\biggr]\biggr)(\mathbf{x}) \nonumber\\
&= \mathcal F^{-1}\bigg[\frac{\varphi_K(\mathbf{h}'\odot \mathbf{t})\varphi_n(\mathbf{t})\varphi_K(\mathbf{h}\odot \mathbf{t})}{\varphi_\varepsilon(\mathbf{t})}\bigg](\mathbf{x}) \nonumber\\
&= \bigg(K_{\mathbf{h}}\ast \mathcal F^{-1}\bigg[\frac{\varphi_n(\mathbf{t})\varphi_K(\mathbf{h}'\odot \mathbf{t})}{\varphi_\varepsilon(\mathbf{t})}\bigg]\bigg)(\mathbf{x}) = (K_{\mathbf{h}}\ast \widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}'})(\mathbf{x}).

&=D_3+D_4+\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}} - F_X\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}.

By adding and subtracting $\tilde V({\hat{\mathbf{h}}})$ and by definition of $D(\mathbf{h})$ in \eqref{eq:Ah} \hyperref[eq:Ah]{of the Main Article}), one obtains

\item \emph{Bias-variance decomposition for the kernel estimator.}\label{st:bias-variance}\\[2pt]
Recalling that $B_{\mathbf{h}}\coloneqq F_X-(K_{\mathbf{h}}\ast F_X)$, the $L^2$-error of the estimator $\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}}$ is decomposed via the triangle inequality as

\item \emph{Application of the concentration inequality.}\label{st:concentration}\\[2pt]
Let $C_V > 1$ be a universal constant to be specified below, and set
\[
\eta \coloneqq \frac{C_V - 1}{2} > 0.
\]
Note that $\eta$ does not depend on $\mathbf{h}'$.
For each $\mathbf{h}' \in \mathcal H_{M_n}$, recall from the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:oracle} that
$
\Gamma \coloneqq C_0(\mathbf{h}') C_1(\mathbf{h}') V(\mathbf{h}')$,
where $C_0(\mathbf{h}') \ge 1$ will be chosen to ensure the summability over the grid, and with $C_1(\mathbf{h}') = C_1 \prod_{j=1}^d |\log h_j'|^{\mathbf{1}_{\{\beta_j=1/2\}}/2}$
from the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:oracle}.
Define the variance penalty term 
\[
\tilde V^2(\mathbf{h}') \coloneqq C_V^2 (1+C_K)^2 \Gamma^2.
\]
Then, for every $\mathbf{h}' \in \mathcal H_{M_n}$,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation}

        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:defvtilde}
\frac{\tilde V(\mathbf{h}')}{1+C_K}
= C_V \Gamma
= (1+2\eta)\Gamma.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {
