    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
The purpose of this definition is to ensure that the penalty term 
$\tilde V(\mathbf{h}')/(1+C_K)$ in the definition of $D(\mathbf{h})$ 
matches the shifted expectation term $(1+2\eta)\Gamma$ from 
Talagrand's inequality (Lemma~\ref{lemma:talagrand}). This allows us to apply 
the concentration inequality uniformly over the grid $\mathcal H_{M_n}$. 
The constant $C_V > 1$ is chosen so that $\eta > 0$, which is required for 
the exponential decay in Talagrand's inequality.

-\frac{\tilde V(\mathbf{h}')}{1+C_K}\bigg)_+\bigg]\nonumber\\

where we recall from \eqref{eq:defupsilon} that
$
\upsilon\coloneqq C_3^2(\mathbf{h}')nV^2(\mathbf{h}')
= C_3^2\mathcal V^2(\mathbf{h}')$.
                                                                
Let \(C_0(\mathbf{h}')\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^d C_{0j}(h_j')\), where we choose, for each \(j=1,\,\ldots,\,d\),
\[
C_{0j}(h_j') \coloneqq \frac{\sqrt{6}C_3}{\eta C_1}
\sqrt{\bigl[ \bigl(\beta_j-\tfrac12\bigr)_+ + (1 + \delta) \bigr] |\log h_j'|},
\quad \delta>0.
\]
Then,
\[
C_0^2(\mathbf h') = \sum_{j=1}^d C_{0j}^2(h_j')
= \frac{6C_3^2}{\eta C_1^2}
\sum_{j=1}^d \bigl[ \bigl(\beta_j-\tfrac12\bigr)_+ + (1 + \delta) \bigr] |\log h_j'|.
\]
On the grid \(\mathcal H_{M_n}\), we have \(h_j' = k_j^{-1}\), hence \(|\log h_j'| = \log k_j\). Thus,
\[ 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        {\begin{split}} 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\sum_{\mathbf h'\in\mathcal H_{M_n}}
\sqrt{\upsilon}
\exp\!\left(-\frac{\eta^2 C_1^2}{6C_3^2} C^2_0(\mathbf h')\right)
&=
\frac{C_3}{n}
\sum_{k_1=1}^{M_n}\cdots\sum_{k_d=1}^{M_n} \prod_{j=1}^dk_j^{-(1+\delta)}
(\log k_j)^{\mathbf 1_{\{\beta_j=1/2\}}/2}\\
&=\frac{C_3}{n}\prod_{j=1}^d\Biggl(\sum_{k=1}^{M_n} k^{-(1+\delta)}
(\log k)^{\mathbf 1_{\{\beta_j=1/2\}}/2}\Biggr)=O(1),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        {\end{split}} 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    \]
since \(\delta>0\) and $\sum_{k=1}^\infty k^{-(1+\delta)}(\log k)^{\gamma_j}<\infty$ for every $\gamma_j\in\{0,\,1/2\}$. 

                                                                
We now show that also the second summability bound in \eqref{eq:bound2} holds. 
Since on the anisotropic grid \(\mathcal H_{M_n}\) we have \(h_j' = k_j^{-1}\) for \(j=1,\,\dots,\,d\), so that \(|\log h_j'| = \log k_j\), and since 
\(V(\mathbf h') \le 1\) by definition of the grid, it follows that
\[
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\begin{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
&\exp\!\left(- \frac{\eta (\eta\wedge 1)C_1}{21C_2} \sqrt{n}\right)
\sum_{\mathbf h'\in\mathcal H_{M_n}}
V(\mathbf h')
\prod_{j=1}^d |\log h_j'|^{\mathbf 1_{\{\beta_j=1/2\}}/2}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad
\le
\exp\!\left(- \frac{\eta (\eta\wedge 1)C_1}{21C_2} \sqrt{n}\right)
\sum_{k_1=1}^{M_n} \cdots \sum_{k_d=1}^{M_n}
\prod_{j=1}^d (\log k_j)^{\mathbf 1_{\{\beta_j=1/2\}}/2} \\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad
=
\exp\!\left(- \frac{\eta (\eta\wedge 1)C_1}{21C_2} \sqrt{n}\right)
\prod_{j=1}^d \biggl( \sum_{k=1}^{M_n} (\log k)^{\mathbf 1_{\{\beta_j=1/2\}}/2} \biggr) \\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad
\le
\exp\!\left(- \frac{\eta (\eta\wedge 1)C_1}{21C_2} \sqrt{n}\right)
M_n^d (\log M_n)^{\frac12\sum_{j=1}^d \mathbf 1_{\{\beta_j=1/2\}}} \\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad
\le
\exp\!\left(- \frac{\eta (\eta\wedge 1)C_1}{21C_2} \sqrt{n}\right)
n^{d/2} (\log n)^{\frac12\sum_{j=1}^d \mathbf 1_{\{\beta_j=1/2\}}}
= O(1),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\end{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
where in the penultimate inequality we used the choice \(M_n = \lfloor n^{1/2} \rfloor\), and in the last step we used that the exponential decay in \(\sqrt{n}\) dominates any polynomial growth. Hence, \eqref{eq:bound2} follows.
                                                                

Combining \eqref{eq:bound1} and \eqref{eq:bound2} with \eqref{eq:meanA}, we obtain
\[
\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}\bigg[\sup_{\mathbf{h}'\in\mathcal H_{M_n}}\bigg(\|\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}'}-\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\widehat F_{n,\mathbf{h}'}]\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}
-\frac{\tilde V(\mathbf{h}')}{1+C_K}\bigg)_+\bigg]\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}.
\]
Substituting this into \eqref{eq:finalbound} yields, for the arbitrary fixed \(\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal H_{M_n}\),
\[
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\begin{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\check{F}_n-F_X\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}]
&\lesssim
\|B_{\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}
+\tilde V(\mathbf{h})
+\mathcal V(\mathbf{h})
+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}} \\
&\lesssim
\|B_{\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}
+
\mathcal V(\mathbf{h})
+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\end{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
Since \(\tilde V(\mathbf{h}) = C_V(1+C_K)\Gamma\) and, by construction, \(\Gamma\) is of the same order as \(\mathcal V(\mathbf{h})\) up to universal constants (see the definition of \(\Gamma\) in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:oracle}), the term \(2\tilde V(\mathbf{h})\) in \eqref{eq:finalbound} is absorbed into the \(\mathcal V(\mathbf{h})\)-term up to a multiplicative constant.
Because the above inequality holds for every fixed \(\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal H_{M_n}\), we may take the infimum over \(\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal H_{M_n}\) on the right-hand side. This gives the oracle inequality for the adaptive kernel estimator:
\[
\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\check{F}_n-F_X\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}]
\lesssim\inf_{\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal H_{M_n}}\big\{\|B_{\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}+
\mathcal V(\mathbf{h})\big\}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}.
\]
Finally, recalling the reduction step, namely$
\|\widehat F_{n,\hat{\mathbf{h}}}^\ast - F_X\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}
\leq 2\|\check{F}_n - F_X\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)} + \varrho_n$,
and taking expectations, we obtain the oracle inequality for the original $\varrho_n$-approximation minimum-$L^2$-distance estimator
\[
\mathsf E_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}\Bigl[\|\widehat F_{n,\hat{\mathbf{h}}}
^\ast-F_X\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}\Bigr]
\lesssim
\inf_{\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal H_{M_n}}
\big\{
\|B_{\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}
+
\mathcal V(\mathbf{h})
\big\}
+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}} + \varrho_n.
\]
\item \emph{Grid approximation.}\\[2pt]
It remains to justify that the infimum over the discrete grid $\mathcal H_{M_n}$ is equivalent, up to universal constants, to the infimum over $(0,\,1)^d$. Let $\mathbf{h}_{\mathrm{opt}} \in (0,\,1)^d$ be a minimizer of
$\|B_{\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}
+ \mathcal V(\mathbf{h})$. 
From the bias-variance trade-off, the optimal bandwidths satisfy
\[
h_{\mathrm{opt},j} \asymp n^{-\frac{E}{2(\alpha_j+1)}} \times (\log n)^{\omega_j},
\]
with $\omega_j \ge 0$ because the logarithmic factors in the variance appear with non-negative exponents (they are zero when \(\beta_j \ne 1/2\) and positive when \(\beta_j = 1/2\)); hence they can only increase the bandwidth relative to the polynomial rate. Consequently,
\[
h_{\mathrm{opt},j} \ge c \, n^{-\frac{E}{2(\alpha_j+1)}} \ge c \, n^{-1/2}
\]
for some universal constant $c > 0$. Since $\mathcal H_{M_n}$ contains all bandwidths of the form $k_j^{-1}$ with $1 \le k_j \le M_n = \lfloor n^{1/2} \rfloor$, for each coordinate $j$ there exists an integer $k_j \le M_n$ such that $k_j^{-1} \asymp h_{\mathrm{opt},j}$.
Set $\mathbf{h}^{(\mathbf{k})} \coloneqq (k_1^{-1},\, \ldots,\, k_d^{-1}) \in \mathcal H_{M_n}$. Then, $h_j^{(\mathbf{k})} \asymp h_{\mathrm{opt},j}$ for all $j$. Consequently,
\[
\frac{V(\mathbf{h}^{(\mathbf{k})})}{V(\mathbf{h}_{\mathrm{opt}})}
=
\prod_{j=1}^d
\biggl(
\frac{h_{\mathrm{opt},j}}{h_j^{(\mathbf{k})}}
\biggr)^{(\beta_j - \frac12)_+}
= O(1),
\]
so $V(\mathbf{h}^{(\mathbf{k})}) \le D_1 V(\mathbf{h}_{\mathrm{opt}})$ for some universal constant $D_1 > 0$. Similarly, since $h_j^{(\mathbf{k})} \asymp h_{\mathrm{opt},j}$, we have $|\log h_j^{(\mathbf{k})}| \asymp |\log h_{\mathrm{opt},j}|$, hence the product of the logarithmic factors differ by at most a multiplicative constant $D_2$. For the bias, the asymptotic equivalence $h_j^{(\mathbf{k})} \asymp h_{\mathrm{opt},j}$, together with the bound $\|B_{\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)} \le L\sum_{j=1}^d C_j h_j^{\alpha_j+1}$ valid for every $\mathbf{h} \in (0,1)^d$, gives
$
\|B_{\mathbf{h}^{(\mathbf{k})}}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}
\le D_3 \|B_{\mathbf{h}_{\mathrm{opt}}}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}
$
for some universal constant $D_3 > 0$. Hence, taking $D := \max\{D_1 D_2,\, D_3\}$, we obtain
\[
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\begin{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
&\inf_{\mathbf{h} \in \mathcal H_{M_n}}
\bigl\{
\|B_{\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}
+
\mathcal V(\mathbf{h})
\bigr\}\le
D
\inf_{\mathbf{h} \in (0,1)^d}
\bigl\{
\|B_{\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}
+
\mathcal V(\mathbf{h})
\bigr\}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\end{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
The reverse inequality is trivial since $\mathcal H_{M_n} \subset (0,\,1)^d$. This proves the equivalence up to universal constants.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@steps} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@steps} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@steps} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@steps} 
        {\end{steps}} 
        {\csname pseudo@steps\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@steps\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@steps\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@steps\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
The proof is thus concluded.

We establish the oracle inequalities in three steps.\\[-5pt]
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@steps}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@steps\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\item\emph{$L^2$-oracle for \(\check{F}_n\).}\label{st:oraclekern}\\[2pt]
This is Theorem~\ref{thm:oracle_ineq}, which gives
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\check{F}_n - F_X\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}]
\lesssim
\mathcal R_n.
\label{eq:L2oracle}
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    

\item\emph{$L^1$-oracle for \(\check{F}_n\).}\label{st:l1oracle}\\[2pt]
Decompose the $L^1$-risk to obtain
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline} 
        {\begin{multline}} 
        {\csname pseudo@multline\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@multline\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@multline\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@multline\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:L1decomp}
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\check{F}_n - F_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}]
\le
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\check{F}_n - \mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\check{F}_n]\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}] \\
+ \mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|B_{\hat{\mathbf{h}}}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}].
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@multline} 
        {\end{multline}} 
        {\csname pseudo@multline\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@multline\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@multline\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@multline\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
The first term on the right-hand side of \eqref{eq:L1decomp} is controlled by the stochastic error bound from Proposition~\ref{prop:variance} and the property \(\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\mathcal V(\hat{\mathbf{h}})] \lesssim \mathcal R_n\) established in Theorem~\ref{thm:oracle_ineq}, which yield
\[\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\check{F}_n - \mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\check{F}_n]\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}] \lesssim \mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\mathcal V(\hat{\mathbf{h}})] \lesssim \mathcal R_n.\]
For the bias term, the comparability assumption \(\|B_{\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)} \le C_1\|B_{\mathbf{h}}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}\) gives
$$
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|B_{\hat{\mathbf{h}}}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}]
\le
C_1 \mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|B_{\hat{\mathbf{h}}}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}].$$
To bound the latter, we apply the triangle inequality to obtain
\[
\|B_{\hat{\mathbf{h}}}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}
\le
\|\check{F}_n - F_X\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}
+
\|\check{F}_n - \mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\check{F}_n]\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}.
\]
Taking expectations and applying \eqref{eq:L2oracle} together with the \(L^2\)-bound from Proposition~\ref{prop:variance} gives 
$$\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|B_{\hat{\mathbf{h}}}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}] \lesssim \mathcal R_n.$$
Hence,
\(\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|B_{\hat{\mathbf{h}}}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}] \lesssim \mathcal R_n\).
Combining \eqref{eq:L1decomp} with the preceding estimates yields the \(L^1\)-oracle inequality for the unprojected estimator,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {

        {

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[\|\check{F}_n - F_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}]
\lesssim
\mathcal R_n.
\label{eq:L1oracle_unproj}

        {

\item \emph{Transfer to approximate projection estimators.}\label{st:transfer}\\[2pt]
By definition, \(\widehat F_{n,\hat{\mathbf{h}}}^{\ast,1}=\widehat F_{n,\hat{\mathbf{h}}}^{\ast}\) and satisfies
$
\|\widehat F_{n,\hat{\mathbf{h}}}^{\ast,1} - F_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}\leq 2\|\check F_n - F_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}+\varrho_{n,1}$.
Taking expectations and applying \eqref{eq:L1oracle_unproj} gives
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}\Bigl[\|\widehat F_{n,\hat{\mathbf{h}}}^{\ast,1} - F_X\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}\Bigr]
\lesssim
\mathcal R_n.
\label{eq:L1oracle_proj}
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Analogously, for the estimator $\widehat F_{n,\hat{\mathbf{h}}}^{\ast,2}$, 
Theorem~\ref{thm:oracle_ineq} gives exactly
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation}

\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}\Bigl[\|\widehat F_{n,\hat{\mathbf{h}}}^{\ast,2} - F_X\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}\Bigr]
\lesssim
\mathcal R_n.
\label{eq:L2oracle_proj}

Inequalities \eqref{eq:L2oracle}, \eqref{eq:L1oracle_unproj}, \eqref{eq:L1oracle_proj}, and \eqref{eq:L2oracle_proj} are the desired oracle inequalities and the proof is complete.

<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:finiteMomentKernel}
Let 
\[
\hat{f}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}(x) \coloneqq \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n \frac{1}{h_j} \tilde{K}_{j,h_j}\!\left(\frac{x - Y_{i,j}}{h_j}\right),\quad x\in\mathbb R,
\]
where \(\tilde{K}_{j,h_j}\) is a kernel function satisfying
$\|\tilde{K}_{j,h_j}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} < \infty$ and $\int_{\mathbb R} |u| \, |\tilde{K}_{j,h_j}(u)| \, \mathrm{d}u < \infty$. 
Then, there exists a constant \(C_{j,h_j} > 0\) such that, almost surely,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:ineqg}
\int_{\mathbb R} |x| |\hat{f}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}(x)|\,\mathrm{d}x \le C_{j,h_j}\biggl(h_j + \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n |Y_{i,j}|\biggr) < \infty.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax}
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Using \(|x| \le |x - Y_{i,j}| + |Y_{i,j}|\) and the change of variable \(u = (x - Y_{i,j})/h_j\), we have
\[
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\begin{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\int_{\mathbb R} |x| \, |\hat{f}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}(x)| \,\mathrm{d}x
&= \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n \frac{1}{h_j} \int_{\mathbb R} |x| \, \left|\tilde{K}_{j,h_j}\!\left(\frac{x - Y_{i,j}}{h_j}\right)\right|\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\le h_j \int_{\mathbb R} |u| \, |\tilde{K}_{j,h_j}(u)| \, \mathrm{d}u
+ \biggl(\frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n |Y_{i,j}|\biggr) \int_{\mathbb R} |\tilde{K}_{j,h_j}(u)| \, \mathrm{d}u\\
&= h_j \int_{\mathbb R} |u| \, |\tilde{K}_{j,h_j}(u)| \, \mathrm{d}u
+ \biggl(\frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n |Y_{i,j}|\biggr) \|\tilde{K}_{j,h_j}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\end{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
Let
\[
C_{j,h_j}\coloneqq \max\left\{\int_{\mathbb R} |u| \, |\tilde{K}_{j,h_j}(u)| \, \mathrm{d}u,\; \|\tilde{K}_{j,h_j}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\right\}.
\]
Since the kernel integrals are finite by assumption, \(C_{j,h_j} < \infty\). Then, \eqref{eq:ineqg} follows.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:finiteMomentRestricted}
Let 
\(g(x) = \widehat{F}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}(x)=\int_{-\infty}^x\hat{f}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}(u) \,\mathrm{d}u\), $x\in\mathbb R$, be the univariate integrated estimator.
Assume that the minimizer 
$$F^\ast \coloneqq \arg\min_{F \in \mathfrak F_1^{\textnormal{fin}}} \|F - g\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}$$
exists, where \(\mathfrak F_1^{\textnormal{fin}} := \{ F \in \mathfrak F_1 :\, F \text{ has finite first moment} \}\). Here \(\mathfrak F_1\) denotes the class of univariate distribution functions on \(\mathbb R\).
For \((M_n)_{n\in\mathbb N}\), let $\mathfrak F_{M_n} := \{ F \in \mathfrak F_1 :\, F(x) = 0 \text{ for } x < -M_n, \;\, F(x) = 1 \text{ for } x > M_n \}$. Let \(\widehat{F}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}^{\ast}\) be a \(\varrho_n\)-approximate \(L^1\)-minimizer on \(\mathfrak F_{M_n}\) defined as
\[
\|\widehat{F}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}^{\ast} - g\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}
\le \inf_{F \in \mathfrak F_{M_n}} \|F - g\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} + \varrho_n,
\]
where \(\varrho_n = O(\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}})\) and \(\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}\) denotes the convergence rate of the unrestricted estimator defined in \eqref{eq:rate} \hyperref[eq:rate]{of the Main Article}, with \(\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}} \to 0\).
Then, \(\widehat{F}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}^{\ast}\) has finite first moment and, provided \(M_n\) is chosen such that
$
\inf_{F \in \mathfrak F_{M_n}} \|F - g\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} = \|F^\ast - g\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} + o(\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}})$,
it achieves the same convergence rate as the unrestricted estimator $\|\widehat{F}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}^{\ast} - g\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}
= \|F^\ast - g\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} + O(\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}})$.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
By Lemma~\ref{lem:finiteMomentKernel}, \(\hat{f}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}\) has finite first moment. 
Since \(F^\ast \in \mathfrak F_1^{\text{fin}}\) by assumption, it has finite first moment.
By construction, \(\widehat{F}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}^{\ast} \in \mathfrak F_{M_n}\), so its associated measure has support contained in \([-M_n,\, M_n]\) and therefore has finite first moment
\[
\int_{\mathbb R} |x| \, \mathrm{d}\widehat{F}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}^{\ast}(x) \le M_n < \infty.
\]
It remains to show that the convergence rate is not compromised. Since \(F^\ast\) has finite first moment, for each \(M > 0\) define the truncated cumulative distribution function
\[
F^\ast_M(x) \coloneqq
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\begin{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
0, & x < -M, \\
F^\ast(x), & |x| \le M, \\
1, & x > M.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\end{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
Then, \(F^\ast_M \in \mathfrak F_M\) and, by the dominated convergence theorem, $\|F^\ast_M - F^\ast\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} \to 0$ as $M \to \infty$.
Hence, for the given rate \(\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}\), we can choose \(M_n\) such that $
\|F^\ast_{M_n} - F^\ast\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} = o(\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}})$.
Therefore, the required condition on \(M_n\) can be satisfied. By the choice of \(M_n\) and since \(\varrho_n = O(\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}})\),
\[ 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        {\begin{split}} 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\|\widehat{F}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}^{\ast} - g\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}
&\le \inf_{F \in \mathfrak F_{M_n}} \|F - g\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} + \varrho_n \\
&= \|F^\ast - g\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} + o(\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}) + O(\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}) \\
&= \|F^\ast - g\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} + O(\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}).
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        {\end{split}} 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
Therefore, the restricted estimator achieves the same rate as the unrestricted estimator.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
\mbox{}\\
The proof adapts the construction from the \(L^1\)-cumulative distribution function lower bound to the product setting.
Let \(j_0 \in \{1,\,\dots,\,d\}\) be a fixed coordinate index (the construction below works for any choice of \(j_0\) and the resulting rate does not depend on this choice). The role of \(j_0\) is that of a localization device: the two test densities are built to differ only on the \(j_0\)-th coordinate, while on all other coordinates they coincide. This has two key consequences. First,
the separation in \(W_1\) between the two hypotheses is proportional to \(\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}\). Second, the \(\chi^2\)-divergence between the convolved densities remains sufficiently small, specifically \(o(n^{-1})\), so that Le Cam's two-point method applies.
The resulting lower bound rate \(\psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}} = n^{-E/2}\), with $E$ as defined  in~\eqref{eq:rate} \hyperref[eq:rate]{of the Main Article}, is governed by the aggregate anisotropic smoothness and noise exponents across all coordinates, not by a single worst-behaving coordinate. The positive-part censoring \((\beta_j-1/2)_+\) means that coordinates with \(\beta_j \le 1/2\) do not contribute to the exponent, effectively reducing the dimension of the problem. This is an anisotropic aggregation over the coordinates that survive the censoring, rather than a worst-coordinate maximum.


\smallskip

We first prove the lower bound. From the \(L^1\)-lower bound proof, there exist functions \(\omega,\, \xi \in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R)\) with properties \ref{it:W1}--\ref{it:W4} and \ref{it:U1}--\ref{it:U5}. These will be used to construct the test densities below. In particular,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        {\begin{itemize}} 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
    \item $\omega \ge 0$, $\operatorname{supp}(\omega) \subset [-\gamma_\omega,\,\gamma_\omega]$, $\int_{\mathbb R} \omega(x)\,\,\mathrm{d}x = 1$ and $\omega \ge c_\omega > 0$ on $[-\gamma_\omega,\, \gamma_\omega]$;
    \item $\operatorname{supp}(\xi) \subset [-\gamma_\xi,\, \gamma_\xi] \subset (-\gamma_\omega,\, \gamma_\omega)$, $\int_{\mathbb R} \xi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x = 0$ and $\Xi(x) \coloneqq \int_{-\infty}^x \xi(s)\,\mathrm{d}s \ge c_\Xi > 0$ for $x \in [1/2,\, 1/2+\eta]$.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        {\end{itemize}} 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Set $R_n \coloneqq n^{1/E}$ and
\(
\lambda_{j_0,n} := n^{-E/2} R_n^{-(\alpha_{j_0}+1)}
\).
Define for each $j$,
\[
\omega_{j,n}(x) \coloneqq 
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {

        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
R_n^{-(\alpha_{j_0}+1)} \omega(R_n^{-(\alpha_{j_0}+1)} x), & j = j_0, \\
\omega(x), & j \neq j_0,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\end{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
and
\[
\xi_{j,n}(x) \coloneqq 
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\begin{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
R_n^{-(\alpha_{j_0}+1)} \xi(R_n^{-(\alpha_{j_0}+1)} x), & j = j_0, \\
0, & j \neq j_0.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\end{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
The test densities are
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:testdensitiesW1}
f_0(\mathbf x) \coloneqq \prod_{j=1}^d \omega_{j,n}(x_j), \qquad
f_1(\mathbf x) \coloneqq  [\omega_{j_0,n}(x_{j_0}) + \lambda_{j_0,n} \xi_{j_0,n}(x_{j_0})]
\prod_{\substack{j=1 \\ j \neq j_0}}^d \omega_{j,n}(x_j),\quad\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb R^d.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
The verification that $f_0,\, f_1 \in \mathcal P_{\text{prod}}$ follows the same arguments as in the $L^1$-lower bound proof. The key points are the following:
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        {\begin{itemize}} 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
    \item since $\omega,\, \xi \in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R)$, we have $\omega_{j,n},\, \xi_{j,n} \in \mathcal N_1^1(\alpha_j, L_j)$ with constants independent of $n$;
    \item for sufficiently large $n$, $\lambda_{j_0,n} \|\xi\|_\infty \le c_\omega/2$, so $\omega_{j_0,n} + \lambda_{j_0,n} \xi_{j_0,n} \ge 0$;
    \item the product structure is preserved by construction;
    \item the finite first moment condition holds because $\omega$ and $\xi$ have compact support.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        {\end{itemize}} 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Then, the product densities $f_0$ and $f_1$ belong to $\mathcal N_1^d(\boldsymbol\alpha,\, L)$ for some $L > 0$ independent of $n$. In fact, if $f_j \in \mathcal N_1^1(\alpha_j,\, L_j)$ for $j = 1,\,\dots,\,d$, then the product density
\[
f(\mathbf x) = \prod_{j=1}^d f_j(x_j), \quad \mathbf x \in \mathbb R^d,
\]
belongs to $\mathcal N_1^d(\boldsymbol\alpha,\, L)$, with $\boldsymbol\alpha = (\alpha_1,\,\dots,\,\alpha_d)^\top$ and
$
L \coloneqq \max_{1 \le \ell \le d} L'_\ell
$,
where $L_\ell' \coloneqq \max\{L_\ell, \,1\}$.
For each $j = 1,\,\dots,\,d$, let $m_j \coloneqq \lceil \alpha_j \rceil - 1$. The partial derivative of order $m_j$ with respect to the $j$-th coordinate is
\[
\partial_j^{m_j} f(\mathbf x) = f_j^{(m_j)}(x_j) \prod_{\substack{\ell=1 \\ \ell \neq j}}^d f_\ell(x_\ell).
\]
For the $L^1$-integrability condition, since $\|f_\ell\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} = 1$ for each $\ell$,
\[
\|\partial_j^{m_j} f\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}
= \|f_j^{(m_j)}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)} \prod_{\substack{\ell=1 \\ \ell \neq j}}^d \|f_\ell\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}
\le L_j < \infty.
\]
For the Hölder condition, for any $\delta \in \mathbb R$,
\[
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\begin{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\|\Delta_{\delta \mathbf e_j} \partial_j^{m_j} f\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}= \|f_j^{(m_j)}(\cdot + \delta) - f_j^{(m_j)}(\cdot)\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}
\prod_{\substack{\ell=1 \\ \ell \neq j}}^d \|f_\ell\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\le L_j |\delta|^{\alpha_j - m_j}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\end{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
This proves that $f \in \mathcal N_1^d(\boldsymbol\alpha,\, L)$.
Thus, $f_0,\, f_1 \in \mathcal P_{\text{prod}}$ for all sufficiently large $n$.


\smallskip

We now compute the separation rate in $W_1$. 
Let $\mu_0,\,\mu_1$ be the probability measures corresponding to $f_0,\, f_1$. Since both are product measures and $W_1$ factorizes over independent coordinates, we have
$
W_1(\mu_0, \,\mu_1) = W_1(\mu_{j_0}^0,\, \mu_{j_0}^1)$,
where $\mu_{j_0}^0 \sim \omega_{j_0,n}$ and $\mu_{j_0}^1 \sim \omega_{j_0,n} + \lambda_{j_0,n} \xi_{j_0,n}$.
In one dimension, $W_1$ equals the $L^1$-distance between the cumulative distribution functions so
$W_1(\mu_{j_0}^0,\, \mu_{j_0}^1) = \lambda_{j_0,n} \int_{\mathbb R} |\Xi_{j_0,n}(x)| \, \mathrm{d}x$,
where $\Xi_{j_0,n}(x) \coloneqq \Xi(R_n^{-(\alpha_{j_0}+1)}x)$ and $\Xi$ is the primitive of $\xi$.
Define $
\kappa_{j_0} \coloneqq \int_{\mathbb R} |\Xi(u)| \, \mathrm{d}u > 0$. By the change of variable $u = R_n^{-(\alpha_{j_0}+1)}x$,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:W1separation}
W_1(\mu_0, \,\mu_1) = \lambda_{j_0,n} \int_{\mathbb R} |\Xi_{j_0,n}(x)| \, \mathrm{d}x
= \lambda_{j_0,n} R_n^{\alpha_{j_0}+1} \int_{\mathbb R} |\Xi(u)| \, \mathrm{d}u
= \lambda_{j_0,n} R_n^{\alpha_{j_0}+1} \kappa_{j_0}
= \kappa_{j_0} n^{-E/2}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    

We now bound the $\chi^2$-divergence. 
Set \(\epsilon \coloneqq (\gamma_\omega-\gamma_\xi)/2 > 0\). Since \(\gamma_\xi < \gamma_\omega\), we have \(\gamma_\xi + \epsilon < \gamma_\omega\). The support of \(f_1 - f_0\) is contained in
\[
\mathcal K_n\coloneqq \Big\{ \mathbf x \in \mathbb R^d :\, |x_{j_0}| \le \gamma_\xi R_n^{\alpha_{j_0}+1} + \epsilon, \; |x_j| \le \gamma_\xi + \epsilon \text{ for } j \neq j_0 \Big\}.
\]
We derive a lower bound on $f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon$ on $\mathcal K_n$.
We show that there exists a constant $c_0 > 0$ such that
\[
(f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)(\mathbf x) \ge c_0 R_n^{-(\alpha_{j_0}+1)}, \quad \forall\, \mathbf x \in \mathcal K_n.
\]
Indeed, on the support of $f_1 - f_0$,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        {\begin{itemize}} 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
    \item for $j = j_0$, since $\gamma_\xi + \epsilon < \gamma_\omega$ and $\omega(x) \ge c_\omega > 0$ for $|x| \le \gamma_\omega$, for sufficiently large $n$ we have $\gamma_\xi R_n^{\alpha_{j_0}+1} + \epsilon \le \gamma_\omega R_n^{\alpha_{j_0}+1}$, and hence
    \[
    \omega_{j_0,n}(x_{j_0}) \ge c_\omega R_n^{-(\alpha_{j_0}+1)}, \quad |x_{j_0}| \le \gamma_\xi R_n^{\alpha_{j_0}+1} + \epsilon;
    \]
   \item for $j \neq j_0$, since $\gamma_\xi + \epsilon < \gamma_\omega$, we have $\omega_{j,n}(x_j) = \omega(x_j) \ge c_\omega > 0$ for $|x_j| \le \gamma_\xi + \epsilon$.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        {\end{itemize}} 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Thus, on $\mathcal K_n$,
\[
f_0(\mathbf x) = \prod_{j=1}^d \omega_{j,n}(x_j) \ge c_* R_n^{-(\alpha_{j_0}+1)},
\]
where $c_* \coloneqq c_\omega^d > 0$.
Now, for any $0 < \varpi < 1$, there exists a compact set $K_\varpi \subset \mathbb R^d$ such that
$
\int_{K_\varpi} f_\varepsilon(\mathbf z) \, \mathrm{d}\mathbf z \ge 1 - \varpi$. 
Choose $K_\varpi$ sufficiently small so that $\mathbf x - K_\varpi \subset \mathcal K_n^*$ for all $\mathbf x \in \mathcal K_n$, where $\mathcal K_n^*$ is a slightly enlarged region where the same lower bound on $f_0$ holds.
Then, for $\mathbf x \in \mathcal K_n$,
\[
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\begin{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
(f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)(\mathbf x)
\ge \int_{\mathbf x - K_\varpi} f_0(\mathbf u) f_\varepsilon(\mathbf x - \mathbf u) \, \mathrm{d}\mathbf u 
\ge c_* R_n^{-(\alpha_{j_0}+1)} \int_{K_\varpi} f_\varepsilon(\mathbf z) \, \mathrm{d}\mathbf z 
\ge c_* (1 - \varpi) R_n^{-(\alpha_{j_0}+1)}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\end{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
Therefore, for $c_0\coloneqq c_* (1 - \varpi) > 0$, we have $
(f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)(\mathbf x) \ge c_0 R_n^{-(\alpha_{j_0}+1)}$ for all $\mathbf x \in \mathcal K_n$.
By possibly reducing the constant, the same lower bound holds on the slightly enlarged region $\mathcal K_n^*$: there exists $c_0' \in (0,\,c_0]$ such that
$(f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)(\mathbf x) \ge c_0' R_n^{-(\alpha_{j_0}+1)}$ for all $\mathbf x \in \mathcal K_n^*$.
Since, for \(n\) sufficiently large, $\operatorname{supp}\big((f_1 - f_0) \ast f_\varepsilon\big) \subset \mathcal K_n^*$,
\[
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\begin{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\chi^2(f_1 \ast f_\varepsilon \parallel f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)
&= \int_{\mathbb R^d} \frac{[(f_1 - f_0) \ast f_\varepsilon(\mathbf x)]^2}{(f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)(\mathbf x)} \, \mathrm{d}\mathbf x\\
&\leq \int_{\mathcal K_n^*} \frac{[(f_1 - f_0) \ast f_\varepsilon(\mathbf x)]^2}{(f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)(\mathbf x)} \, \mathrm{d}\mathbf x \\
&\le \frac{1}{c_0'} R_n^{\alpha_{j_0}+1} \int_{\mathcal K_n^*} [(f_1 - f_0) \ast f_\varepsilon(\mathbf x)]^2 \, \mathrm{d}\mathbf x\\
&\le \frac{1}{c_0'} R_n^{\alpha_{j_0}+1}\|(f_1 - f_0) \ast f_\varepsilon\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}^2 \\
&\le \frac{1}{c_0'} R_n^{\alpha_{j_0}+1}\|f_1 - f_0\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}^2 \|f_\varepsilon\|_{L^1(\mathbb R^d)}^2 \\
&= \frac{1}{c_0'} R_n^{\alpha_{j_0}+1}\|f_1 - f_0\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}^2.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\end{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
We compute $\|f_1 - f_0\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}^2$. Since $f_1 - f_0$ factorizes, we have
\[
\|f_1 - f_0\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^d)}^2
= \lambda_{j_0,n}^2 \|\xi_{j_0,n}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R)}^2 \prod_{\substack{j=1 \\ j \neq j_0}}^d \|\omega_{j,n}\|_{L^2(\mathbb R)}^2
= \lambda_{j_0,n}^2 R_n^{-(\alpha_{j_0}+1)} \|\xi\|_{L^2(\mathbb R)}^2 \prod_{\substack{j=1 \\ j \neq j_0}}^d \|\omega\|_{L^2(\mathbb R)}^2.
\]
Thus,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:chi2boundW1}
\chi^2(f_1 \ast f_\varepsilon \parallel f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon)
\le \frac{C}{c_0'} \lambda_{j_0,n}^2 R_n^{\alpha_{j_0}+1}
= \frac{C}{c_0'} n^{-E} R_n^{-(\alpha_{j_0}+1)}
\lesssim n^{-E - (\alpha_{j_0}+1)/E}
\lesssim n^{-1}
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
because $\alpha_{j_0} > 0$ implies $E + (\alpha_{j_0}+1)/E \ge 2\sqrt{\alpha_{j_0}+1} > 1$ for every $E > 0$. Therefore,
$\chi^2(f_1 \ast f_\varepsilon \parallel f_0 \ast f_\varepsilon) = o(n^{-1})$.
By Le Cam's two-point method,
\[
\inf_{\hat{\mu}_n} \sup_{\mu_X \in \mathcal P_{\text{prod}}} \mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[W_1(\hat{\mu}_n,\, \mu_X)]
\ge \frac{1}{2} W_1(\mu_0,\, \mu_1)
\left[ 1 - \| (\mu_1 \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n} - (\mu_0 \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n} \|_{\mathrm{TV}} \right].
\]
From \eqref{eq:chi2boundW1}, Pinsker's inequality and tensorization of the $\chi^2$-divergence yield
$
\| (\mu_1 \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n} - (\mu_0 \ast \mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n} \|_{\mathrm{TV}} \to 0$.
Thus, for $n$ sufficiently large, using \eqref{eq:W1separation}:
\[
\inf_{\hat{\mu}_n} \sup_{\mu_X \in \mathcal P_{\text{prod}}} \mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[W_1(\hat{\mu}_n,\, \mu_X)]
\ge \frac{1}{4} W_1(\mu_0,\, \mu_1)\ge \frac{\kappa_{j_0}}{4} \psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}.
\]
Therefore,
\[
\inf_{\hat{\mu}_n} \sup_{\mu_X \in \mathcal P_{\text{prod}}} \mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}[W_1(\hat{\mu}_n,\, \mu_X)] \gtrsim \psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}},
\]
which completes the proof of the lower bound.

                                                      
                                                      
\smallskip



We now prove the upper bound. For each \(j = 1,\,\dots,\,d\), let \(\widehat{F}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}\) be the univariate integrated estimator, and let \(\widehat{F}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}^{\ast}\) be the restricted \(L^1\)-projection estimator defined in Corollary \ref{cor:finiteMomentRestricted}, with \(\varrho_{j,n} = O({n}^{-1/2})\) and \(M_n\) chosen as in Corollary~\ref{cor:finiteMomentRestricted}. Define the product estimator
\[
\widehat{F}_{n,\mathbf{h}}^*(\mathbf x) \coloneqq \prod_{j=1}^d \widehat{F}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}^\ast(x_j), \quad \mathbf x = (x_1,\,\dots,\,x_d)^\top \in \mathbb R^d,
\]
and let \(\widehat{\mu}_{n,\mathbf{h}}^\ast \coloneqq \bigotimes_{j=1}^d \widehat{\mu}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}^\ast\) be the associated probability measure. By Corollary~\ref{cor:finiteMomentRestricted}, each \(\widehat{\mu}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}^\ast\) has finite first moment. Hence, by the factorization property of \(W_1\) for product measures with \(\ell^1\)-cost and using identity \eqref{eq:identity} \hyperref[eq:identity]{of the Main Article},
\[ 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        {\begin{split}} 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
W_1^{(\ell^1)}(\widehat{\mu}_{n,\mathbf{h}}^\ast,\, \mu_X) 
&= W_1^{(\ell^1)}\!\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^d \widehat{\mu}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}^\ast,\, \bigotimes_{j=1}^d \mu_{X,j}\right)\\
&= \sum_{j=1}^d W_1^{(\ell^1)}(\widehat{\mu}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}^\ast,\, \mu_{X,j})\\
&= \sum_{j=1}^d\| \widehat{F}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}^* - F_{X,j}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@split} 
        {\end{split}} 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@split\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
\]
Taking expectations, 
\[
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}\big[ W_1^{(\ell^1)}(\widehat{\mu}_{n,\mathbf{h}}^\ast,\, \mu_X)  \big]
=\sum_{j=1}^d \mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}}\big [\| \widehat{F}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}^* - F_{X,j}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\big].
\]
From Theorem~\ref{thm:ubrate}, applied in dimension $d=1$ with the restricted projection space $\mathfrak F_{M_n}$, for each $j$ there exists a constant $C_j > 0$ such that
\[
\sup_{f_j \in \mathcal N_1^1(\alpha_j, L_j)} 
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}} \big[\|\widehat{F}_{j,n,\mathbf{h}}^* - F_{X,j}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\big]
\le C_j \, n^{-E_j/2},
\]
where we define
\[
E_j \coloneqq \bigg[1 + \frac{(\beta_j - \frac12)_+}{\alpha_j + 1}\bigg]^{-1}.
\]
The choice $\varrho_{j,n} = O({n}^{-1/2})$ and $M_n$ as in Corollary~\ref{cor:finiteMomentRestricted} ensures that the projection and truncation errors do not affect the rate. Therefore,
\[
\sup_{\mu_X \in \mathcal P_{\text{prod}}} 
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}} \big[ W_1^{(\ell^1)}(\widehat{\mu}_{n,\mathbf{h}}^\ast,\, \mu_{X}) \big]
\le \sum_{j=1}^d C_j \, n^{-E_j/2}.
\]
From the definition of $E$,
\[
\frac{1}{E} = 1 + \sum_{\ell=1}^d \frac{(\beta_\ell-\frac12)_+}{\alpha_\ell+1}
\ge 1 + \frac{(\beta_j-\frac12)_+}{\alpha_j+1} = \frac{1}{E_j}>0.
\]
Therefore, for $n \ge 1$, $n^{-E_j/2} \le n^{-E/2}$. Consequently,
$\sum_{j=1}^d C_j n^{-E_j/2}
\le n^{-E/2}\sum_{j=1}^d C_j
\lesssim n^{-E/2}$. Hence,
\[
\sup_{\mu_X \in \mathcal P_{\text{prod}}} 
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}} \big[ W_1^{(\ell^1)}(\widehat{\mu}_{n,\mathbf{h}}^\ast,\, \mu_{X}) \big]
\lesssim \psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}.
\]
This completes the proof of the upper bound.

\smallskip

Combining the lower and upper bounds, we obtain the minimax rate 
\[
\inf_{\hat{\mu}_n} \sup_{\mu_X \in \mathcal P_{\text{prod}}} 
\mathsf{E}_{(\mu_X\ast\mu_\varepsilon)^{\otimes n}} [W_1(\hat{\mu}_n,\, \mu_X)] \asymp \psi_n^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}}.
\]
This completes the proof.
